
FILE: 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Presentation Deck.pptx
This May 2019 presentation reviews the progress of the Washington Charter School Initiative (WACSI) for the US Program Pacific Northwest team, including senior leadership from USP President Allan Golston and PNW Director David Bley. The document covers the initiative's history since 2012, including key legal milestones: WA voters approving charter schools, the WA Supreme Court ruling them unconstitutional in 2015 (the same month first schools opened), subsequent legislative action to preserve them, and a final ruling restoring their constitutionality. Average annual spend from 2013–2018 was $23M, with a new three-year request of $54.3M proposed to continue funding through 2022.

A scorecard evaluating quality, access, and equity outcomes shows mixed results: some indicators are in acceptable or excellent ranges (e.g., 72-day and 60-day metrics, 70% and 60% on certain access measures), while financial sustainability scores are critically low. CREDO report findings provide early evidence of mixed but promising academic outcomes, ranging from 1 month to 1–1.5 years of additional learning depending on school and subject.

A central finding is that only 1 of 12 charter schools is projected to achieve financial sustainability, with an estimated sector-wide operating gap of $6.9M–$12.3M. The root cause analysis points to insufficient state per-pupil revenue—approximately $1,500 per pupil below the level needed for sustainability. Increasing enrollment alone cannot close the gap once schools reach full authorized capacity by SY22/23.

The recommended strategy maintains current funding levels through 2022 with several key shifts. Primary levers include advocacy to increase per-pupil state funding (e.g., levy equalization, special education, and facilities funding), extend authorization windows, and strengthen legislative support. Secondary levers focus on CMO path-forward planning. Advocacy investments of $4.5M are directed through partners including WA Charters, Stand for Children, League of Education Voters, Washington Business Roundtable, Ed Reform Now, and the Black Education Strategy Roundtable. Tactics include engaging rural alliances, building relationships with key legislators, monitoring the Washington Education Association, and activating parent advocacy networks.

This document connects to USP strategy by illustrating the foundation's sustained investment in charter sector development in Washington State as a model for equitable school access and high-quality outcomes, while surfacing the critical policy and financial sustainability challenges that threaten the sector's long-term viability.

FILE: 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Team Org Chart.pptx
This document is an organizational chart for the Pacific Northwest (PNW) Program Strategy Team (PST) at the Gates Foundation, specifically in the context of the Washington State Charter School Initiative Progress Review, dated May 2019. The chart maps reporting relationships and role titles across the team, centered on the WA Charter School Initiative and the broader Washington State Strategy.

At the top of the structure is David Bley as Director, with Sarah Buhayar serving as Deputy Director for Strategy, Planning and Management. Jean-Claude Brizard is identified as Senior Advisor and Deputy Director for the P-16 Community Investment Team and Washington State Strategy, signaling the integration of charter school work within a broader P-16 educational continuum approach.

Key programmatic staff include Senior Program Officers covering areas such as Measurement, Learning and Evaluation (MLE) — represented by Amber Banks and Juan Sanchez — as well as Policy and Advocacy (Lindsay Hunsicker), and a range of content-focused officers including John Pruette, Phoebe Lipkis, Marie Sauter, Isabel Muñoz-Colon, Telca Porras, LiLi Liu, and Buck Child as Senior Portfolio Officer. Nelson Khov serves as Associate Program Officer and Anne Martens as Program Officer for Communications.

Support and coordination roles include program assistants (Julie Butters, De'Neka Gary), program coordinators (Sydney Holman at .50 FTE, Sandra Hearn at .50 FTE), and a Senior Program Coordinator (Tracy Sutton) and Senior Program Assistant (Ann Holstrom).

Kollin Min is listed as Senior Program Officer and Lead for Family Homelessness, reflecting the PNW team's cross-cutting work beyond K-12 education. Marquita Davis serves as Deputy Director for Early Learning, indicating the team's scope across the P-16 pipeline.

This org chart provides strategic context for understanding the team configuration supporting the WA Charter School Initiative at a progress review juncture in 2019. It highlights the intersection of charter school strategy with broader Washington State educational investments, MLE infrastructure, communications capacity, and community investment approaches. The structure reflects the Foundation's integrated approach to K-12 reform in Washington State, connecting charter school development to policy advocacy, early learning, and postsecondary pathways within a single regional team.

FILE: 121119 Q4 Family Planning Strategy Refresh Check-in Illustrative Pathway to Informed FP Choice.pptx
This presentation slide deck presents an illustrative pathway to informed choice for family planning (FP), developed as part of a Q4 2019 Family Planning Strategy Refresh Check-in at the Gates Foundation. The document maps the sequential conditions and barriers that must be addressed for women and girls to achieve informed reproductive choice, spanning individual awareness, community norms, and service delivery levels.

The pathway traces a woman's journey from basic awareness that she has reproductive choices, through knowledge of fertility and contraception, aspirations for her future, attitudes toward contraceptive use, partner and family support, religious and community leader influence, peer and satisfied-client exposure, self-efficacy, method availability, gender and social norm environments, affordability, integrated care access, geographic accessibility, provider quality and bias, method satisfaction, and ongoing continuation support.

Each step is grounded in specific country-level evidence from sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia, including data from DRC, Burkina Faso, Niger, Bangladesh, Angola, Ethiopia, Nigeria, Ghana, Tanzania, Uganda, India, and others. Key data points include: only 20% of young first-time mothers in DRC have correct fertility knowledge; nearly two-thirds of Burkinabe youth who view pregnancy as problematic are not using contraception; providers in five countries impose twice the eligibility restrictions required by national guidelines; and a 19-country study found nearly 40% of past contraceptive users discontinued due to method-related issues.

The framework emphasizes that no single pathway applies universally, but a common set of barriers exists across populations. The document positions the Foundation's FP strategy impact as contingent on understanding and systematically addressing these barriers across multiple levels. Outcomes targeted include increased modern contraceptive prevalence rate (mCPR), improved maternal and newborn health (MNCH), economic development, women's empowerment, and gender equality. While this document is oriented toward global health and family planning programming rather than U.S. domestic education or workforce strategy, it reflects cross-cutting themes of equity, gender, and data-driven decision-making relevant to Gates Foundation strategic priorities.

[Slide 1]
Illustrative Pathway to Informed Choice for FP
This is an illustrative map to show what changes or conditions support informed choice at the individual, community and service delivery levels. We know from the literature that there is not a single path for all women but there is a set of barriers common across various populations. Our impact is dependent on understanding and addressing key barriers.
She is aware that she has choices about reproduction and fertility  
Because of their social position and prevailing gender norms, some women/girls do not believe they can make a decision about whether, when and how many children to have.
She understands how pregnancy happens and the possibility of avoiding pregnancy 
Only 20% of young, first-time mothers in DRC age 15-24 have correct knowledge of their fertile period, and 26% are unaware a woman can become pregnant after she gives birth but before her menses returns.*
She has aspirations for her life
Girls often do not connect contraceptive use with their ability to achieve their aspirations  in life (education, wealth, etc.).*
She doesn’t want to get pregnant but hasn’t considered using contraceptives 
Almost half of sexually active married and unmarried Burkinabe youth say that being pregnant would be a problem. Of those who think it would be a big problem, however, almost two thirds (61%) are not using a method.*
She has full, accurate information about contraception 
Married adolescents in Niger and Bangladesh have misconceptions about FP: 43% and 43% respectively believe contraceptives are dangerous to women's health, 43% and 32% believe contraception can cause infertility if used before first birth, and 35% and 27% believe contraception causes birth defects.*
She believes the pros outweigh the cons of using contraceptives 
A meta-analysis of 46 studies across 13+ countries found that social behavior change programs had an effect on perceived benefits of FP across mass media (OR=1.1), interpersonal communication (OR=1.7), and combined packages (OR=1.6). Additionally, those perceptions were associated with higher mCPR (OR=1.5).*
Her partner and/or family influencers support her in her decision to use FP
In Angola, partner approval of FP was associated with triple the odds of women using modern contraception.* In Ethiopia, 65% of girls choose a modern FP method after Smart Start counseling, rising to 82% when counseled with their husbands.
Her decision to use FP is supported by religious and local leaders
In four Nigerian states, a multivariable analysis showed significantly higher contraceptive uptake among women who had exposure to positive FP messages from religious leaders relative to those with no exposure (OR=1.7).*
She has had exposure to satisfied clients

FILE: K-12 Education Co-Chair Presentation 03042019.pptx
This March 2019 co-chair presentation provides a comprehensive strategy review of the Gates Foundation's K-12 Education portfolio. The document outlines the overarching vision: significantly increasing the number of Black, Latinx, and low-income students who earn a high school diploma, enroll in postsecondary institutions, and are on track to obtain a credential with labor-market value.

The portfolio is organized into four major strategic pillars: (1) Advanced Education R&D, focused on step-change improvements through higher-risk, long-range bets; (2) Solutions, aimed at increasing availability and use of proven interventions; (3) High-Quality Charters, focused on sector health and scalable innovation; and (4) Networks for School Improvement (NSI), targeting improved student outcomes across school networks.

A significant financial proposal is presented: a $60M reallocation within the four-year outlook, drawn from the Solutions portfolio. Of this, $40M would remain within Solutions for priority work, while $20M would shift to Advancing Actionable Knowledge within Cross-Cutting K-12. The total four-year budget includes approximately $51M for evaluation (4% of total spend), $17M for knowledge dissemination, and a $40M reserve fund.

Teacher and principal preparation receive dedicated attention, highlighting investments in Transformation Centers, a new center for minority-serving institutions, alternative certification program capacity-building, and data infrastructure including open-access dashboards and interoperability tools with a focus on Texas and California district hiring demand.

The Advancing Actionable Knowledge section diagnoses a fragmented evidence ecosystem—overcrowded synthesis, lack of federal coordination, weak data infrastructure—and identifies principal decision-making behaviors as central to adoption: principals rely primarily on peer networks, values alignment, their own data, and trusted relationships rather than formal research. The strategy proposes leveraging digital principal networks, regional research partners, behavioral insights, and implementation case studies to improve evidence discoverability and usability.

The presentation also introduces cross-cutting work in curriculum, social-emotional learning, postsecondary pathways, education-to-employment pathways, data privacy and governance, and standards and accountability policy. This document is directly relevant to USP K-12 strategy planning and resource allocation decisions for 2019–2022.

FILE: K-12 Education Team Org Chart 03042019.pdf
This document is the organizational chart for the Gates Foundation K-12 Education team as of early 2019, presented as part of the K-12 Education 2019 Strategy Review. It provides a comprehensive view of the team's structure, reporting relationships, and role classifications across all major program areas.

At the top of the hierarchy is Bob Hughes, Director of K-12 Education. Reporting to him are several Deputy Directors leading distinct portfolios: Adam Tucker, Jamie McKee, and Josh Edelman (all overseeing Networks for School Improvement); Don Shalvey (High-Quality Charters and Solutions); Henry Hipps (Solutions); Sara Allan (Advanced Education R&D); Cecilia David (Sr. Program Assistant); and Mary Alice Heuschel (Knowledge, Evaluation, Research, and Data Strategy).

The Networks for School Improvement (NSI) portfolio is the largest by headcount, with three Deputy Directors each overseeing teams of Senior Program Officers, Program Officers, Associate Program Officers, and Program Assistants. Key staff include Vivian Mihalakis, Harvey Chism, Alan Coverstone, Teresa Rivero, Wendy Sauer, Eli Pristoop, Adam Goldfarb, Pras Ranaweera, Ky Vu, Jill Hawley, and Jennifer Husbands, among others.

The High-Quality Charters and Solutions team under Don Shalvey includes Michelle Rojas, Ryen Borden, Kai Kung, Vanessa Murrieta, Amy Slamp, and support staff. Henry Hipps's Solutions team includes Bill Tucker (Sr. Advisor), Rachel Leifer, Brad Bernatek, Taunya Nesin, Jaymes Hanna, and Akilah Allen.

The Advanced Education R&D team under Sara Allan includes Carina Wong (Sr. Advisor), Karen Johnson, Emily Lockwood, Isa Ellis, Lewis Leiboh, Kenie Richards, and Viraj Kamdar, with Cecilia David providing senior program assistant support.

The Knowledge, Evaluation, Research, and Data Strategy team under Mary Alice Heuschel is staffed with roles spanning portfolio management, evaluation, business analysis, and research, including Marie Gibson, Susan Fairchild, Bryan Richardson, and several analysts and associate program officers.

This org chart reflects the foundation's investment in a large, differentiated team structure to execute its K-12 strategy, with distinct workstreams for school improvement networks, charter quality, solutions development, advanced education R&D, and knowledge/data infrastructure. It is useful for understanding team capacity, leadership accountability, and how program areas were resourced during the 2019 strategy review period.

FILE: USPAC Optional Appendix B 03042019.pptx
This document is an optional appendix (Appendix B) prepared for a USPAC meeting in early 2019, providing contextual analysis of the political and public opinion landscape relevant to Gates Foundation advocacy priorities at both federal and state levels. The appendix contains two slides addressing distinct policy environments. The first slide examines federal policymaker prioritization of foreign aid and global development. It notes that foreign aid does not feature prominently in the agenda of the 116th Congress for either party. Democrats are focused on oversight of the Trump Administration, healthcare, infrastructure, and ethics/election reforms, while Republicans are focused on judicial nominations and supporting the Administration. Two structural factors depress development on the priority list: domestic issues dominate voter concerns, and members of both parties in competitive seats perceive support for global health and development as a political liability. Public opinion data reinforces this, showing that when voters think about foreign affairs, global development issues rank near the bottom. The second slide shifts to state-level policymaker prioritization of education. It identifies governors' top priorities as job creation and economic growth, stabilizing state budgets, healthcare, and infrastructure. Education, when it does appear on gubernatorial agendas, is typically framed around school funding, quality, and teacher pay. Notably, while voters consistently rank education as a top issue in statewide elections, their definitions of priority education issues diverge significantly from Gates Foundation priorities, with foundation priorities falling to the bottom of voter-defined education concerns. Gubernatorial campaign advertising data shows education as the second most mentioned issue in candidate platforms, yet deeper polling reveals a mismatch between voter and foundation framings. This appendix serves as a strategic context document for USPAC discussions, helping the foundation understand the external political environment and the challenges of advancing its K-12 and global health/development priorities within the current legislative and electoral landscape.

Republicans will likely focus on key judicial and political nominations and providing cover for the Administration.

FILE: USPAC Optional Appendix C 03042019.pptx
This document is an optional appendix (Appendix C) prepared for a USPAC meeting in 2019, presenting a snapshot of progress across North America-level PST (Program Strategy Team) priorities and U.S. Program (USP) state and local priorities. The first slide maps North America-level global health and development investments — including GPEI, GAVI, Global Fund, Polio, HIV, Malaria, TB, Nutrition, MNCH, Family Planning, and Agriculture — alongside U.S.-facing priorities such as Pathways, NSI (Network School Improvement), HEA (Higher Education Act), ESSA, and Postsecondary Data. This framing situates the U.S. Program within the broader Gates Foundation portfolio context. The second slide focuses specifically on state and local progress across USP priority areas. It identifies a range of initiatives being tracked across multiple geographies, including: P-20 longitudinal data systems, Pathways (both K-12 and postsecondary), High Quality Instructional Materials (HQIM/curricula), NSI improvement, Developmental Education reforms, State Education Finance, Teacher Preparation, Principal Leadership, Early Learning, Charter Seat Growth, Coalition Building (including rural coalitions), and Decision Maker Engagement. The geographic spread suggests multi-state engagement, with different states prioritizing different combinations of these focus areas. Key strategic themes include systems-level data infrastructure (P-20 data systems appearing repeatedly), workforce and economic mobility pathways, instructional quality, and network/school improvement. The document serves as a visual progress tracker or portfolio map for USPAC members, enabling a high-level assessment of where USP investments are concentrated and how they align with the foundation's broader North American strategy. It connects to USP's integrated theory of change linking K-12 improvement, postsecondary access and completion, and economic mobility outcomes for students of color and low-income students.

FILE: USPC Co-Chair Presentation 03042019.pptx
This March 2019 presentation was prepared for the US Policy, Advocacy, and Communications (USPAC) strategy review co-chair meeting, bringing together senior foundation leadership including directors of K-12, Postsecondary Success, Pacific Northwest, External Communications, Donor Government Relations, and US Mobility and Economic Opportunity. The document provides an overview of USPAC's 'Working in Place' strategy framework and portfolio performance heading into the 2019 strategy cycle.

The core strategic model is organized around three objectives: (1) Monitor Impact and Trends, (2) Understand and Shape the Environment, and (3) Drive Priorities in Place. Key bodies of work include political analysis, direct advocacy, policy development, coalition building, narrative shaping, and constituency cultivation. Budget allocations across three strategic initiatives total approximately $110.3M with 41 FTE.

The presentation identifies major macro political challenges confronting USPAC's work: increased polarization between progressive and right-wing movements, dwindling public trust in institutions, growing intra-party divisions, uneven economic growth, and the limitations of 501c3 advocacy activities. USPAC acknowledges being outresourced in most geographies by entrenched special interest actors and that foundation priorities are falling down the political agenda.

In response, the strategy outlines a series of adaptations: increasing staff time on direct engagement with decisionmakers, reframing priorities for greater political salience, targeting new demographics of decisionmakers, investing in research that connects foundation priorities to top political priorities, building incentive structures for policymaker engagement, and increasing budget flexibility across envelopes for nimble cross-cutting investments.

Geographic focus areas include specific US states such as California, North Carolina, Tennessee, and Texas, as well as Canada and global health and development work with the US federal government and the UN. State-level priorities highlighted include longitudinal data systems in California, education equity coalitions in North Carolina, pre-K access in Tennessee, and school improvement in Texas. Canada work focuses on advancing global health priorities with key decisionmakers. The presentation reflects a critical self-assessment moment for USPAC as it seeks to evolve its advocacy model in response to a more challenging and polarized political environment.

FILE: 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 3.pptx
This document is a 2020 Federal Postsecondary Progress Snapshot slide, functioning as Appendix 3 to a broader USP Strategy Update. It provides a status assessment of key federal postsecondary policy priorities relevant to the Gates Foundation's U.S. Program, assessed against a backdrop of Congressional activity, administration actions, and the emerging disruptions from COVID-19.

Key findings include: (1) Pell Grant funding has grown incrementally by 30% over the prior decade through consistent Congressional action, but the economic crisis demands a more dramatic and targeted federal response; (2) Postsecondary data transparency policy has maintained bipartisan Congressional support despite significant headwinds over the prior five years, and the administration advanced program-level data publication on the College Scorecard; (3) FAFSA simplification via the FUTURE Act progressed even when broader Higher Education Act (HEA) reauthorization stalled, going further in scope than originally anticipated; and (4) bipartisan support is growing among both agenda-setters and rank-and-file policymakers for the Foundation's priority issues, though accountability policy still requires additional targeted solutions and coalition-building.

The document notes that the pipeline of Congressional champions for Foundation priorities is strong, but flags that post-election recalibration and new member engagement will be necessary. It also signals a strategic shift, noting that Instructional Supports and Pathways pillars are likely to become more active federal focus areas as COVID-19 amplifies demand for stronger federal and national solutions around distance learning and pathways quality.

Strategically, this snapshot connects to the Foundation's postsecondary agenda by tracking federal enabling conditions—financial aid adequacy, data infrastructure, and accountability frameworks—that underpin student success outcomes. It reflects an inflection point where the COVID-19 crisis is reshaping federal priorities and creating new opportunities or urgency around systemic solutions such as free college, federal-state partnerships, and debt forgiveness. This document would be relevant to USP staff tracking federal advocacy, postsecondary policy windows, and cross-cutting strategy recalibration in 2020.

FILE: 041520 USP Strategy Update Presentation Deck.pptx
This April 2020 presentation deck provides a U.S. Program strategy update focused on the impact of COVID-19 on postsecondary education institutions and students, as well as an analysis of federal policy responses. Originally planned as a deeper dive into federal postsecondary policy development, the session was updated to address the rapidly changing COVID context.

The document outlines pre-existing structural fragilities in the postsecondary sector — including demographic enrollment declines, state disinvestment in higher education, tuition increases, and unsustainable price discounting — and describes how COVID-19 has dramatically amplified these pressures. Key data points include: over 10% of institutions in significant financial distress (per BMGF-funded models), projected fall 2020 enrollment declines, 80% of colleges moved to remote instruction, and industry projections that up to 20% of institutions could fail if enrollment declines accelerate.

The presentation details COVID-19's multi-dimensional impacts on students across three domains: financial (job loss, food and housing insecurity, Pell coverage gaps), mobility (credit transfer barriers, risk from institutional closures, administration's limited closure protections), and learning (digital divide, low prior online teaching capacity, mental health gaps). These impacts are noted as disproportionately affecting low-income students and students of color.

From a strategy perspective, the document assesses implications for U.S. Program postsecondary outcomes, warning of a near-certain end to the decade-long increase in graduation rates, growing attainment gaps by race and income, and a projected 1–2 year delay in reaching the 2025 goal of supporting 300–400 institutions in transforming to increase graduation rates and close equity gaps.

The final section covers the federal government's COVID-19 supplemental funding response, particularly the Phase 3 CARES Act, which included $30.8 billion in education funding. Provisions analyzed include student loan payment pauses, satisfactory academic progress waivers, Federal Work-Study and SEOG flexibilities, TEACH Grant modifications, and HBCU capital financing deferrals. The document also discusses maintenance-of-effort requirements for states receiving stabilization funds and job retention provisions.

This presentation reflects the North America and US Program coordinated approach to federal postsecondary policy and serves as a strategic checkpoint for how the foundation should adapt its postsecondary strategy in response to the COVID-19 crisis.

FILE: 061020 USP Data Co-Chair Update Final.pptx
This June 2020 presentation was prepared for a USP Data Strategy co-chair update and discussion, summarizing progress in developing a comprehensive data strategy for the U.S. Program. The document outlines foundational co-chair questions that anchor the strategy around four themes: monitoring data for transparency and accountability, identifying school and institutional performance, understanding what works through research, and translating data and research into action by policymakers and educators.

The strategy's theory of change is organized around four pillars — Demand, Systems, Governance, and Use — drawn from a global good framework. Key exemplars cited include ADRF (Administrative Data Research Facility), RIPL, and TERA as models for building state-level education-to-workforce data ecosystems. The strategy goal is to address gaps in the education-to-workforce data ecosystem so practitioners and policymakers can answer critical questions and implement evidence-based, equitable reforms.

Priority activities identified include: defining core critical questions all states should answer regularly; evaluating what works in K-12 and postsecondary via transition outcome metrics; creating exemplar states; and exploring the role of technology leaders (Microsoft, Amazon, Google) in developing open-source data solutions. Priority outcomes focus on speed (reducing barriers, deploying cloud solutions and data lakes, updating privacy legal frameworks) and specificity and scale (increasing research quality and ensuring data access for researchers, practitioners, and policymakers).

The document describes an extensive strategy development process encompassing external and internal landscape scans of data funders and Global team efforts, a scan of USP data investments, a critical questions framework leveraging state scans, and identification of strategic options including potential big bets over 10–20 year horizons. Deliverables include a 3–5 year strategy execution plan, theory of change and action, operating model and staffing plan, MLE plan, and PAC priorities.

Insights from funder and expert interviews affirmed demand-centered, user-driven approaches and support for public goods orientation, governance, interoperability standards, and open-source solutions. Challenges noted include working within public sector constraints (buy-in, capacity, leadership transitions) and COVID-19's impact on state budgets and priorities, particularly in focus states like California and Texas. The North America team is actively monitoring state conditions to guide investment decisions.

FILE: 061020 USP Postsecondary Success Strategy Goal Targets.pptx
This May 2020 presentation responds to concerns raised during the 2020 Strategy Review about Postsecondary Success (PS) strategy goal-setting methodology, specifically around Goal 1: demonstrating proof of scale with 300–400 institutions increasing postsecondary attainment rates and closing equity gaps by race and income in educational completion and post-college value outcomes. The document outlines a two-part strategy goal structure: Goal 1 focuses on institutional transformation at scale, while Goal 2 focuses on developing public goods (tools, knowledge, infrastructure) to enable broader system improvement. Strategy leadership addressed two specific concerns raised by leadership during the Strategy Review: (1) that relative gap-closing goals are problematic because they can be achieved by reducing performance of higher-performing groups, and (2) a desire for clarity about the absolute size of impact. In response, the PS team committed to translating graduation rate gaps into absolute numeric targets as well as overall graduation rate improvements, not just improvements for focus populations. They also committed to monitoring institutional access rates and selectivity to prevent institutions from gaming outcomes through increased selectivity. The goal-setting methodology employs a hybrid bottom-up and top-down modeling approach. The bottom-up model anchors on effect sizes from transformation approaches aligned with the PS strategy, including the CUNY ASAP program (two-year institutions) and CUNY ACE (four-year institutions), as well as Georgia State University's comprehensive transformation model. These exemplars inform optimistic-case assumptions about completion rate improvements from integrated solution adoption across advising, developmental education reform, and courseware. The presentation notes that individual solution-by-solution adoption is unlikely to yield the ~10–15 percentage point graduation rate gains seen by CUNY's highly structured models, and that research on disaggregated impacts by race and income remains limited. Final strategy goal targets are expected to be derived from institution-level targets set when intermediaries select and meaningfully engage with the 300–400 target institutions, anticipated around 2023. This document is relevant to understanding how PS strategy quantifies impact, sets accountability benchmarks, and operationalizes its equity commitments within the broader USP framework.

Over the past year, the PS strategy has been refining its student outcome goal toward a focus on achieving equitable completion and value.

Postsecondary Success
May 2020
We shared our 2-part goal to (1) improve student outcomes resulting from institutional transformation (essentially proving the system can improve at scale) and (2) develop public goods that contribute to system improvement: 

GOAL 1 (today’s focus): Demonstrate proof of scale with 300-400 institutions increasing postsecondary attainment rates and closing equity gaps (by race and income) in educational completion and post-college (value) outcomes.
We are estimating the impact of our strategy in terms of the number of institutions (our unit of change) supported in their transformation efforts, and the number of additional low-income students and students of color earning credentials of value from these institutions.
We are modeling additional completions (focus population and otherwise) based on the numbers that our prospective focus institutions would yield by increasing productivity to close completion gaps (in race and income).
Final strategy goal targets will be derived from institution-level targets determined when our intermediaries select and meaningfully engage with the 300-400 institutions (2023).
GOAL 2: Develop public goods (including key tools, knowledge, and infrastructure) that will help many more institutions close equity gaps in educational completion and economic outcomes.

This document is Appendix A to a USP Pathways Roundtable presentation, dated September 2020. It presents a draft framework for defining 'good' in education-to-employment pathways spanning grades 9 through 16. The central concept is a comprehensive ideal systems architecture that connects high school to postsecondary education to career entry. The slide text indicates this is explicitly a draft in development, suggesting it was shared for stakeholder input and discussion rather than as a finalized strategic document. The framework appears intended to serve as a normative guide — articulating what a well-functioning pathway system should look like — which is a foundational step in the Gates Foundation U.S. Programs Pathways strategy. By defining quality across the full grades 9-16 continuum, this architecture would support alignment of investments, grantee expectations, and evaluation criteria across the Pathways portfolio. The roundtable format suggests the document was used to facilitate structured dialogue among external partners, practitioners, or internal strategy teams about the components and sequencing of an effective pathway system. This connects directly to USP's broader Pathways strategy, which focuses on ensuring students — particularly students of color and those from low-income backgrounds — can navigate from secondary education through postsecondary credentials and into meaningful careers. The systems architecture framing reflects an interest in coherence and coordination across institutions and actors, rather than isolated interventions. As an appendix, this document likely provided supplementary definitional and conceptual grounding to support the main roundtable agenda, helping participants share a common language around pathway quality. Its development status signals ongoing co-creation and iteration, consistent with how USP often engages external voices in shaping strategy frameworks before formalizing them into investment priorities.

FILE: 090320 USP Pathways Roundtable Presentation Deck.pptx
This September 2020 roundtable presentation deck was prepared as supporting material for a Gates Foundation U.S. Program discussion on high school to postsecondary pathways. The session brought together leaders from three organizations — OneGoal (CEO Melissa Connelly), College Advising Corps (Founder/CEO Nicole Hurd), and Georgetown University Center on Education and the Workforce (Chief Economist Nicole Smith) — to discuss challenges and solutions related to postsecondary transitions for low-income students and students of color.

The roundtable had three core objectives: (1) framing existing barriers for low-income students and students of color in transitioning from high school to postsecondary pathways that lead to economic mobility, with particular attention to how COVID-19 intensified momentum loss risk; (2) sharing successful models for personalized advising that help students get on and persist in postsecondary paths; and (3) exploring how digital delivery platforms can accelerate scale and reach.

Organization profiles highlighted two key models. OneGoal operates a three-year model beginning in students' junior year of high school, embedding postsecondary advising into a credit-bearing course while continuing support through the first year post-graduation. Originally launched in Chicago in 2007, it now serves 13,000 fellows across six regions. College Advising Corps deploys recent college graduates as full-time in-school advisers and virtual eAdvisers to support college enrollment and completion among first-generation, low-income, and underrepresented students at scale nationally.

Georgetown CEW research framed the labor market context, emphasizing that postsecondary education is increasingly a prerequisite for employment in a managerial and professional economy, underscoring the urgency of improving college access and completion for underrepresented students.

This document is strategically relevant to the Gates Foundation's Pathways and Postsecondary portfolios, particularly around personalized advising, digital delivery of support services, and scaling proven models to address the college graduation gap for students of color and low-income students. The timing — September 2020 — situates this discussion squarely within the pandemic context, when concerns about student momentum loss and disengagement from postsecondary pathways were acute. The roundtable appears designed to inform potential investments or partnerships in the advising and college access space.

organization as it tackles one of our nation’s greatest injustices: the college graduation gap. Prior to assuming this role, Melissa served as OneGoal’s Chief Program Officer. In this role, she was chiefly responsible for executing the programmatic vision to ensure all students have a legitimate opportunity to earn a college degree. She has spent over a decade in education leveraging her experiences, empathy, and drive to make certain her accomplishments are not an anomaly, but the norm for underserved youth. Melissa has earned a Bachelor of Arts from the University of Illinois-Chicago, a Master of Arts in Teaching, and a Master of Education Administration from Dominican University. She also recently completed the prestigious Surge Institute Fellowship for emerging leaders of color in education. Melissa is most proud of the work she is doing as a mother of two, Ashland and Addae.

restructuring long-term occupational
and educational projections. This framework forms the underlying methodology for Help Wanted, Recovery and The Future of Work, three reports in an ongoing series that projects education demand for occupations in the U.S. economy.

FILE: 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Appendix A.pptx
This document is Appendix A to the October 2020 USP Data Strategy discussion, presenting a Draft Critical Questions Framework intended to guide the U.S. Program's data and measurement work. The appendix format suggests this framework supplements a broader USP Data Strategy discussion document, serving as a reference tool for operationalizing the strategy across K-12, Postsecondary, Pathways, and other USP focus areas. Strategically, this framework reflects USP's effort in 2020 to build a coherent, cross-cutting data infrastructure that could support evidence-based decision-making at both the foundation and field levels. The emphasis on scaling indicator availability and use points to an ambition not just to track internal grantmaking outcomes, but to influence how partner organizations and the broader education field measure and act on data. This aligns with broader USP priorities around using data to drive equity-focused improvements in educational outcomes. The collaborative approach — working with each USP team individually — suggests the framework was designed to be domain-sensitive while maintaining cross-portfolio coherence.

FILE: 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Appendix F.pptx
This document is Appendix F of the USP Data Strategy discussion materials from October 2020. It outlines how the USP Data Strategy will engage with and support Program Strategy Teams (PSTs) and the North America Team (NAT) in managing data-related investments across the U.S. Program. The appendix references Appendix E for details on working with the North America Team to create enabling conditions for data reform, indicating this is part of a broader, multi-part strategy document. A key feature of this appendix is the proposed USP Data Engagement Model, which defines criteria for determining where individual investments fall within an engagement framework. These criteria include: the level of criticality to the USP Data Theory of Action; the need to coordinate projects, budgets, and staff across multiple PSTs (e.g., workforce data portfolio) or for centralized/joint funding accountable to cross-USP data outcomes; the need for application across all USP data-related investments (e.g., data privacy and security principles); the need for centralized or specialized expertise (e.g., data interoperability, infrastructure development, privacy/security technology); and the need to coordinate relationships and outcomes in specific geographies such as states or with key partners such as ADRF and NSC. The appendix also presents a framework showing how PST and FST (Field Strategy Team) data priorities align to USP Data Engagement Tiers, though the detailed tier content is referenced in a slide not fully captured in the text. This document is relevant for understanding the governance and coordination structure of USP's data investment portfolio, particularly how centralized data functions interact with program-specific teams. It reflects a cross-cutting approach to data strategy that touches K-12, Postsecondary, Pathways, and workforce domains. Staff working on data infrastructure, interoperability, privacy, or cross-team investment coordination within USP should reference this appendix alongside the full October 2020 USP Data Strategy discussion deck.

FILE: 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Appendix H.pptx
This document is Appendix H of the USP Data Strategy discussion materials from October 2020, focused on data de-identification as a primer for Gates Foundation U.S. Programs (USP) staff and partners. The appendix appears to be a supporting technical reference document accompanying a broader USP Data Strategy presentation or discussion package. Data de-identification is a foundational concept in responsible data use, particularly relevant to education-focused philanthropic work where student-level data privacy is both a legal and ethical priority. De-identification refers to the process of removing or obscuring personally identifiable information (PII) from datasets so that individuals cannot be readily identified, enabling data to be used for analysis, research, and program improvement while protecting individual privacy. In the context of USP's data strategy, this primer likely supports the foundation's efforts to build robust data infrastructure and governance frameworks that allow grantees, partners, and internal teams to leverage educational data — such as student outcomes, demographic information, and program participation records — in ways that comply with laws like FERPA (Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act) and other applicable privacy regulations. The inclusion of this technical appendix within the broader USP Data Strategy discussion signals that the foundation was investing in helping stakeholders understand the technical underpinnings of responsible data sharing and use. This is consistent with USP's broader strategic interest in using data to drive equitable outcomes across K-12, Postsecondary, and Pathways work. The document connects to cross-cutting USP priorities around evidence-building, learning agendas, and ensuring that data practices center racial equity by enabling disaggregated analysis while protecting vulnerable student populations. As part of the October 2020 USP Data Strategy series, this primer would have informed foundation staff and partners on best practices for handling sensitive education data within grant-making and program evaluation contexts.

FILE: 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Email.pdf
This email, sent by Adam Petkun on October 13, 2020, serves as the distribution cover for pre-read materials ahead of the USP Data Strategy Discussion scheduled for October 20, 2020. The meeting was led by Allan Golston with Jennifer Engle as content owner. The email distributes a comprehensive set of 13 documents outlining the U.S. Program's proposed data strategy across its education-to-workforce portfolio.

The pre-read package includes a proposal memo, presentation deck, and eight appendices covering distinct aspects of the data strategy. Key areas addressed include: a critical questions framework to measure performance along the education-to-workforce pipeline (P16W); case studies on powering regional P16W initiatives with state data from the Statewide Exemplars Book of Work (BOW); scaling postsecondary and workforce data for impact through the National Systems BOW; leveraging Student Information System (SIS) and Learning Management System (LMS) data through the R&D BOW; and activating Policy, Advocacy, and Communications (PAC) context to ensure supportive enabling conditions for data reform.

Additional appendices address how the USP Data Strategy will engage with and support Place-based Strategy Teams (PSTs) and the National Advocacy Team (NAT), a systems framework for the data strategy, and a primer on data de-identification practices.

The distribution list includes senior USP leadership and program staff such as Bob Hughes, Bryan Richardson, Dan Kaufman, Jean-Claude Brizard, June Davis, Patrick Methvin, Peter Koziol, Rhett Dornbach-Bender, Robert Nabors, Ryan Rippel, Sara Allan, Sidney Hacker, Tiffany Smith, and Randy Pinol, indicating the cross-cutting, program-wide significance of this data strategy discussion.

Strategically, this document package represents a significant moment in USP's effort to build a coherent, cross-cutting data infrastructure to support its postsecondary and workforce goals. The focus on state data systems, institutional-level SIS/LMS data, and enabling policy conditions reflects USP's ambition to use data as a lever for equitable student outcomes across the education-to-workforce continuum. The inclusion of de-identification and PAC context signals attention to responsible data use and the policy environment necessary to sustain data reform at scale.

FILE: 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Presentation Deck.pptx
This October 2020 presentation outlines a proposed USP Data Strategy developed by the USP Data team, led by Jennifer Engle (DD, USP Data), with involvement from K-12, Postsecondary, Pathways, North America Team, and Foundation Strategy Office staff. The overarching goal is to strategically address gaps in the education-to-workforce data ecosystem so that practitioners and policymakers have the information needed to implement evidence-based reforms that equitably improve outcomes for students of color and low-income students.

The strategy is organized around six investment areas: (1) Scale National Systems — supporting a voluntary national student-level postsecondary data system via NSC and a cross-state data platform via ADRF; (2) Build Statewide Exemplars — directly supporting Texas agencies in upgrading P-20W data infrastructure, with learnings applicable to priority states like California and Washington; (3) Innovate with R&D — supporting demonstration projects with SIS/LMS providers in Texas districts to make local microdata accessible and actionable; (4) Develop Public Goods — creating common equity-focused metrics frameworks, interoperability standards, and promoting FAIR(S) data access; (5) Support Local Use — providing technical assistance and tools for local data-driven decision-making; and (6) Enable Research and Evaluation — supporting evidence generation and dissemination across USP investment areas.

The three-year strategic focus (2021–2023) prioritizes the first four investment areas, with particular emphasis on Texas as a statewide exemplar. The strategy aligns with USP's K-12, Pathways, and Postsecondary programmatic work, targeting grade 9 through workforce indicators from a Critical Questions Framework. Key equity concerns center on the invisibility of Black, Latinx, Asian, Indigenous, and low-income students in education data systems due to lack of disaggregation.

The presentation was structured as a meeting discussion seeking approval to move the strategy through Annual Planning and into execution planning. Discussion questions addressed whether the strategy is compelling as an integrated approach, whether it balances ambition with practicality, whether major gaps exist, and whether leadership is aligned for approval. A proposed three-year budget request for 2021–2023 was included in the appendix, noted as subject to refinement given ongoing budget reallocation across the US Program.

FILE: 110920 Postsecondary EdTech Roundtable Presentation Deck.pptx
This October 2020 roundtable presentation deck compiles perspectives from three EdTech partners — EdSolutions, Zearn, and Lumen — convened as part of a postsecondary EdTech roundtable supported by the Gates Foundation. Although the filename references postsecondary, the substantive content of the extracted slides skews heavily toward K-12 math learning, with Zearn occupying the majority of the deck.

EdSolutions and its affiliated Center for Education Market Dynamics (CEMD), incubated at Cambiar Education with BMGF support, are positioned as market intelligence providers for the K-12 sector. Their model aims to offer free data to school districts on purchasing behavior and outcomes, while charging for-profit entities and researchers for analytics — creating a sustainable, mission-aligned business model to surface clearer market signals between buyers and providers of educational tools.

Zearn is presented as a nonprofit K-5 (expanding to middle school and Algebra I) math learning platform built around the principle that all students, particularly Black, brown, and female students, can be 'math kids.' Key highlights include: an EdReports top-rated curriculum integrating hands-on and digital learning; strong COVID-19 response including multilingual parent resources, remote learning supports, and Android device compatibility; rapid user growth from 15,000 to 6.3 million students since 2014-15; earned revenue growing to $14.5M; and academic outcome data showing a 64% increase in likelihood of proficiency for all students and 124% for previously struggling students through consistent platform use. Zearn's equity strategy includes a universally free student experience, affordable adult/school pricing, diverse on-screen representation, and intentional design for learning differences.

A particularly notable data point: in March 2020, Zearn's usage data diverged sharply by student income level for the first time, revealing COVID-19's unequal impact on learning access. Zearn partnered with Opportunity Insights to make this data publicly available, driving national visibility into income- and geography-based disparities in learning continuity.

The deck connects to USP strategy through its focus on equity-centered EdTech scaling, market infrastructure for education innovation, and evidence of narrowing achievement gaps through technology-enabled math instruction. The roundtable format suggests a convening role for the Gates Foundation in aligning EdTech partners around shared goals of equitable access and measurable student outcomes.

FILE: 11162020 K-12 Education Learning Session - Presentation Deck.pptx
This November 2020 presentation deck was prepared for a K-12 Education Roundtable convening thought leaders to discuss the impact of COVID-19 on K-12 education, with a particular focus on Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty. The session had three objectives: understanding the emerging pandemic-era K-12 picture for underserved students, highlighting successful remote and hybrid learning models, and identifying opportunities to strengthen the field.

The deck documents significant disparities in school reopening: schools serving students in poverty were 48% remote versus 28% in lower-poverty districts, and districts serving Black and Latino students were three times as likely to start remotely. Projected learning loss is expected to be greatest among Black, Latino, and low-income students, particularly in math and STEM. Student wellbeing challenges—including trauma, depression, and safety concerns at home—are highlighted as compounding academic obstacles.

On the digital divide, the presentation notes 15–16 million K-12 public school students (roughly 30%) lack adequate internet or devices for distance learning. The Gates Foundation, alongside the Walton Foundation, is funding a landscape analysis estimating 60% of disconnected students lack both internet and an adequate device. Disconnection disproportionately affects students of color and low-income households.

Two grantee organizations are featured: Chiefs for Change and Curriculum Associates. Chiefs for Change is highlighted for scenario planning tools (a 'Day in the Life' toolkit), federal advocacy on the digital divide (raising $700K for devices and connectivity, facilitating Congressional testimony), and a Teaching and Learning Community of Practice with 75+ system leaders serving 20 million students. Their Johns Hopkins University partnership supports curriculum audits in multiple districts, with Baltimore City, Aldine ISD, and Providence Public Schools transitioning to high-quality instructional materials.

Curriculum Associates presents its i-Ready platform, emphasizing assessment data actionability, data-driven goal-setting, grade-level rigor, and the role of supplemental instruction. Core beliefs center on making assessment data understandable and ensuring students achieve conceptual understanding.

The document connects to USP K-12 strategy by framing COVID-19 as both a crisis and an opportunity to advance equity, strengthen instructional quality through HQIM, and close persistent resource and opportunity gaps for the foundation's priority student populations.

FILE: 111820 Postsecondary Value Commission Appendix A.pptx
This presentation is Appendix A from the Postsecondary Value Commission, shared with Gates Foundation's Postsecondary Success team in November 2020. It outlines a framework for defining, measuring, and assessing postsecondary value for students — with particular emphasis on equity for students of color (Black, Latinx, Indigenous, and AAPI), low-income students, and women.

The document is organized around three core components: (1) defining and measuring student investment, (2) measuring economic returns via thresholds, and (3) incorporating non-economic benefits. The Commission's definition of postsecondary value centers on access, affordability, credential quality, economic security, and the advancement of racial and economic justice.

On student investment, the Commission proposes expanding the federal Cost of Attendance (COA) definition to include costs often overlooked — such as health insurance, internet, cell phone plans, and living expenses for students residing with family. The framework also calls for including students who pay full sticker price (currently excluded from federal net price data) in institutional value assessments.

The economic returns section introduces a tiered threshold model ranging from Threshold 0 (minimum return on investment / break-even) to Threshold 5 (wealth parity). Thresholds 0–3 are operationalizable using College Scorecard data, while Thresholds 4–5 (wealth-related) are conceptual due to data limitations. The framework measures outcomes at 1, 3, 5, 10, and 15 years post-completion or departure, and emphasizes disaggregation by race, income, and gender.

The presentation also examines key determinants of institutional value performance, identifying selectivity, diversity, graduation rates, net price, program mix (e.g., % STEM), and institutional expenditures as correlated factors. Graduation rate is highlighted as an actionable lever — institutions with higher graduation rates are more likely to pass Threshold 0 — though the data show that similar graduation rates can yield vastly different outcomes depending on institutional context. University of Texas Rio Grande Valley and UT Permian Basin are cited as high-performing, highly-diverse institutions, contrasted with less diverse, lower-value institutions with comparable graduation rates.

This document connects directly to USP strategy by supporting data infrastructure and accountability frameworks that help identify which institutions and programs deliver genuine value to underserved students, informing both policy advocacy and institutional improvement efforts.

[Slide 1]
Assessing Value for Students through institutions and programs
1
Postsecondary Success
November 18, 2020
To uncover inequities and move toward greater parity, it is important to examine postsecondary returns specifically for students of color (Black, Latinx, Indigenous, and AAPI), low-income students, and women compared with their peers. 
This section explores three components we have examined to conceptualize and measure value to students.
Defining and Measuring Student Investment

A definition and measurement framework for postsecondary value must consider students’ investment, in addition to their returns.
Measuring Economic Returns Via Thresholds

The framework examines the value students receive through a series of thresholds ranging from minimum economic return to wealth parity. We have tested these thresholds using student-level data from the University of Texas System and publicly-available institution-level data from the College Scorecard.
Incorporating Non-Economic Benefits

In addition to the wage and wealth premiums associated with postsecondary attainment, there is considerable evidence that demonstrates an array of non-economic benefits.

Definition: Students experience postsecondary value when provided access and support to complete quality, affordable credentials that offer economic security and prepare them to advance racial and economic justice in our society.

FILE: 111820 Postsecondary Value Commission Appendix D.pptx
This document is Appendix D of materials prepared for the Postsecondary Value Commission, dated November 18, 2020, and presented under the Postsecondary Success portfolio. The slide deck is focused on identifying and addressing risks and mitigations associated with the Postsecondary Value Commission's work. The Postsecondary Value Commission was a Gates Foundation-supported initiative aimed at defining and measuring the economic and social value of a postsecondary credential, with a particular emphasis on equity and outcomes for students from underserved and low-income backgrounds. As an appendix document, this presentation likely served as a supplementary analytical tool for internal Gates Foundation discussions or commission meetings, cataloging potential risks to the commission's research, recommendations, or public communications and pairing those risks with corresponding mitigation strategies. The framing of 'risks and mitigations' suggests the document addressed concerns such as methodological challenges in defining postsecondary value, potential political or reputational risks associated with the commission's findings, equity-related risks of measuring value in ways that disadvantage certain student populations or institution types, and operational risks in commission governance and stakeholder engagement. In the broader USP strategy context, the Postsecondary Value Commission represented a significant investment in shaping the national policy and research landscape around credential value and return on investment for higher education. Its outputs were intended to influence how institutions, policymakers, and students understand the economic return and equity implications of postsecondary pathways. This appendix document would have supported Foundation staff and commission leadership in proactively managing uncertainties that could undermine the commission's credibility or impact. The document connects to Gates Foundation's broader postsecondary strategy by reinforcing the importance of evidence-based definitions of institutional and credential value, ensuring that equity considerations remain central to value frameworks, and managing the reputational and strategic risks inherent in a high-profile, multi-stakeholder initiative. FILE: 111820 Postsecondary Value Commission Appendix E.pptx
This document is Appendix E from the Postsecondary Value Commission's Action Agenda, presented to Gates Foundation Postsecondary Success staff on November 18, 2020. It outlines a comprehensive set of complementary recommendations directed at three audiences — institutional leaders, federal policymakers, and state policymakers — organized across five core categories: (1) Equalize Access to Increase Value, (2) Remove Affordability as an Impediment to Value, (3) Eliminate Completion Gaps to Ensure Value, (4) Improve Data to Expose and Address Inequitable Value, and (5) Promote Justice by Providing Equitable Value.

For institutions, recommendations include equitable recruiting and admissions policies, redirecting institutional aid toward unmet need and eliminating loan debt for Black, Latinx, Indigenous, AAPI, and low-income students, aligning learning outcomes to labor market needs, and conducting equity audits. For federal policymakers, key asks include doubling the Pell Grant, expanding federal need-based aid and grants for Minority-Serving Institutions, implementing a federal student-level data network with disaggregated post-college outcomes, and enhancing the College Scorecard. State-level recommendations focus on outcomes-based funding, increased appropriations to MSIs and community colleges, transfer pathway improvements, and statewide equity audits.

A second slide presents questions every institution should be able to answer for prospective students and families, covering demographic composition, cost, debt burden, completion rates, earnings outcomes, return on investment timelines, and equity audit status. All metrics are to be disaggregated by race, income, and gender.

The document notes that bold text indicates alignment with current or planned BMGF program or policy work, and italics indicate potential future work, signaling strategic prioritization within Gates Foundation's postsecondary portfolio. Key strategic context includes the Commission's intent to apply pressure on college ranking systems such as US News & World Report and the Washington Monthly's plan to incorporate the Commission's findings into its 2021 rankings. Funder partners have expressed interest in supporting consumer-information components of the broader 'Value Movement.' This document is directly relevant to BMGF's postsecondary equity and data transparency strategy, particularly around institutional accountability, federal and state policy advocacy, and the use of disaggregated data to close racial and income gaps in postsecondary outcomes.

[Slide 1]
Action agenda from the postsecondary Value COmmission
Complementary recommendations for institutions and policymakers across five core categories
1
Postsecondary Success
November 18, 2020
Equalize Access to Increase Value
Remove Affordability as an Impediment to Value
Eliminate Completion Gaps to Ensure Value
Improve Data to Expose and Address Inequitable Value
Promote Justice by Providing Equitable Value
Increase access through equitable recruiting, enrollment, and admissions policies at the institution and program level to reduce stratification by race, income, and gender.
Allocate institutional aid toward meeting unmet need and eliminating loan debt for Black, Latinx, Indigenous, AAPI, and low-income students and connect students to public benefits programs as needed for further support.
Equalize students’ pathways to degree completion and career opportunities by aligning learning outcomes to labor market needs, enhancing advising including career services, and offering meaningful in-school employment opportunities (in partnership with the business community) that build students’ skills, social capital, and networks.
Use data to reorient institutional strategic plans and accreditation self-studies toward the goal of equitable value and develop a culture dedicated to using data to identify and remedy enrollment, completion, and value gaps for Black, Latinx, Indigenous, AAPI, low-income students and women.
Promote a more just society by preparing students to combat injustice throughout their careers; creating a more equitable campus climate; instituting equitable hiring, promotion, and pay practices to ensure a diverse faculty and staff; actively engaging with local communities to promote equity and justice.
Increase federal support for college access programs to continue to increase postsecondary enrollment for Black, Latinx, Indigenous, AAPI, and low-income students.
Dramatically increase federal need-based aid (e.g., double the Pell grant) for low-income students and federal grants for Minority-Serving Institutions to equalize financial support for students of color, low-income students, and the institutions that serve them.
Incentivize institutions by making equitable completion and value (e.g., meeting minimum thresholds) a factor in colleges’ eligibility for federal grants and loans, and incentivize employers to have a stake in equitable value through federal employee education assistance policies.
Implement a federal student-level data network that provides disaggregated information about all students’ post-college outcomes (e.g., employment, earnings, loan repayment) and enhance the College Scorecard to provide transparency into which colleges are (or are not) providing value to students of color, low-income students, and women.
Require all Title IV institutions to conduct and report on an equity audit that assesses the impact of their policies and practices on Black, Latinx, Indigenous AAPI, and low-income students and women,

FILE: 111820 Postsecondary Value Commission Appendix F.pptx
This presentation slide (Appendix F) from the November 2020 Postsecondary Value Commission briefing outlines a strategy for 'building a value movement' by leveraging broad partner collaboration to advance and amplify the Commission's findings. The document describes an ecosystem of interconnected actors organized around three pillars: programmatic work, policy advocacy, and data and research.

A central element is the Scaling Partners Network (SPN), comprising 27 core programmatic and policy advocacy partner organizations that have collectively committed to amplifying discourse around equitable value and economic mobility. The SPN is expected to expand to include additional mission-aligned partners. Several SPN members are also positioned to participate in the forthcoming Value Data Collaborative, which will work with postsecondary institutions to voluntarily collect and analyze institutional data against the Value Framework developed by the Commission.

On the policy advocacy side, coalitions such as PostsecData and the Third Way Alliance are identified as key vehicles for advancing the Commission's recommendations at the policy level. These coalitions represent a parallel track to the programmatic and data efforts, ensuring that findings translate into actionable policy change.

The Funder Collaborative — a group of foundations engaged with the Value Commission — has shown early interest in co-funding components of the broader movement, signaling potential for leveraging BMGF investments with external philanthropic capital.

Strategically, the document frames the Postsecondary Value Commission as a catalytic force capable of generating a 'ripple effect' across programmatic, policy, and research domains. The integration of the SPN, Value Data Collaborative, and policy advocacy coalitions reflects a coordinated theory of change aimed at institutionalizing the concept of equitable postsecondary value — ensuring that credentials lead to meaningful economic mobility, particularly for students of color and low-income students.

This connects directly to BMGF's U.S. Postsecondary Success strategy by operationalizing a value framework that holds institutions accountable for student outcomes and by mobilizing a cross-sector coalition to sustain and scale these efforts beyond the Commission's immediate work.

FILE: 111820 Postsecondary Value Commission Presentation Deck.pptx
This November 2020 presentation deck documents a Gates Foundation meeting with the Postsecondary Value Commission, a body launched in April 2019 comprising 30 experts and thought leaders charged with defining, measuring, and advocating for equitable postsecondary value. Key attendees included USP President Allan Golston, Chief Strategy Advisor Sue Desmond-Hellmann (also serving as Commission Co-Chair), Postsecondary Success Director Patrick Methvin, and USP Data staff including Jennifer Engle and Jamey Rorison.

The Commission developed three core deliverables: (1) a shared definition of postsecondary value — 'Students experience postsecondary value when provided access and support to complete quality, affordable credentials that offer economic security and prepare them to advance racial and economic justice in our society'; (2) a measurement framework for how institutions and programs create equitable value, particularly for students of color, low-income students, and women; and (3) a policy, advocacy, and communications action agenda for widespread application of the definition and framework.

The presentation outlines five core principles: Equity Matters, Institutions and Programs Matter, Policy Matters, Public Returns Matter, and Measuring Value Matters. These principles frame postsecondary education as currently functioning as a 'great divider' rather than a 'great equalizer,' particularly in the context of COVID-19 disruptions that have intensified student questioning of college value.

A comprehensive Value Framework is presented, covering equitable access, affordability, support, completion, earnings, and wealth — with institutions positioned as economic engines, justice educators, equitable employers, and engaged community members. The framework also incorporates broader societal outcomes including GDP contributions, civic engagement, and public revenues.

An economic returns measurement approach using threshold tiers (Thresholds 0–5) is introduced, with short- and long-term outcome tracking at 1, 3, 5, 10, and 15 years post-completion or departure. Thresholds 0–3 can be estimated using College Scorecard data; Thresholds 4–5 (related to wealth) remain conceptual due to data limitations.

Public opinion data from a Glover Park Group survey (March 2020) commissioned by BMGF shows that while most voters, parents, and students view college as necessary and valuable, concerns about cost and debt temper enthusiasm, and equity perceptions are divided — with a slight majority of students and parents recognizing disparate value for students of color and low-income students.

This work connects directly to USP's Postsecondary Success strategy, focusing on Black, Latinx, Indigenous, and low-income students, and supports broader field-building efforts around institutional accountability, equitable outcomes data infrastructure, and policy reform.

FILE: 121020 Postsecondary Advising Appendix A.pptx
This appendix presentation, dated December 2020 and sourced from BCG, provides a detailed case study of the advising-related technology stack at Community College of Philadelphia (CCP) as an illustrative example of institution-level and market-wide challenges in postsecondary advising technology. The document is structured around CCP's advising redesign effort, which began in 2015 following a 10% graduation rate in 2014 — significantly below the national community college average and with especially low rates for African American males. By 2018, CCP's 150% first-time full-time graduation rate had improved to 16%, but remained well below the ~30% national benchmark.

The presentation identifies several market-wide pain points in advising technology: vendors overselling capabilities (e.g., Civitas), lack of institutional guidance for tech selection, low trust in tools slowing adoption, and poor interoperability among products such as Banner (Ellucian), Starfish, Degree Works, and CCP's own website. CCP's tech stack requires advisors to use approximately five tools simultaneously and build degree plans manually in Excel, with no pre-built logic for two-year degree paths. The Starfish degree planner implementation took over two years rather than the expected six months, largely due to the complexity of CCP's prerequisites and the diversity of its student population.

From the student perspective, the disconnected tools create significant friction: students must navigate multiple platforms to identify required courses, check transfer eligibility, and schedule advising appointments. Mobile accessibility is poor, with Degree Works not mobile-friendly, and Banner's registration app lacking degree planning logic. Students risk registering for incorrect or non-transferable courses due to the absence of integrated sequencing and prerequisite logic.

Advisor appointments are consumed by degree audit navigation rather than meaningful student support, with ratios reaching 2,000:1 and limited reach to non-core-major and non-degree-seeking students. The presentation also highlights a lack of data continuity across systems — advisors cannot see prior conversations with admissions, and academic data mismanagement leads to student confusion and mistrust of technology.

Key success factors cited include cohort-based technical assistance (via IPASS and AACC Pathways), dedicated institutional leadership through a newly created VP of Academic and Student Success role, and cross-institutional buy-in. However, the conclusion is that the supply side has failed to meet institutional needs, with outdated, poorly integrated products that increase transactional burdens rather than enabling self-service and equity-focused advising reform. This document is relevant to Gates Foundation USP postsecondary strategy around technology-enabled advising, student success infrastructure, and equity gap closure at community colleges.

FILE: 121020 Postsecondary Advising Appendix B.pptx
This document is Appendix B of a December 2020 Postsecondary Success presentation prepared by Tyton Partners for the Gates Foundation, focusing on market dynamics within the postsecondary advising and student success technology sector. It provides a detailed competitive landscape analysis intended to inform foundation strategy around technology investments that support student advising and success.

The advising technology market is characterized by a bifurcated structure: approximately 10 incumbent providers hold roughly 50% of market share and dominate core solution categories (Analytics & Reporting, Course Planning & Degree Audit, Identification of At-Risk Students, and Caseload Management), while a long tail of 100+ upstarts compete across point solution categories. Key incumbents include EAB, Blackboard, Hobsons, Salesforce, Aviso, and Anthology, with several also controlling Student Information Systems (SIS), giving them outsized data leverage.

The document identifies two major structural problems in the market. First, incumbents have underinvested in student support technology, relying on legacy platforms, limiting product innovation, and actively resisting interoperability with competitors to protect their market positions. Their control of enterprise systems like SIS creates integration barriers that disadvantage both upstarts and institutions. Second, upstarts in non-core categories face significant scaling challenges: difficulty achieving institutional sales due to incumbent gatekeeping of SIS data, burdensome integration requirements, and limited ability to leverage student data effectively.

The market trajectory analysis (without foundation intervention) projects a slow evolution: over 5–7 years, institutional demand for integrated solutions may pressure incumbents into partnerships with strategically important upstarts, eventually leading to market consolidation over 7+ years. Importantly, the document notes that BMGF target institutions—those with constrained adoption capacity, limited funds, and fewer internal resources—will experience these market shifts more slowly than well-resourced institutions, suggesting that market forces alone will not adequately serve the foundation's priority populations.

From a strategic standpoint, this appendix supports the foundation's postsecondary success work by mapping the structural barriers that prevent effective advising technology from reaching underserved students and institutions. It frames a potential role for philanthropic intervention to accelerate interoperability, support mission-aligned upstarts, and counteract incumbent tendencies that deprioritize innovation for under-resourced institutions. The analysis is grounded in Tyton Partners' market sizing data, Pitchbook funding data, and the D2D 2020 survey, providing an evidence base for investment or field-building decisions in the postsecondary advising technology space.
