FILE: 121020 Postsecondary Advising Appendix C.pptx
This appendix presentation, dated December 2020, provides two detailed case study exemplars related to postsecondary advising and student success coaching, likely in support of a broader postsecondary strategy document or investment narrative.

The first case study examines Carolina Works, a randomized controlled trial (RCT) conducted across 10 North Carolina community colleges funded through the U.S. Department of Education's First in the World grant. The initiative tested proactive, technology-mediated success coaching using predictive analytics and case management tools from Aviso Retention. Key findings include: coaching impacts grow over time as coaches build relationships with students; implementation fidelity is critical — colleges with highest fidelity scores saw the greatest outcome gains; coach stability matters — students at institutions without coach turnover experienced greater benefits; male and Black students showed notable gains in retention outcomes. The Carolina Works model aligns with what the document calls SSIPP principles: personalized, holistic, proactive, sustained coaching with strategic use of early alert systems.

The second case study profiles the University of South Florida (USF), a large public R1 institution in Tampa with 32,000+ undergraduates and a 36% Pell percentage. USF launched a university-wide student success initiative in 2009, accelerated by an IPASS grant in 2015 that enabled a case management approach. Key outcomes include retention rate growth from 88% to 91% and six-year graduation rate improvement from 68% to 75%. USF's approach centered on breaking down institutional silos between student affairs, academic affairs, and IT; forming cross-functional persistence committees; and adopting an agile, centralized technology infrastructure using MuleSoft integration, Appian, and home-grown predictive analytics tools. Lessons learned emphasize that technology alone is insufficient — institutional culture supportive of student success must precede technology adoption, and trained professionals who care about students are irreplaceable.

Together, these case studies illustrate core principles relevant to Gates Foundation USP postsecondary strategy: the importance of proactive and personalized advising models, the role of predictive analytics and integrated technology stacks, equity-focused targeting to close outcome gaps for underserved students, and the institutional conditions (leadership commitment, cross-functional collaboration, implementation fidelity) required for advising interventions to succeed at scale. These exemplars likely informed grant-making or strategy development around student success infrastructure investments.

FILE: 121020 Postsecondary Advising Appendix D.pptx
This December 2020 confidential appendix presentation documents BMGF's postsecondary advising investment history and forward-looking strategy through 2023. It traces three phases of BMGF advising strategy from 2010 to present: (1) Connecting Advising and Completion (2010–2014), (2) Expanding Advising Technology Access (2015–2017), and (3) Embedding Advising Within Institutional Transformation (2018–present). A central prior initiative was IPASS (Integrated Planning and Advising for Student Success), funded 2013–2017, which aimed to elevate advising as a priority and accelerate the advising technology market through both supply- and demand-side strategies. BMGF's historical approach largely subsidized demand and built institutional awareness of technology but did not directly fund the supply side. Key market players referenced include Hobsons/Starfish/PAR and EAB/Grades First. The document introduces SSIPP (an evidence-based framework for advising redesign) as a foundation for future investments, noting that SSIPP-aligned programs such as CUNY's ASAP and Portland Community College's Advising Redesign Initiative have demonstrated improved retention and completion rates across racial and ethnic subgroups. The 2021–2023 theory of action focuses on a $4.9M four-year demonstration investment aimed at addressing root-cause barriers including departmental silos, disconnected technology stacks, and protective incumbents. Strategies include testing use of data standards, exploring integrated solutions, and executing a robust learning agenda. An additional $1.4M in remaining funding is allocated to a 2021–2022 research agenda to inform next-stage high-impact plays. Multiple alternative theories of action were evaluated against four criteria: scale of potential impact, timeline to impact, reliance on third parties, and execution complexity. The Foundation ultimately prioritized addressing lack of integration in the advising ecosystem as the primary strategic focus before pursuing larger investments in 2023. This document connects to USP's postsecondary success strategy by framing advising infrastructure and technology integration as levers for improving student completion outcomes, particularly for underserved student populations.

Advising is a key lever in the Postsecondary Success portfolio, as high-quality advising supports students in making informed decisions about college enrollment, persistence, and completion. This meeting likely addressed the foundation's investments or strategic direction related to advising interventions, technology-enabled advising solutions, or partnerships with institutions and intermediaries to scale effective advising practices.

The distribution of this email to co-chairs and a broad internal audience reflects the governance structure of USP strategy review meetings, where senior leadership engages with program teams on portfolio priorities and potential investment decisions. The inclusion of multiple appendices alongside a core memo and presentation deck indicates the meeting was designed to support substantive discussion and possibly decision-making around the Postsecondary Advising sub-strategy. This document serves primarily as an administrative and logistical record of pre-read distribution rather than containing strategic content itself, but it indexes the full body of materials reviewed at the December 10, 2020 session.

FILE: 121020 Postsecondary Advising Presentation Deck.pptx
This December 2020 confidential presentation deck was prepared for a Gates Foundation Postsecondary Success advising learning session, bringing together internal USP staff and external experts including representatives from Aviso Retention, EAB, ASU Enterprise Partners, Tyton Partners, Boston Consulting Group, USC Center for Urban Education, EDUCAUSE, and others. The document presents the strategic rationale for Gates Foundation investment in postsecondary advising technology and the integration landscape.

The core argument is that technology integration is the critical bottleneck preventing postsecondary institutions from scaling effective, equitable advising practices. The presentation frames the current state as one in which departmental silos, disconnected technology product stacks, and an oligopolistic vendor market together create a confusing and inefficient student experience. The ideal future state envisions integrated technologies serving as a catalyst for high-quality, student-centered advising that produces dramatic and equitable improvements in outcomes and educational value.

The strategic framework centers on the SSIPP design principles (Sustained, Strategic, Integrated, Proactive, Personalized) and situates improved student advising as a core enabler within the broader Guided Pathways transformation framework. The document notes that Gates Foundation investments over the prior decade helped create the advising technology category and produced evidence-based frameworks, but that integration barriers have intensified, requiring external intervention to solve market failures around interoperability.

Key data points highlight persistent equity gaps: graduation rates at two-year institutions increased only 0.9 percentage points (2010–2016) and the equity gap for underrepresented minority and low-income students widened during this period. The presentation emphasizes that personalized advising support, amplified by appropriate technology, can improve retention, graduation rates, and time-to-degree while reducing excess credit accumulation.

COVID-19 is noted as a contextual factor increasing technology adoption and favorable institutional perception, though the underlying integration challenges are expected to worsen as adoption grows. The strategic opportunity identified is to advance the integration solution and services market as the highest-impact near-term lever. This work connects directly to the USP Postsecondary Success strategy goal of creating student experiences that result in dramatic and equitable increases in outcomes and educational value, with a secondary aim of generating broader market change and public goods beyond the foundation's direct grantees.

FILE: 5a USP Global Good 2020 Appendix A USP Data Strategy Initiative 011420.pptx
This appendix presentation, dated December 2019 and included in the USP Global Good 2020 materials, outlines the early development of a USP Data Strategy Initiative focused on identifying core metrics and the comprehensive data systems needed to answer critical research questions across the education-to-workforce pipeline at scale. The document draws on a model previously used to develop the Postsecondary (PS) Data Strategy approximately five years prior, in which internal and external partners collaborated to coalesce around a consensus set of postsecondary metrics and build a roadmap for improving public data systems.

The strategy framework is organized around four interconnected components: (1) developing critical research questions across the P20W+ pipeline, (2) identifying a common core set of metrics, (3) mapping those metrics to comprehensive and connected data systems, and (4) enabling robust research and use. The document distinguishes between bespoke and scaled data collection and emphasizes a feedback loop between research use and data system improvement.

The presentation categorizes research use cases into four levels of analytical complexity — descriptive, exploratory, diagnostic, and predictive — and maps each to the types and granularity of data required. It notes that national or state aggregate data (e.g., IPEDS) are sufficient for descriptive analysis but inadequate for diagnostic or predictive work, which require student-level data linked across local, state, and national systems.

A key section surveys the current education-to-workforce data ecosystem to identify gaps, highlighting variability in data collection across P20W systems and the underutilization of local, student-level data for research. Example schemas for K–12 and postsecondary state P20W data systems are presented, covering domains such as student identifiers, outcomes, educational activities, interventions, conditions, and resources. Texas and Washington are cited as example state P20W systems. The National Student Clearinghouse and institutional student information systems are also referenced.

The document signals the need to revisit and expand an existing USP P16 indicators framework to incorporate metrics related to mobility and value, and to align with the PS metrics framework and ongoing Mobility metrics scoping work. Overall, this appendix establishes the strategic rationale and early conceptual architecture for a coordinated USP data strategy aimed at improving the foundation for research and reform across the full education-to-workforce continuum.

FILE: K-12 Education Co-Chair Presentation 01272020.pptx
This January 2020 co-chair presentation provides an overview of the Gates Foundation K-12 Education strategy for the 2020 strategy review cycle. It introduces the K-12 team leadership structure, including the Director, multiple Deputy Directors covering Solutions, Networks for School Improvement (NSI), Charters and Teacher Preparation, Strategy Planning and Management, and Education R&D.

The strategy framework organizes investments into four categories: (A) R&D of New Solutions, (B) Emerging and Existing Solutions, (C) Place-Based Models, and (D) Model Codification, all supported by (E) Enabling Conditions. The overarching goal is to significantly increase the number of Black, Latinx, and low-income students who earn a high school diploma, enroll in postsecondary institutions, and are on-track in their first year to earn a credential with labor market value.

The portfolio overview references multiple bodies of work rated by impact performance, with Coherent Instructional Systems highlighted as a key focus area. Additional K-12-focused work managed by the Global Policy and Advocacy team is referenced but presented separately.

A substantial deep dive covers Networks for School Improvement (NSI), presenting Cohort 1 baseline outcome and implementation data from school year 2018-19. Outcomes are drawn from local network measures, with a transition to standardized measures beginning in 2020. Named network partners include Baltimore, New Visions, Network for College Success, CORE, and CA Ed Partners. Implementation assessments use a multi-source methodology including Program Officer check-ins, formative evaluation from Columbia University, and network health surveys from the University of Pittsburgh.

Cost analysis by Education Resource Strategies shows relatively similar annual per-school costs across networks, with the majority of school-level NSI costs potentially coverable through reallocation of existing resources. Key resource requirements include content-focused coaching, common planning time, teacher professional development, and data management capacity.

A learning and evidence timeline maps expected evidence milestones annually through a key Q4 2022 decision point, at which the foundation will determine whether to maintain, adapt, scale, or stop NSI investments. This presentation connects directly to USP K-12 strategy by framing the portfolio logic, surfacing early implementation evidence, and setting up the co-chair review of strategic priorities for 2020.

FILE: K-12 Education Investment Plan 01272020.pdf
This document presents the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation US Program K-12 Education Financial Resource Plan for 2020–2023, prepared as part of the 2020 Strategy Review. It outlines planned investments totaling $1,433.2 million across four years, organized into three primary investment groups: Solutions, Delivery, and Enabling Conditions, plus a legacy College Ready Strategy line.

The current (2020) plan allocates the largest share to Solutions at $681.8M (47.6% of total), followed by Delivery at $512.1M (35.7%), and Enabling Conditions at $237.1M (16.5%). The legacy College Ready Strategy receives a minimal $2.3M (0.2%), reflecting its wind-down status.

The document compares the 2020 plan against the Previous Plan (2019 Plan), which totaled $1,264.1M across the same period. The new plan represents an increase of approximately $169.1M overall. Year-over-year differences are broken down by group: Solutions sees the largest increase at +3.9% ($14.4M in 2020, $30.2M in 2021, $34.8M in 2022), reflecting a strategic shift toward investing in educational solutions. Delivery sees a modest decrease of -2.8% overall, with a slight reduction of $4.8M in 2020 but recovery in subsequent years. Enabling Conditions shows a -0.8% net difference, with increases in 2020 and 2021 offset by a $9.3M reduction in 2022. The legacy College Ready Strategy decreases by -0.3% as it is fully phased out after 2020.

This investment plan is a core strategic planning document connecting financial resource allocation to the K-12 Education strategy's three workstreams. The emphasis on Solutions investments signals a strategic priority toward developing and scaling high-quality instructional materials and approaches. The Delivery category supports the dissemination and implementation infrastructure, while Enabling Conditions funds policy, advocacy, and ecosystem-level work. The document reflects the foundation's evolving K-12 strategy as it moves away from the College Ready framework toward a more integrated approach to improving student outcomes. It serves as a reference for portfolio managers and leadership tracking budget commitments, year-over-year changes, and strategic rebalancing across the K-12 education portfolio within the US Program.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Co-chair Presentation 01272020.pptx
This January 2020 co-chair strategy review presentation provides an overview of the Postsecondary Success (PS) strategy for the Gates Foundation U.S. Program. The document was prepared for December 2019 and presented in early 2020 as part of the annual strategy review cycle. The presentation introduces the PS team leadership, including Director Patrick Methvin and Deputy Directors overseeing Transformation, Delivery, Solutions, Strategy/Planning/Management, and Data/MLE, alongside North America Team leadership.

The strategy is organized around a value chain model with four interconnected components: Solutions (35% of budget), Institution Transformation Networks (24%), Delivery/Intermediaries for Scale (19%), and Transformational Resources (17%), with 5% allocated to Strategy, Planning & Management. Solution areas include Advising, Affordability (on hold pending Value Commission output), College Readiness (including developmental education and K-12 bridging), and Digital Learning.

Two strategy-level goals frame the work. Goal 1 ('Proof of Scale') focuses on demonstrating direct impact on 300-400 BMGF network institutions pursuing comprehensive transformation to close equity gaps, with meaningful student KPI movement expected no earlier than 2025 (Phase 3 of the strategy). Goal 2 ('Develop Public Goods') tracks the availability, awareness, adoption, and efficacy of individual public goods that contribute to broader field advancement beyond the core network.

The presentation emphasizes that the full ecosystem value chain requires approximately five years before student KPIs move at scale in an attributable way, framing this as a key consideration for co-chair engagement during the interim period. Key enablers identified include economic pressure, political context, and data/measurement/evaluation infrastructure.

By 2025, the strategy envisions: 300-400 network institutions actively leveraging supports with early student KPI movement; intermediaries with strengthened capacity and brokering quality supports; critical transformational resources widely available and adopted; and evidence-based solutions proven effective at scale. The political and policy environment (federal and state) is noted as an enabler that is difficult to fully control. This document reflects the early execution phase (Phase 1: 2019-2022) of the PS strategy and is relevant to understanding how the foundation translated strategy development into portfolio management and accountability frameworks.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Investment Plan 01272020.pdf
This document is a Financial Resource Plan for the U.S. Program's Postsecondary Success strategy, covering the planning period 2020–2023, with 2019 as a baseline reference year. It was sourced from the Decision Support Application as of December 18, 2019, and presents investment allocations broken down by Body of Work (BoW) group across six categories: Delivery, Enabling Context, Global Policy & Advocacy, Institutional Transformation, Other Body of Work Types, and Solutions.

The current investment plan (2020–2023) totals $480.3 million across the four-year period. The largest allocations go to Delivery (24.2%, $116.5M), Global Policy & Advocacy (22.5%, $108.0M), and Institutional Transformation (18.7%, $90.0M), followed by Solutions (17.1%, $81.9M), Enabling Context (12.5%, $60.0M), and Other Body of Work Types (5.0%, $24.0M). Annual totals are relatively stable: $127.3M in 2020, $115.2M in 2021, and $119.0M in both 2022 and 2023.

The document also compares the current plan against the Previous Plan (2019 Resource Plan), which had a total of approximately $484.9M across the same period (with 2019 at $123.6M and subsequent years around $123.5M, $118.9M, and $118.9M). The difference section highlights shifts in BoW group allocations: Delivery increased by 4.0%, Institutional Transformation decreased by 4.1%, Global Policy & Advocacy remained roughly flat (+0.1%), and Solutions decreased by 1.5%. Overall, the total difference is approximately $4.6M less in the new plan compared to the previous plan.

A key methodological note in the document states that the current planning cycle is the first time teams are allocating 4-Year Outlook to Bodies of Work by year, and that future planning cycles will enable more granular year-over-year comparison by Body of Work Group. This reflects an evolution in the foundation's internal planning and resource tracking capabilities.

Strategically, this document provides a high-level financial framework for how the Postsecondary Success strategy is distributing resources across different types of work. The shift toward Delivery and away from Institutional Transformation signals a potential strategic recalibration in how the foundation expects to achieve impact in postsecondary education — possibly emphasizing direct program delivery and policy influence over systems-change efforts within institutions. This plan serves as a foundational reference for understanding funding priorities and trade-offs within the U.S. Program's Postsecondary Success portfolio during the 2020–2023 strategic period.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Optional Appendix 01272020.pptx
This appendix presentation was prepared for the 2020 Strategy Review co-chair meeting and provides strategy-level measurement data for the Postsecondary Success program area. The document is dated December 2019 and covers field-level trends, institutional targeting logic, and progress tracking mechanisms for the PSS strategy.

The document opens with field-level dashboards tracking attainment, access, affordability, and completion trends in postsecondary education. Key findings highlight persistent equity gaps: attainment remains stratified for Black, Latinx, and low-income populations; enrollment of Black and low-income students has declined in recent years; affordability remains a critical barrier with low-income families spending 44% (4-year) or 31% (2-year) of income on college costs after grant aid; and while completion rates have improved in most sectors since 2008, equity gaps persist or have grown for target populations.

A central section outlines the institutional targeting framework for Strategy Goal 1—closing equity gaps in educational attainment and economic outcomes. Starting from 6,268 Title IV-eligible institutions, the strategy narrows to approximately 341 unique institutions through criteria including access orientation (admitting at least 25% of applicants), minimum enrollment thresholds with at least 25% focus population students, graduation rates between 10–70% for target populations, and minimum financial stability. These 341 institutions were identified through simulated top-25 lists from 12 newly selected intermediaries for scale (IFS) plus an existing pilot intermediary, with final selection of 4–6 intermediaries anticipated in 2022. Closing equity gaps at these institutions for one student cohort is projected to substantially reduce the number of Pell and students-of-color leavers.

Progress tracking occurs through two mechanisms: (1) Frontier Set institutional data collected via the NSC Postsecondary Data Partnership (PDP), including KPIs on credit accumulation, completion, and time-to-completion not captured by IPEDS, supplemented by Institutional Transformation Assessment (ITA) data on solution and capacity implementation; and (2) a value chain pipeline dashboard tracking the progression of service requests from solution and capacity providers through to delivery, allowing the team to identify churn points and improve efficiency. Notable examples include Delaware State's improvement in first-year credit accumulation through developmental education reforms, and an HBCU intermediary webinar reaching 21+ institutions on adaptive courseware implementation.

This document is directly relevant to USP's Postsecondary Success strategy, providing measurement infrastructure context for evaluating intermediary performance, institutional impact, and progress toward equitable postsecondary outcomes for low-income students and students of color.

These dashboards display a subset of field-level indicators that we track annually about postsecondary education, including attainment, access, affordability, and completion. 
Recent data show that attainment remains stratified with lower rates for Black, Latinx, and low-income populations.
While enrollment has increased for our target populations since 2008, the number of Black and low-income students has actually declined in more recent years, a troubling trend.
Affordability for low-income students remains a major concern with families making $30K paying the equivalent of 44% (4yr) or 31% (2yr) of their income for one year of college after grant aid (with lower incomes paying even higher shares).
And, while completion rates have increased in most sectors since 2008, equity gaps remain (or have grown) for most target populations, which also threatens progress toward more equitable college attainment.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Team Org Chart 01272020.pdf
This document is an organizational chart for the Gates Foundation's US Program - Postsecondary Success team, updated as of November 5, 2019 and filed under a January 2020 date. It provides a comprehensive view of the team's structure, reporting lines, and staff roles as of that period.

At the top of the hierarchy is Patrick Methvin, Director of Postsecondary Success. Reporting directly to him are five Deputy Directors overseeing distinct functional areas: Robin Martin (Strategy, Planning & Management / DDSPM), Jennifer Engle (Data & MLE, based in Washington D.C.), Rahim Rajan (Solutions), Archie Cubarrubia (Transformation), and Chrystie Hill (Delivery). Marissa Green serves as Sr. Program Assistant at the director level.

The Data & MLE division under Jennifer Engle includes senior and program officers focused on data, scaling MLE, solutions MLE, strategy-level measurement, workforce data, and a research analyst role. Several staff are Washington D.C.-based, reflecting geographic distribution across the team.

The Solutions division under Rahim Rajan includes program officers covering P-16 Solutions, Developmental Education (DevEd), Advising, Digital Learning, and a shared Solutions R&D role. Notable is Sr. Portfolio Officer Natasha Fedo and Sr. Program Coordinator Carolyn Swaab supporting this area.

The Transformation division under Archie Cubarrubia encompasses roles in Transformation R&D, Transformation LAB, Transformation Capacities, Transformation Frameworks, Transformation Tools, Transformation MLE, the Frontier Set initiative, and Understanding Transformation. Several positions are remote (Colorado, Illinois) or Washington D.C.-based.

The Delivery division under Chrystie Hill includes program officers for Delivery Networks, Partner Development, and Delivery Networks & Scale, with remote staff in Illinois and North Carolina.

The Strategy, Planning & Management division under Robin Martin includes roles in Strategy Integration & Execution, Strategy Execution, Team Learning, and Events & Operations project management.

The chart also details program assistant and coordinator support assignments across all divisions, identifying which senior staff each administrative role supports. Several positions are noted as interim, on leave, or temporary (AgileOne contractors), reflecting the team's staffing state in late 2019/early 2020. Two positions are listed as TBH (to be hired): Program Officer for Transformation Tools and Program Officer for System Development.

This org chart is relevant for understanding the internal capacity and organizational design of the Postsecondary Success strategy area within the Gates Foundation's US Program, reflecting its multi-pronged approach to college transformation, delivery, data, and solutions at scale.

FILE: 012521 USP Deep Dive PS Courseware Placemat.pdf
This January 2021 placemat was prepared for a USP Deep Dive session on postsecondary courseware, presenting a structured overview of design elements and associated edtech tools being evaluated by the Gates Foundation's U.S. Program. The document organizes courseware features into seven thematic categories, each paired with specific product demos, and targets both student-facing and faculty-facing audiences.

The seven design element categories are: (1) Video and Interactivity — emphasizing rich multimedia, short lecture-like videos, and active learning content, with demos from Chem101, OpenStax, Outlier, Sapling Learning, Study Hall, and Zearn; (2) Collaboration and Group Engagement — focusing on peer-to-peer connections, study group formation, and game mechanics, with demos from CircleIn, Engageli, Packback, Quantway and Statway, and Top Hat; (3) Encouraging Evidence-Based Teaching (EBT) — covering embedded professional development, action-oriented dashboards, formative activities, and real-time faculty alerts, with demos from Acrobatiq, Agile Mind, BioBeyond, Chem101, and Circles; (4) Data-Driven Insights — featuring faculty dashboards combining engagement and learning data, recommended next actions, and analytics that inspire regular instructional use, with demos from Achieve, CENTURY, LightSail, and Quantway and Statway; (5) Using and Scaling Evidence-Based Interventions — including communication tools, automated instructor nudges, student feedback mechanisms, hints and error-sensitive feedback, and affective state assessments, with demos from ALEKS, Chem101, CircleIn, Packback, Top Hat, Waymaker, and Zearn; (6) Authoring/Customization — enabling instructors and course designers to access content repositories, additional assessments, and tools to evaluate learning object effectiveness, with a demo from Waymaker; and (7) Metacognition and Self-Directed Learning — supporting self-regulation, student-facing dashboards, learning plans, and learner resilience, with demos from ALEKS, CENTURY, and Quantway and Statway.

Strategically, this document reflects the Gates Foundation's USP interest in identifying and scaling high-quality postsecondary courseware that supports student success, particularly for underserved populations in community colleges and broad-access institutions. The emphasis on evidence-based teaching, real-time data analytics, and metacognitive support aligns with broader USP goals around improving learning outcomes, reducing equity gaps, and empowering faculty with actionable insights. The diversity of tools reviewed suggests this was part of a systematic landscape assessment to inform future investments or partnerships in the edtech courseware space.

FILE: 012521 USP Deep Dive_Student Outcomes & Racial Equity_Placemat.pdf
This January 2021 USP Deep Dive placemat focuses on racial equity and student outcomes, framed around the theme of 'building relevance, motivation, and confidence.' The document is structured around six equity pillars and maps recommended actions across four domains: products and programs, pedagogy and adult mindsets, administrative practices and structures, and policy.

The six equity pillars are: (1) Get to know the students — through disaggregated data and student voice; (2) Promote adult introspection and cultural competence — including training on implicit bias and socio-cultural awareness; (3) Affirm student identity and culture — treating student backgrounds as assets rather than deficits; (4) Avoid assumptions and hold all students to high standards — using frameworks like Targeted Universalism, Liberatory Design, and Equity-Centered Design; (5) Account for adult behavior and implementation — creating tools and training to mitigate bias in learning environments; and (6) Build community and relationships — including identity-based cohorts and cross-difference collaboration.

The document includes key statistics: 68% of superintendents and 55% of principals believe culturally responsive materials are essential; 69% of ELA teachers, 53% of math teachers, and 62% of science teachers significantly modify materials for cultural relevance; and a 20% increase was observed in teachers seeking professional learning on cultural relevance.

Four case studies are profiled with outcome data mapped against the equity pillars: Kingmakers of Oakland (serving ~800 Black male students/year, achieving 3.2% graduation increase, 50% suspension rate reduction, 25% higher GPAs); Zearn (6.3 million students, showing 1.5–2.0 percentage point higher growth in high Black/Latino enrollment schools); Puente Project (300,000 students to date, 56% community college transfer rate vs. 44% statewide); and Project Basta (1,550+ first-generation graduates of color, 80% securing career-pathway employment within six months).

Definitions provided include racial equity, culturally relevant education, targeted universalism, social identity threat, and racially affirming practices. The document connects to the Gates Foundation's USP strategy by providing a framework for evaluating investments and programs through a racial equity lens, emphasizing both process equity (centering those most impacted) and outcome equity (eliminating race as a predictor of socioeconomic outcomes). It is intended as a reference tool for grantmakers assessing how education solutions address systemic barriers facing students of color.

FILE: 02192021 K-12 Education Advancing Math Outcomes in Target Geographies Presentation Deck.pptx
This February 2021 K-12 Education Learning Session presentation focuses on advancing math outcomes for priority students — specifically Black and Latino students — in target geographies. The deck was presented by Gates Foundation K-12 and Pathways team members including directors of Early Learning & Pathways, Global Policy & Advocacy: North America, and a Senior Advisor for Pathways.

The document establishes the strategic rationale for a math-focused investment approach by framing math as a cumulative, mastery-based subject where foundational gaps compound over time, with Algebra 1 serving as a critical gateway milestone. It draws on New Classrooms' 'Iceberg Problem' research to illustrate how concept deficits from 2nd through 8th grade undermine students' ability to succeed in Algebra 1 and beyond.

The presentation makes a direct case for urgency around racial equity in math achievement: Black and Latino students' average math scores are comparable to the lowest-performing OECD countries, and COVID-19 disproportionately exacerbated math learning loss in schools serving students of color — with those schools seeing math scores at only 59% of historical averages versus 77% in reading. The deck identifies multilevel drivers of the achievement gap including classroom-level bias against Black, Latino, and female students' math ability; system-level inequities such as restricted access to advanced courses (e.g., only 38% of high schools with high concentrations of Black and Latino students offered calculus in 2015-16); and societal factors including historical oppression and a $23 billion annual funding gap disadvantaging districts primarily serving students of color.

The agenda signals that the session is structured to review the strategic importance of math and Algebra 1, discuss how to leverage existing K-12 assets and explore potential strategic pivots, and establish goal-setting frameworks for a math-focused strategy in target geographies. This positions the session as an early-stage strategy development conversation, with the Foundation considering whether and how to sharpen its K-12 portfolio around math outcomes.

This presentation connects to broader USP strategy by linking K-12 math proficiency to postsecondary access and STEM career attainment — particularly for students of color who are underrepresented in high-growth, well-paying jobs. It serves as a foundational framing document for what would become the Foundation's more explicit focus on math outcomes and Algebra 1 as a milestone investment area.

FILE: 021921 USP Pathways Strategy Review Appendix A.pptx
This document is Appendix A to the February 2021 USP Pathways Strategy Review, focused on strategy monitoring and outcomes tracking. It presents the Pathways Education System framework, which is centered on an overarching system goal: ensuring that high school and postsecondary systems implement integrated, intentionally designed structures spanning grades 11–16. These structures are meant to enable delivery of credit-bearing courses and learning experiences, coupled with mentoring, postsecondary and career advising, and social skill development, all articulating to a credential of value.

The North Star for the Pathways Strategy is that Black, Latino, and young people experiencing poverty obtain credentials of value and develop the professional skills, agency, and social capital needed to thrive in the workforce. This equity-centered framing is central to the body of work (BOW) outcomes described in the document.

The appendix outlines a monitoring framework with two primary components: (1) student outcomes tracking using pathways-aligned leading indicators, and (2) execution milestones dashboards. The student outcomes section notes that indicators will be updated to align with the forthcoming USP Data Education-to-Workforce critical questions framework. Data availability is categorized as available, survey or intermittent, not available, or forthcoming. The USP Data team is identified as a key partner in increasing the collection, availability, and use of these data, disaggregated by race and income, both nationally and in Texas.

The execution milestones dashboard is intended to track critical markers toward achievement of both body of work and strategy outcomes over time, providing a tool for ongoing strategic accountability.

This document connects to broader USP strategy by operationalizing how the Pathways portfolio will measure progress, linking data infrastructure (USP Data team), equity priorities, and specific geographic focus (Texas) with system-level change goals. It reflects the Foundation's emphasis on integrated HS-to-postsecondary-to-workforce pipelines and the role of data in driving and monitoring that change.

FILE: 021921 USP Pathways Strategy Review Appendix C.pptx
This February 2021 appendix document accompanies the Education Pathways Strategy Review and provides detailed investment approach summaries across seven Bodies of Work: Integrated Advising, Accelerated Postsecondary Pathways, Pathways Intermediary Functions, Credit Mobility Platforms, Narratives and Knowledge Mobilization, Texas Pathways System Exemplar, and Policy & Advocacy. It also references a Risk and Mitigation Plan summary on slide 15.

The Integrated Advising body of work hypothesizes that three levers — demand generation through data and knowledge mobilization, R&D of scalable model components including digital enablement, and regional proof points — are needed to expand access to high-quality, identity-affirming advising for Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty.

The Accelerated Postsecondary Pathways body of work targets grade 11-14 program structures (AA by Year 13 models) that integrate dual enrollment, career pathways, and guided pathways design with digital delivery. The strategy proposes a portfolio investment approach and a learning agenda addressing program quality, regional adoption variation, funding and policy drivers, and impact for the target population — particularly Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty.

The Pathways Intermediary Functions investment focuses on organizations that broker partnerships across K-12, postsecondary, and employer ecosystems to drive systems change and increase equitable access.

The Credit Mobility Platforms body of work addresses the significant challenge that 36 million Americans have some college but no degree and that students lose an average of 43% of credits when transferring. It identifies two core barriers: misaligned institutional incentives and lack of shared credit infrastructure. The vision articulates improved outcomes for students, institutions, employers, and taxpayers through technology-enabled credit portability, with particular attention to disproportionate impacts on Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students.

The Narratives and Knowledge Mobilization body of work (partially captured) focuses on place-based narrative influence and knowledge sharing to support broader adoption of pathways approaches.

This document is central to understanding the Gates Foundation's USP Pathways investment logic during the 2021 strategy period, providing the detailed hypotheses and learning agendas that underpin the overall Pathways strategy.

FILE: 021921 USP Pathways Strategy Review Appendix D.pptx
This appendix document, prepared for the February 2021 USP Education Pathways Strategy Review, summarizes the progress and key learnings from previous pathways-related bodies of work spanning K-12, Postsecondary Success, and P-16 Special Initiatives. It serves as historical context and evidence base for the emerging integrated Pathways strategy.

The document traces the evolution of Gates Foundation pathways investments from Early College High School efforts (2002-2009), through College Ready Data work (2009-2016), to the Education to Employment Pathways initiative (2017-2020), Guided Pathways in community colleges (2013-2020), P-16 Community Investment Team (2018-2021), and USP Data connectivity work (2019-2021). This arc represents a strategic shift from school-based models toward systems- and solutions-level approaches.

Key learnings are organized around four thematic areas. First, on Quality Career Pathways, the document identifies effective regional intermediary attributes including strong K-12, postsecondary, and employer partnerships, articulated grade 11-14 programs aligned to labor market demand, and clear regional policy agendas. A critical gap noted is the lack of standard outcome measures and over-reliance on philanthropic funding for intermediary sustainability.

Second, on Occupational Identity, research found that identity formation is driven by exposure, engagement, and participation, and that Black, Latino, and low-income youth have fewer low-risk opportunities to explore career identities. Social and community networks are identified as central navigational supports. A youth pathways toolkit is being tested to address gaps in validated programmatic interventions.

Third, on Social Capital Interventions, the document highlights the absence of reliable social network measurement tools and presents emerging elements of effective program design, including network structure, peer relationship coaching, professional development for staff, and virtual delivery as a scaling mechanism.

Fourth, on Postsecondary Pathways, key learnings cover postsecondary advising solutions emphasizing cultural relevance, near-peer models, and hybrid delivery; early college coursework showing racial disparities in dual enrollment and five evidence-based equity practices; and emerging field consensus on modernized high school math course sequences aligned to postsecondary programs.

This appendix directly informs the 2021 USP Pathways strategy by documenting what prior investments established, where evidence gaps remain, and which models are ready for scaling or deeper integration into a coherent education-to-employment framework.

Early College High School as Part of Small Schools Strategy (2002-09): Created significant number of model 9-12 schools that supported students experiencing poverty to be prepared for postsecondary education and accelerate their path toward a college credential, with strong evidence of impact on postsecondary enrollment and completion.
College Ready Data (2009-2016): Supported NSC to create StudentTracker tools for high schools to access postsecondary outcomes data; invested in College Ready Indicator Systems that defined predictive indicators of postsecondary success.
Education to Employment Pathways (2017-20): Seeded innovation in postsecondary advising and transition models, expansion of dual enrollment, integrated work-based learning models, and new high school math pathways that prevent remediation in postsecondary.
Guided Pathways in Community Colleges, Dev Ed Reform, PS (2013-2020): Created two-year coherent programs of study to guide students’ courses with increasing focus on alignment with labor market demand, now adopted by 400 community colleges. Created new math pathways and corequisite models to reduce remediation.
P16 Community Investment Team (2018-2021): Support community-based intermediaries to drive collective focus on HS-PS  transition outcomes.
USP Data (2019-21): Support education to workforce data system connectivity.

FILE: 021921 USP Pathways Strategy Review Appendix E.pptx
This appendix, part of the 2021 USP Pathways Strategy Review, focuses on the foundation's Math Pathways initiative and its vision for modernizing high school mathematics aligned to postsecondary and career outcomes. The core hypothesis is that a modernized, equitable math pathway — articulated from high school through postsecondary education and into high-wage careers — will improve equity in student learning, course completion, and credential attainment. The document outlines a proposed high school course sequence: a common Integrated Math I and II sequence for all students in grades 9–10, followed by two fourth-year dual-credit options aligned to college pathways. Integrated Math I and II are described not as repackaged Geometry and Algebra II, but as technology-mediated, relevant courses incorporating data science, computing, and other modern mathematical topics.

The document surveys current field momentum as of late 2020 and early 2021, including: the Launch Years report published in March 2021 with messaging frameworks and implementation resources; Georgia's progress toward approving modernized Algebra II standards incorporating statistics; Washington's legislative funding request for Algebra II revision; California's UC system revising A-G admissions requirements to recognize data science and discrete math; and a multi-state CBMS Math Alignment Initiative involving 21 state teams. Curriculum development highlights include the Dana Center's Transition to College Math (TCM) framework, a Modern Algebra II framework, and emerging high school data science courses from Los Angeles Unified/UCLA, Stanford's Jo Boaler (piloting in Merced in 2021–22), and UC Berkeley's rapidly growing Foundations of Data Science course.

Next steps outlined include Dana Center continuing to build expert consensus in 2021, a joint K-12 and Pathways team collaboration on an integrated HS Math Pathways strategy aligned to grades 3–9 math work, and a plan to bring forward foundation role and resource recommendations by Fall 2021. The document frames this as a decade-long, multi-phase change effort with three overlapping phases: catalyzing cross-sector mobilization, developing curriculum solutions, and supporting implementation at scale. This connects to the broader USP strategy around postsecondary pathways, equitable access, and integrated K-12 to postsecondary math strategy.

A modernized mathematical pathway, well-articulated from high school to and through postsecondary education and into high-wage, high-skill, and in-demand careers, intentionally designed to dismantle institutional and systemic barriers that block equitable access and opportunities to succeed in mathematics, and containing mathematics courses with rigorous, relevant, engaging, high quality, and inclusive instruction, will improve equity in student learning, course outcomes, and attainment of credentials that promote upward social mobility.

FILE: 021921 USP Pathways Strategy Review Presentation Deck.pptx
This February 2021 presentation deck was prepared for a Co-Chair approval meeting seeking sign-off on the proposed goals and areas of focus for the USP Education Pathways functional strategy. The deck was presented by USP leadership including Directors of K-12, Postsecondary, Early Learning & Pathways, USP Policy & Communications, and USP Data, along with the North America Team and FSO administration.

The core strategic framing centers on accelerating student momentum across critical education transitions — from high school through postsecondary and into employment — with a focus on Black, Latino, and students experiencing poverty. The strategy is organized around 'Momentum Metrics,' a set of evidence-based indicators shown to predict success at the next level, spanning preparing, applying, enrolling, and career-entry stages. Key metrics include 9th grade on-track status, FAFSA completion, dual enrollment participation, college match, gateway course completion, credential attainment, and post-graduation employment and wages.

The problem statement highlights significant racial and socioeconomic disparities: dual enrollment access gaps (12% White vs. 7% Black vs. 8% Latino nationally), undermatching affecting 41% of students, low transfer rates from community colleges for lower-income students, COVID-driven enrollment declines (down 16% overall, 25% at community colleges), and persistent employer bias affecting wages and employment outcomes for Black and Latino graduates even with bachelor's degrees.

The Pathways system vision calls for integrated, intentionally designed structures spanning grades 11-14 that coordinate K-12 and postsecondary systems around aligned goals, deliver career-connected learning, and provide personalized advising and relationship supports that build social capital.

The theory of action is organized into two major workstreams: (1) Drive Innovation in Grades 11-14 Program Structures, encompassing Accelerated Postsecondary Pathways (dual enrollment/early degree models), New High School Math Pathways modernizing grades 10-12 curricula, Credit Mobility platforms to shift transfer incentives, and a Pathways Intermediary Function to validate regional coordinating entities; and (2) Mobilize Uptake of Pathways Solutions through Integrated Advising Solutions using data-driven, digitally-enabled models. Two critical enablers cut across both workstreams: Pathways Advocacy & Policy (led by the North America Team) to drive state cross-system incentives and funding alignment, and Education to Employment Data Infrastructure (led by USP Data) to build integrated data systems. The North Star Goal is that Black, Latino, and youth experiencing poverty obtain credentials of value and gain professional skills, agency, and social capital to thrive in the workplace.

FILE: 06022021 K-12 Fund Appendix.pptx
This June 2021 appendix presentation supports a K-12 Fund investment overview, detailing a coordinated set of proposed investments aimed at increasing quality in the instructional product market and improving outcomes for priority students, particularly students of color and low-income students. The overarching ambition is for supported offerings to collectively reach 15% market share, bringing access to high-quality math products to approximately 8 million students.

Key investment areas include: (1) Zearn — the Foundation is considering scaling Zearn as a high-quality, mission-aligned partner with investments spanning A/B testing ($3–4M), tutoring product development ($4.5M), commercial capabilities ($1–2M recurring), expansion to grades 6–8 and Algebra 1 ($3M), and implementation networks ($3M). Zearn serves 6.5M students, with strong diversity metrics (20% Black, 28% Latino, 58% low-income), and aspires to reach 9 out of 10 of the 40M K–8 and algebra students in the US. (2) Illustrative Mathematics and Kiddom — a potential ~$5M Social Investment Fund (SIF) minority investment in Kiddom, a hybrid LMS/digital curriculum platform that hosts OER curricula including Illustrative Mathematics. The investment would be structured through a philanthropic follow-on round with side letters ensuring charitable purpose, data rights, governance input, and reporting requirements. (3) Supplemental provider SIF investments to add Illustrative Math as core curriculum, exploring partners such as ST Math, Achieve 3000, and Amplify. A grand challenge is also proposed to incentivize culturally relevant Illustrative Math development.

Supporting activities include: funding the Council of the Great City Schools to develop standardized, scalable RFPs that aggregate demand around quality elements (including EdReports Green ratings, SEL, cultural relevance, and unfinished learning diagnostics); funding implementation networks building on the Effective Implementation Cohort; and supporting the Council of Chief State School Officers' High Quality Instructional Materials and Professional Development Network across 13 states to develop 3-year strategic procurement plans. Additional activities include continuing support for English Language Learner content, funding EdReports to evaluate and promote high-quality materials, and building R&D infrastructure for product efficacy studies.

This document connects directly to USP K-12 strategy around high-quality instructional materials adoption, standards-aligned curriculum, equitable access, and market transformation through both direct investment and demand-side coordination.

FILE: 06022021 K-12 Fund Presentation.pptx
This June 2021 presentation to Gates Foundation K-12 leadership outlines a revised investment strategy for driving market share of high-quality K-12 instructional products, with a particular focus on math. The original investment thesis proposed a private-equity-style 'buy and build' fund (~$1B) to acquire majority stakes in for-profit edtech providers and build a full-stack product offering. That approach was abandoned due to rising valuations, insufficient philanthropic capital, and legal barriers to an arms-length investment vehicle. Instead, the presentation proposes a coordinated portfolio of grants and minority-stake program-related investments (PRIs) using existing foundation tools to achieve equivalent market impact.

The strategic rationale centers on an identified market failure: absent philanthropic intervention, providers will not sufficiently invest in efficacy-focused R&D for priority students. Market trends noted include provider consolidation toward full-stack offerings, growth in OER usage with variable quality, continued importance of print for K-6, and an influx of ESSER (Elementary and Secondary School Emergency Relief) funding creating a near-term adoption window.

The proposed coordinated portfolio targets three investment tracks: (1) A $15–20M, four-year grant to Zearn to develop A/B testing infrastructure, a tutoring product addressing COVID-related learning loss, 6–8 and Algebra 1 product expansion, sales and marketing capabilities, and district implementation networks. Zearn holds EdReports 'green' ratings and over-indexes to priority student populations. (2) Approximately $20M to incentivize digitization of Illustrative Mathematics (IM) curriculum through minority SIF investments in digital platforms (Kiddom is named as an initial diligence target), supplemental providers, and a potential grand challenge. The goal is to make IM the 'DNA' of a high-quality integrated math ecosystem. (3) Approximately $10M in supporting activities including scalable RFP development to aggregate district demand, expansion of implementation networks, CCSSO state adoption strategy support, and supply-side convenings to align around a shared quality definition.

The overarching ambition is for foundation-supported products to collectively reach 15% market share of K-12 students — approximately 8 million students — in math, while pulling the broader market toward quality. This presentation is directly relevant to the USP K-12 strategy around high-quality instructional materials (HQIM), math achievement for priority students, and the use of patient capital and market-shaping tools to accelerate equitable access to rigorous curriculum.

[Slide 1]
June 2, 2021
Driving Market Share of High-Quality Products
K-12 Fund

[Slide 2]
Executive Summary | Driving Market Share of High-Quality K-12 Products
Our work to date has affirmed an unmet market need for high-quality, full-stack products
Our initial investment thesis was to pool patient capital and – replicating plays made by private equity firms like Silver Lake – “buy and build” a full product stack through majority investments in for-profit players
The investment thesis and unmet market need have been validated through conversations with investors, buyers, providers, and educators 
Absent intervention from philanthropy, we do not believe providers will sufficiently invest in efficacy-focused R&D; we continue to believe that patient capital is required to fund the development and increased distribution of products explicitly designed to improve outcomes for our priority students
Rising valuations, lack of availability of sufficient philanthropic capital (~$1B), and extensive legal hurdles to launching an arms-length investment vehicle require a shift in approach
We propose an alternative route to drive quality in the marketplace by using existing foundation tools (e.g., grants and minority stake program related investments) to make a set of coordinated investments that collectively will replicate the benefits of the fund

A coordinated portfolio of investments will increase quality at scale, improving outcomes for our priority students
Supporting 3-4 high-quality mission-aligned providers with a focus on math, each at a different stage of scale and product maturity, will provide multiple routes to develop high-quality full stack offerings. We have identified three initial opportunities for investment:
Scaling Zearn: $15-20M, 4-year grant to a high-quality, mission aligned partner. This will increase Zearn’s product quality and scale, including support for 6-8 math and Algebra product builds
Digitizing Illustrative Math: ~$20M to incent providers to digitize and scale Illustrative Math curriculum. Minority SIF investments, grants, and a grand challenge will support digital players to adopt and expand Illustrative Math OER content and improve quality 
Supporting activities: ~$10M to support adoption and implementation of high-quality materials by states and districts. Grants will develop scalable RFPs to aggregate demand, expand district implementation networks, support the Council of Chief State School Officers to develop state adoption strategies, and convene supply-slide players to align around a shared definition of quality

Our ultimate ambition is to reach 15% of K-12 students through foundation-supported products; improving both their quality and scale 
By investing in these products, our ambition is that supported offerings will collectively reach 15% share of the market - bringing access to high-quality math products to ~8M students and pushing the broader market towards quality

FILE: 08102021 K-12 Math Update Presentation.pptx
This August 2021 presentation to US Program leadership outlines a K-12 math strategy update as the Gates Foundation approaches the end of its 2018–2022 education strategy cycle and prepares for a 2022+ strategy. The document frames four key strategic considerations: (A) the strategy lifecycle — transitioning from a five-year approved strategy toward a new direction; (B) persistent racial inequities in education; (C) COVID-19 as both a challenge and accelerant for opportunity; and (D) coherence — roughly 35% of existing investments already touch math, and the Foundation sees an opportunity to create greater alignment across R&D, solutions, and delivery work.

The strategy centers on Algebra 1 as a critical milestone, noting that while course passage rates are relatively high, algebraic proficiency remains low and gaps persist across racial and ethnic groups. Students who pass Algebra 1 by 9th grade are more likely to access advanced coursework, graduate high school, and succeed in college. The Foundation commissioned a McKinsey landscape assessment (May 2021) to scan the evidence base for drivers of Algebra 1 outcomes.

Key investment focus areas include: adoption and use of high-quality instructional materials (HQIM), access to high-intensity tutoring, teacher quality and job-embedded professional development, and teacher diversity. Investment priorities are tiered based on the strength of existing efficacious interventions in the market.

The document introduces a value chain framework for math improvement that spans: research and developing breakthroughs, identifying effective solutions, making solutions available through market and network catalysis, supporting adoption by removing barriers, integrating evidence-based solutions at the local level, building enabling infrastructure, and facilitating feedback loops including beneficiary voice and human-centered design.

Geographically, the strategy prioritizes five USP focus states — California, Florida, New York, Texas, and Washington — while also pursuing national-level HQIM market presence. An ecosystem framework drawing on the UChicago 5Essentials model identifies multiple interdependent supports needed for student success, including curriculum and instruction, school culture, data-driven management, leadership, and school-community engagement. The meeting aimed to align leadership on draft strategy goals, identify potential investment opportunities, and clarify what additional information was needed ahead of formal strategy approval in 2022.

FILE: 12022021 K-12 Assessment Presentation.pptx
This December 2021 presentation provides a comprehensive assessment of the K-12 education assessment and accountability landscape, framed around persistent racial and ethnic achievement gaps in math and reading (per NAEP and PISA data), and evaluates the current federal accountability system under ESSA alongside a future state vision for improvement.

The current state analysis identifies the federal accountability system's core assumption — that increased transparency through standardized assessments will drive local actors to improve — and critiques its limitations. Despite some gains in graduation rates, racial disparities remain stagnant, and recent NAEP scores show historic declines for 13-year-olds. The presentation notes a significant shift away from the federal government's more activist school improvement stance prior to 2015, highlighting ESSA's rigid assessment requirements alongside limited support for interventions.

Using Texas and Dallas ISD as illustrative case studies, the presentation maps the layered assessment ecosystem that districts navigate — from state summative assessments (STAAR) to interim tools (NWEA), English proficiency measures (TELPAS), SEL surveys (Panorama), and college readiness assessments (SAT, AP). This illustrates how districts layer locally-driven assessments on top of state requirements to meet instructional and predictive needs.

The future state vision centers on giving local actors — students, teachers, principals, and district leaders — access to a more comprehensive view of student, school, and system performance. This vision aligns with the emerging US Program Early Childhood to Workforce Data Framework, which organizes indicators across four domains: student outcomes and milestones (academic proficiency, SEL, on-track composites, attainment, earnings), school and district conditions (climate, curriculum quality, resource levels), out-of-school conditions (housing, health, internet access), and student characteristics for disaggregation (race/ethnicity, income, disability status).

The presentation also references a Gates Foundation-commissioned 2020 landscape scan of 'bright spot' districts — those previously identified as low-performing under NCLB that demonstrated positive academic shifts for Black, Latino, and low-income students — and notes that outperforming districts incorporate indicators beyond federal and state requirements in their accountability systems. This connects to USP strategy around data infrastructure, equity-centered accountability, and supporting local improvement actors.

THE CURRENT FEDERAL ACCOUNTABILITY SYSTEM HAS SEEKS TO REDUCE RACIAL ACHIEVEMENT GAPS, RELYING HEAVILY ON DATA TRANSPARENCY AS A LEVER
KEY ASSUMPTION Increased transparency around student performance, as measured by standardized assessments, will drive local actors to improve the system
FEDERAL INPUTS
Accountability regulations and guidance to states to hold districts and schools accountable. 
Federal resources (Title 1 funds) for states to deploy to schools in need of improvement.
FEDERAL ACTIVITIES
Offer flexibility but maintain compliance through reviews of state plans outlining accountability system structure. 
Support interventions minimally, at state and district levels.
Rigid assessment requirements (i.e., peer review requirement)
KEY OUTPUTS
State accountability systems identifying schools in need of additional resources and improvement support.
High levels of public transparency through disaggregated data and tools (e.g., school, district, and state report cards).
DESIRED OUTCOMES
Improve achievement and equitable outcomes through effective resource allocation and interventions.
ACTUAL OUTCOMES
Racial disparities are stagnant despite some improvement in overall high school graduation rates and academic performance.
Recent NAEP scores show historic declines for 13-year-old students.
MOVING FORWARD Accountability measures provide significant value to those experiencing the system firsthand (i.e., students, caregivers, educators) as these stakeholders seek to drive improvement

FILE: K-12 Education 03042021 .pdf
This document is a transmittal email dated February 8, 2021, sent by Kara Greif (Officer, Strategy, Planning & Management, K-12 Education) to the Strategy Submissions, Co-chair Pre-reads, and Strategy distribution lists, with copies to key Gates Foundation U.S. Program staff including Allan Golston, Henry Hipps, Robert Nabors, Bob Hughes, Carina Wong, and others. The email serves as the formal submission package for the K-12 Education strategy review scheduled for March 4, 2021 (03042021). The strategy team lead identified is Bob Hughes (bob.hughes@gatesfoundation.org), with Kara Greif designated as the notetaker for the review session. The submission package includes a comprehensive set of documents: a Table of Contents, Acronym List, Strategy Review Memo, Co-Chair Presentation Deck, Investment Narrative, Investment Plan, Investment Scoring Top 5/Bottom 5, Investment Scoring Summary, Team Org Chart, 2020 Meeting Follow-ups and Action Items, Optional Appendix List, and Optional Appendix A. This email represents a standard Gates Foundation strategy review cycle submission for the K-12 Education program area. The breadth of attached materials — spanning narrative strategy documents, investment scoring analyses, organizational structure, and prior meeting action items — indicates this is a full strategy review rather than a routine check-in. The inclusion of co-chair pre-reads and the involvement of senior foundation leadership (co-chairs, program presidents) signals this is a high-level strategic decision-making session. The K-12 Education strategy at this time was focused on improving educational outcomes for students of color and students from low-income backgrounds through investments in high-quality instructional materials, effective teaching, and education system transformation. This submission package would have informed co-chair deliberations on investment priorities, strategic direction, and portfolio management for the K-12 program area going forward into the 2021 planning cycle.

FILE: K-12 Education Co-Chair Presentation 03042021.pptx
This March 2021 co-chair presentation provides an overview of the Gates Foundation K-12 Education strategy for the 2021 Strategy Review cycle. The document introduces the K-12 leadership team including Director Bob Hughes and several Deputy Directors overseeing portfolios spanning Charters, Solutions, Networks for School Improvement, and Cross-Cutting work.

The K-12 strategy is structured around four portfolios: R&D of New Solutions (20% of 4-year spend), Existing & Emerging Solutions (32%), Delivery (29%), and Enabling Conditions (19%). All portfolios are oriented toward improving outcomes for Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty, with the ultimate goal of increasing the number of students who earn a diploma, enroll in postsecondary education, and are on-track in their first year to obtain a credential with labor market value.

Current R&D priorities include Middle Years Math (including tutoring) and Secondary Writing. Delivery priorities focus on Networks for School Improvement and Students with Disabilities. Solutions priorities encompass Coherent Instructional Systems, Social Emotional Learning, Teacher Preparation, and Market Dynamics.

The strategy is assessed as slightly off track due to COVID-19 disruptions. The presentation highlights that 15–16 million public school students face a digital divide, projecting 7–14 months of learning loss for disconnected students and an additional 232,000 high school dropouts, disproportionately affecting Black, Latino, Native American, and rural students. In response, the foundation is focusing on high-quality resources for learning loss recovery, digital instruction, and SEL supports.

A notable deep dive explores the foundation's approach to solution efficacy evidence, categorized into three types: Direct Efficacy (causal evidence linking specific products to student outcomes), Indirect Efficacy (correlational evidence across contexts), and Input Evidence of Efficacy (synthesis of research pointing to product features likely to improve outcomes). Examples cited include Mathematica's Efficacy & Enactment Study, Algebra 1 Grand Challenge evaluations, and Networks for School Improvement summative evaluations by Mathematica, RAND, and AIR. This framework reflects the foundation's recognition that it is cost-prohibitive to fund causal studies for every available solution, requiring a diversified evidence strategy. The Coherent Instructional Systems portfolio is flagged as a focus for discussion with a specific impact performance rating referenced.

We fund four policy areas: instructional supports (high standards, accountability), data, finance, and supporting strong student pathways.
Data, Measurement & Evaluation
We invest in the infrastructure and evidence needed to ensure we and the field learn from our work in order to improve student outcomes
