FILE: K-12 Education Investment Plan 03042021.pdf
This document is the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation US Program K-12 Education Investment Plan for fiscal years 2021–2024, presented as a financial resource plan in millions of dollars. It compares the current 2021–24 plan against the previous 2019 Plan across four investment groups: Solutions, Delivery, Enabling Conditions, and Global Policy & Advocacy.

Under the current 2021–24 plan, total investments over the four-year period are projected at $1,261.9 million. Solutions is the largest investment category at $663.4M (52.6% of total), followed by Delivery at $364.7M (28.9%), Enabling Conditions at $121.8M (9.6%), and Global Policy & Advocacy at $112.0M (8.9%). Annual spending is set to decline from $335.2M in 2021 to $296.4M in 2024, reflecting a deliberate tapering of investment over the plan period.

Compared to the 2019 Plan, the 2021–24 plan reflects a net reduction of $79.4M (approximately -4.8% of the prior total of $1,341.3M). The most significant shifts are: Solutions increases by $92.1M (+13.4%), reflecting a strategic reorientation toward scaling and developing high-quality instructional materials and approaches. Delivery decreases by $142.5M (-22.5%), suggesting a pullback from direct service or implementation support investments. Enabling Conditions decreases by $6.0M (-6.8%), and Global Policy & Advocacy decreases by $23.0M (-16.6%), indicating reduced emphasis on advocacy and systemic policy levers relative to the prior plan.

This investment plan reflects the K-12 strategy's evolving priorities within the US Program, with a stronger concentration of resources in Solutions — likely aligned with the foundation's focus on high-quality instructional materials (HQIM), curriculum adoption, and evidence-based academic interventions. The reduction in Delivery and Global Policy & Advocacy spending may signal a strategic shift away from broader systemic reform efforts and toward more targeted, scalable academic solutions.

The document is a high-level financial summary without narrative justification, serving primarily as a planning and accountability tool for Gates Foundation leadership. It provides a clear view of how K-12 Education resources are being reallocated across investment categories over a four-year horizon, and is relevant for staff tracking USP resource allocation, portfolio strategy decisions, and the evolution of the K-12 theory of change from the 2019 to 2021 planning cycles.

FILE: K-12 Education Investment Scoring Summary 03042021.pdf
This document is the Gates Foundation U.S. Program K-12 Education 2021 Strategy Review Investment Scoring Summary, covering all active and recently closed investments of $5M or more as of early 2021. It provides investment IDs, approved amounts, allocation amounts, impact and execution performance ratings, investment owners, funding models, and start/end dates across three major strategic pillars: Solutions, Delivery, and Enabling Conditions.

Under Solutions, the document covers R&D of New Solutions (Middle Years Math) including investments in New Schools Fund/AERDF ($25M), New Classrooms Innovation Partners ($12M and $9M), and Existing & Emerging Solutions under Coherent Instructional Systems including Khan Academy ($12M and $10.5M), Mathematica ($9.2M), EdReports ($7M), Student Achievement Partners ($7M), New Profit capacity building ($6.1M), Teacher Preparation via Texas Tech/US PREP Cohorts II and III, and Market Dynamics investments in New Profit, Owl Ventures, and Reach funds.

Under Delivery, the largest cluster is Networks for School Improvement (NSI), with over 25 active investments in intermediary organizations running improvement networks across cities including New York City, Baltimore, Denver, Dallas, Chicago, and California. These investments range from roughly $6M to $15M and target 9th-grade on-track and college-ready outcomes for Black, Latino, and low-income students using continuous improvement methodologies. Cohort 3 NSI grants (approved September 2020) for organizations including Fund for Educational Excellence, High Tech High, New Visions, Teaching Matters, and CORE Districts are noted as too early to evaluate. High-Quality Charters investments include Charter School Growth Fund ($27.5M), CSGF Facility Fund III ($10M), City Fund Charter Seat Investment ($11.4M), and Summit Public Schools Special Education work ($5.6M).

Under Enabling Conditions, the document covers the Teacher2Teacher network (Anne Lewis Strategies, $6.3M) and College Ready Legacy grants from the prior strategy period, including investments in Summit Public Schools, Tennessee Department of Education, Texas Tech/US PREP Cohort I, Kentucky Department of Education, Tennessee SCORE, New Profit Personalized Learning Collaborative, and University of Kentucky Assessment for Learning Project — most of which are closed or winding down with no reinvestment planned.

Family Interest Grants managed by the K-12 team include Harvard University's Proving Ground ($20M) and Columbia University's technology-enhanced tutoring project ($7.9M).

A recurring theme across the 2020 scoring cycle is the significant disruption caused by COVID-19, which impacted student data collection, delayed network launches, suspended standardized testing, and forced virtual pivots. Many NSI grants received 'Not Meeting Impact Targets' ratings in 2020 due to COVID rather than fundamental model failures. The document reflects a portfolio in transition — with legacy College Ready grants closing and a new cohort of NSI and math-focused investments ramping up — and provides a comprehensive performance baseline for the foundation's K-12 strategy review.

FILE: K-12 Education Investment Scoring Top 5 Bottom 5 03042021.pdf
This document presents the K-12 Education 2021 Strategy Review investment scoring for the top five and bottom five performing investments, providing detailed performance rationales, key results, and reinvestment prospects for each. All investments use a Managed and Funded model.

Top Performing Investments: (1) New Visions for Public Schools (INV-008746, $13.99M) operates a network of 43 NYC high schools focused on 9th grade on-track outcomes for Black and Latino students; execution exceeds expectations though impact targets were not fully met in 2020 due to mixed results across student subgroups. (2) Branch Alliance for Educator Diversity/BranchED (INV-007663, $3.81M) improved teacher preparation at minority-serving institutions, meeting 85% of quality goals and achieving enrollment and completer gains; rated as meeting impact targets and exceeding execution expectations with reinvestment expected. (3) SAGA Innovations (INV-006717, $1.82M) delivered technology-informed, high-dosage tutoring in Chicago and NYC for grades 5-8, reducing per-student cost from $3,100 to $1,800 and showing 0.26 SD improvement in math test scores; exceeds impact targets. (4) Partnership for Los Angeles Schools (INV-002223, $1.0M) supported professional learning for high-quality instructional materials adoption, enabling LAUSD to adopt EdReports green curricula reaching 600,000 students; exceeds both impact and execution expectations with reinvestment confirmed. (5) Chiefs for Change (INV-019225, $1.6M) coordinated COVID-19 school reopening plans with McKinsey and national partners, producing 25+ resources distributed to 500 CCSSO members; exceeded impact and execution expectations.

Bottom Performing Investments: (1) Eskolta School Research and Design (INV-003664, $14.99M) supports a network of high schools in NYC and Boston using evidence-based interventions; rated not meeting impact targets or execution expectations in 2020 due to COVID disruptions, slow Boston start, and leadership transition. (2) Crossroads Education (INV-006712, $748K) attempted a near-peer tutoring model for middle school math in Indiana but never achieved adequate utilization or intensity of use; will not reinvest. (3) New Teacher Center (INV-001917, $1.0M) failed to coordinate effectively with Charlotte-Mecklenburg school district in its Professional Learning Partnership, resulting in low stakeholder satisfaction and service dosage; will not reinvest. (4) Florida Department of Education (INV-007935, $2.04M) aimed to support district-charter compacts in Duval and Miami-Dade counties; Duval efforts collapsed due to staff turnover, and Miami-Dade progress was slow; will not reinvest. (5) Education Analytics (INV-000978, $33K) assessed GovSpend database for tracking K-12 district spending but found the data unreliable; not meeting impact targets though execution met expectations; will not reinvest, but learnings are informing a new 'Center on Market Information' initiative.

This document reflects USP K-12's commitment to rigorous performance management, racial equity in student outcomes, and evidence-based portfolio decisions including early exit from underperforming grants and reinvestment in high-performing partners.

FILE: K-12 Education Strategy Review Mar 2021 – Digital Binder FINAL.pdf
This March 2021 co-chair meeting digital binder presents the Gates Foundation K-12 Education strategy review, covering progress, lessons learned, 2021 priorities, and investment decisions across the portfolio. The strategy targets Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty, aiming to increase diploma attainment, postsecondary enrollment, and on-track credential completion.

The strategy is organized across four portfolios: R&D of New Solutions (20% of spend), Existing and Emerging Solutions (32%), Delivery via Networks for School Improvement (29%), and Enabling Conditions (19%). Overall strategy progress is rated slightly off track, primarily due to COVID-19 disrupting school operations and compromising student outcome data.

Key progress highlights include: Saga Education's high-dosage tutoring model showing strong impact (0.26 SD gain in math test scores, 0.37 SD on grades) and cost reduction from $3,100 to $1,800 per student; the launch of the Algebra 1 Grand Challenge with 416 applicants (55% led by people of color); and expansion of the Advanced Education R&D Fund (AERDF) toward a $250M five-year fund with commitments from Gates, Chan Zuckerberg Initiative, and Walton. AERDF is adding new programs in assessments for variable learners and early literacy.

Coherent Instructional Systems work accelerated digital curriculum adoption during COVID. EdReports green-rated materials expanded, with 31% of math materials meeting alignment criteria. Mathematica was commissioned to study comparative efficacy of 4-5 middle school math curricula. Adoption reached 19% and 29% of sampled large districts for secondary math and ELA respectively. An Effective Implementation Cohort was launched across 20 districts.

Teacher preparation targets 50% of new teachers in CA and TX hired from high-quality programs by 2030. Texas is ahead of schedule; California is making early progress through residency grant programs.

Networks for School Improvement (NSI): 21 networks across 23 states serving 500+ schools and 150,000 students. COVID severely disrupted data collection; the RCT was delayed from 2022 to 2024. Despite disruptions, convergence is emerging around instructional materials, early warning systems, and postsecondary access supports.

Investment reallocation of $217.7M over four years shifted resources from NSI (reduced by $126M in unallocated reserves) and Students with Disabilities toward Algebra 1 data and R&D ($60.5M), a curricula investment fund ($50M), and a new Assessments body of work ($13.5M).

A major co-chair discussion topic is Assessments and Accountability, exploring how COVID-era testing waivers create an opportunity to redesign student progress measurement systems. The foundation is considering values around purpose of assessment, level of system focus, coalition-building beyond traditional partners, and decoupling innovation from current accountability frameworks.

Top-performing investments include New Visions for Public Schools NSI, BranchED teacher preparation coaching, SAGA Innovations tutoring, and Partnership for Los Angeles Schools professional learning. Bottom performers include Eskolta NSI, Crossroads Education near-peer tutoring, New Teacher Center professional learning, and Florida Department of Education charter compact.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Co-chair Presentation 03042021.pptx
This March 2021 co-chair meeting presentation provides a comprehensive strategy review of the Gates Foundation's Postsecondary Success portfolio. The presentation was delivered to senior USP leadership including the USP President, Directors, and Deputy Directors across the Postsecondary Success team.

The strategy overview describes a four-pillar theory of change: Solutions (advising, college readiness including developmental education and K-12 bridging, and digital learning), Transformation (institution-focused transformation networks), Delivery (intermediaries for scale), and Enabling Context (transformational resources). The 2021-2024 budget allocations across these pillars were 22%, 17%, 27%, and 31% respectively, with 3% allocated to SPM functions.

A major section of the presentation addresses the dual impacts of COVID-19 and the racial justice reckoning on higher education. Key data points include: a 3.6% overall undergraduate enrollment decline, a 13.1% unprecedented drop in freshman enrollment, a 10.1% decline at community colleges, and a 21.7% fall in immediate post-high school enrollment. Financial strain is evident with 650,000 fewer higher education workers by end of 2020 and projected 5-10% revenue declines in 2021. The pandemic disproportionately affected Native American, Black, rural, and low-income students, and severely stressed HBCUs serving approximately 300,000 students with limited digital infrastructure.

In response, the team executed significant strategic pivots and resource reallocations within the approved 2021-2024 baseline, supplemented by $82M from the USP Strategic Opportunities fund and $20M from the USP Racial Equity Initiative — a net portfolio increase of $46.6M (10%). Key shifts include: nearly doubling the Solutions budget with an expanded Develop Courseware body of work ($62.4M) and a new Digital Learning Infrastructure body of work ($27.5M); adding two new Transformation bodies of work — Shape Growth of National Scale Enterprises ($22.5M) and Campus Climate Tools & Resources ($7M); expanding the Equitable Value Reform Movement ($8.6M); and reducing the Delivery budget by $15.6M and PS Policy, Advocacy & Communications by $12.7M.

The presentation underscores a strategic shift toward supply-side innovation, technology-enabled solutions, and explicit racial equity commitments, while maintaining the overall postsecondary theory of change. It connects directly to USP goals around closing opportunity gaps for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Investment Plan 03042021.pdf
This document is a Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation Investment Plan for the Postsecondary Success strategy within the U.S. Program division, sourced from the Decision Support Application as of January 8, 2021. It presents a side-by-side comparison of two financial resource plans: the Previous Financial Resource Plan (2020–2023) and the Current Plan (2021 Resource Plan), spanning fiscal years 2020 through 2024.

The Previous Financial Resource Plan totaled $478.9M across five years (2020–2024), with the largest allocations going to Delivery (24.3%, $116.5M), Global Policy & Advocacy (22.6%, $108.0M), and Institutional Transformation (19.2%, $92.0M). Solutions accounted for 17.1% ($81.9M), Enabling Context 12.5% ($60.0M), and Other Body of Work Types 4.3% ($20.5M).

The Current Plan (2021 Resource Plan) reflects a significantly increased total investment of $518.4M, representing a meaningful strategic reallocation. The most notable shift is in the Solutions category, which more than doubled in proportional weight to 26.7% ($138.2M), making it the largest single investment category in the new plan. This signals a strategic pivot toward scaling and deploying proven solutions. Delivery decreased slightly to 22.2% ($115.2M), while Global Policy & Advocacy dropped to 18.4% ($95.3M) and Institutional Transformation to 17.3% ($89.9M). Enabling Context increased to 12.8% ($66.6M).

The Difference section quantifies year-over-year changes between the two plans. The most significant positive variance is in Solutions, where the current plan adds $28.1M in 2021, $21.5M in 2022, and $13.0M in 2023 compared to the previous plan. Total differences across all categories are $22.9M (2021), $18.6M (2022), and $6.2M (2023), reflecting a front-loaded increase in overall investment.

This investment plan is a key strategic planning artifact for the Postsecondary Success team, illustrating how the foundation is recalibrating its portfolio emphasis from earlier-stage work (institutional transformation, policy advocacy) toward solutions-focused investments. The document is useful for understanding budget trajectories, inter-category trade-offs, and the evolving theory of change within Postsecondary Success. It provides foundational context for grant-making decisions, portfolio reviews, and stakeholder discussions about resource deployment in the postsecondary education space.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Investment Scoring Summary 03042021.pdf
This document is a $5M+ Investment Summary Report for the Gates Foundation's U.S. Program Postsecondary Success strategy, created January 22, 2021. It provides performance scoring and narrative summaries for 22 investments totaling over $150 million across multiple strategic bodies of work.

The report covers investments organized under six strategy areas: Delivery (developing and aligning intermediaries for scale), Enabling Context (building a national postsecondary data ecosystem), Global Policy & Advocacy (communications and pathways policy), Institutional Transformation (developing pathways frameworks and understanding transformation), Solutions (advising, developmental education, and digital learning), and a PS Reserve category.

Key investments include: Jobs for the Future's Student Success Center Network ($19.8M), supporting guided pathways at scale with 92 sites; Rockefeller Philanthropy Advisors' Frontier Set Technical Assistance ($7.5M), serving as the Service Design and Delivery ecosystem funding mechanism; National Student Clearinghouse Research Center's Postsecondary Data Partnership in two phases ($7.8M and $6.8M), expanding to ~390 institutions; The Aspen Institute's community college IP sites ($14.4M); American Institutes for Research's Frontier Set evaluation ($10.8M); NASPA's advising backbone investments ($10.4M and $8.8M); and WICHE's digital learning solution network investments ($13.3M and $8M).

Performance ratings use a green/yellow/red system across Impact Performance, Execution Performance, and Reinvestment Prospects. High performers include AACC Pathways Institutes, APLU IP Sites, AASCU IP Sites, Aspen Institute community colleges, and Tyton Partners Solutions Market Measurement. Investments facing challenges include VentureWell's Frontier Set network administration (not meeting impact targets in 2020), WICHE's expanding interstate data systems (will not reinvest), and the AIR Frontier Set Evaluation (partially meets targets, will not reinvest).

A cross-cutting theme is the Frontier Set initiative, a network of 31 high-performing and high-potential institutions supported by multiple intermediaries (Aspen, AASCU, APLU, UIA) to generate insights on institutional transformation. The initiative was set to sunset at end of 2021. The Service Design and Delivery (SD&D) ecosystem, funded through the Rockefeller Philanthropy Advisors investment, connects demand partners (institutional intermediaries) with supply partners (solutions and capacities) toward the common goal of institutional transformation.

Equity is a consistent theme across investments, with multiple grants tracking Black-White, Hispanic-White, and Pell-to-non-Pell gaps. COVID-19 response adaptations are noted across numerous investments, with many partners pivoting to virtual services and developing pandemic-specific resources. The document reflects a strategic inflection point as the Frontier Set initiative nears completion and the foundation considers next-phase investments focused on intermediaries for scale, data infrastructure sustainability, and solution network maturation.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Investment Scoring Top 5-Bottom 5 03042021.pdf
This confidential Gates Foundation document, dated January 22, 2021, presents the top and bottom performing investments within the Postsecondary Success strategy of the U.S. Program. It evaluates ten investments (plus one purchase order) across impact performance, execution performance, and reinvestment prospects for the 2020 scoring year.

Top performing investments include: (1) INV-000896 with the Institute for Higher Education Policy ($3.5M), supporting the Postsecondary Value Commission to define college value and develop an equitable value framework and interactive data tool; (2) INV-001567 with Education Commission of the States ($2M), pooled funding for Strong Start to Finish developmental education reform, involving collaboration with Ascendium and Kresge Foundation; (3) INV-016699 with Western Interstate Commission for Higher Education ($1.29M), a COVID-19 response investment accelerating the Every Learner Everywhere Digital Learning Network, which produced 78+ new assets and served thousands of institutions; (4) INV-002399 with University of Southern California ($300K), supporting USC's Center for Urban Education to operationalize equity principles across Solution Networks for Black, LatinX, Indigenous, and low-income students; and (5) INV-001505 with Excelencia in Education ($500K), leveraging the Seal of Excelencia to drive institutional transformation for Latinx student success.

Bottom performing investments include: (1) INV-006528 with VentureWell ($6.6M), managing the Frontier Set network, rated as not meeting impact targets and slightly off track on execution, with the foundation deciding not to reinvest; (2) INV-009893 with Ithaka Harbors ($2.5M), developing the ALiS adaptive statistics courseware, which failed to close achievement gaps and produced important lessons about curriculum design and equity; (3) INV-020743 with Asteroidea Ventures LLC ($918K), the Scaling Partners Network, which encountered a data security breach and contract violation leading to a no-reinvest decision; (4) INV-001543 with ReD Associates ($680K), ethnographic research on college administrators delayed by COVID and complicated by an internal equity scandal; and (5) purchase order 53288 with InterWorks ($30K) for Tableau licenses that went unused due to pandemic disruptions and low engagement.

The document reflects the Postsecondary Success strategy's focus on equity, developmental education reform, institutional transformation, and the scaling of evidence-based solutions. It highlights the foundation's active portfolio management approach, including amended grant scopes, close oversight of underperformers, and strategic pivots in response to COVID-19. Lessons learned from underperforming investments inform future grantmaking around curriculum quality, equity integration, and partner accountability.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Optional Appendix A 03042021.pptx
This appendix slide deck accompanies a March 4, 2021 Postsecondary Success strategy session and focuses on digital learning updates across three areas: COVID-19 digital learning responses, courseware development, and digital learning infrastructure.

On COVID-19 response, the document highlights accelerated technical assistance through Every Learner Everywhere, including a Faculty Playbook with 8,000+ downloads and 17 sessions at the ASU Remote Virtual Teaching conference reaching 45,000 registrants. A Quality Access Program was developed to help faculty make informed digital learning (DL) adoption decisions, with 22 courseware products from 21 providers in beta. A Digital Learning Policy Task Force was launched, with New America championing broadband relief for Pell recipients resulting in a $50 monthly subsidy passed in December 2020 COVID funding. Longitudinal surveys by Tyton Partners and Digital Promise tracked faculty and student sentiment toward online learning.

On courseware, the document reports a $4.5M investment in OpenStax to expand OER content for top 20 gateway courses, supplemented by $8M from Hewlett and Koch. OpenStax now offers 42 titles used across 60%+ of U.S. institutions with 14 million users. A refreshed courseware R&D strategy targets: (1) developing and scaling high-quality courseware, (2) supporting adjunct faculty in adopting evidence-based teaching practices (EBTs), and (3) exploring digital learning infrastructure as an emerging body of work. An earlier multi-year adaptive courseware investment at 8 large public research universities yielded adoption in 211 courses enrolling 288,000 students, a 13% increase in course pass rates, and an estimated $16.6M in tuition savings. APLU is pursuing deeper disaggregation of outcomes by race and Pell status.

2021 investments include launching the first wave of courseware development with partners for exemplar, development, and upgrade fund courses; a beta release of the Quality Access Program discovery tool; a field scan of EBTs with courseware; and renewal of the WICHE solution network.

On digital learning infrastructure, an SPO search is nearly complete. Conversations with UNCF and HBCUs are ongoing, with a co-funder partnership being actively constructed. Wave 1 HBCU selections and an initial services/technology bundle with aligned providers and edtech partners are planned for 2021. This document connects to USP's postsecondary strategy around equitable access to high-quality learning tools, with particular attention to gateway course success, affordability, and HBCU engagement.

[Slide 1]
COVID-19 Digital Learning Responses
Accelerated COVID19 related Every Learner Everywhere TA for aligned institutions in the areas of online course design, remote instruction, and courseware adoption and implementation. Produced 78 new field assets including Faculty Playbook (8K+ unique downloads). Developed and delivered 17 sessions at July 2020 ASU Remote Virtual Teaching conference (45K registrants and 26K unique attendees, participation was free and all content still available and streaming)
Development of Quality Access Program to enable faculty and course coordinators to make better, more informed DL adoption decisions (21 unique providers supplying 22 courseware products included in beta launch)
Launch and support a national Digital Learning Policy Task Force to provide recommendations to federal, state, and institutional policymakers to ensure equitable and affordable access to high-quality digital learning. New America championed broadband relief for Pell recipients ($50 monthly subsidy) as part of the recent Covid19 funding passed by Congress in December 2020
Launched three-phase longitudinal survey to monitor and measure faculty and institution response to COVID-19 (Tyton Partners) and a companion National Student Survey to measure student sentiment towards online learning (Digital Promise). This included a broader sampling of 3-4K faculty per survey and a longitudinal study with 852 faculty members. The survey reports experienced 2K downloads and were featured at major conferences (EDUCAUSE, OLC Accelerate, ASU Remote Summit) and publications like Inside Higher Ed, Chronicle of Higher Education, Campus Technology, and Diverse: Issues in Higher Education.
Open Stax Investment and Funder Collaborative 
$4.5M investment to OpenStax to complete their build out of OER content for Top 20 General Education gateway courses with additional $8M in aligned support from funding partners (Hewlett, Koch). OpenStax now offers 42 titles; its titles are used in more than 60% of US colleges and universities, with more than 14 million users.
Refresh of Develop Courseware BoW strategy 
A more comprehensive courseware R&D strategy to (1) develop and scale high-quality courseware ensuring that high-quality courseware (2) enables faculty (largely adjuncts) to shift their instructional practice and adopt evidence-based teaching practices (EBTs); and (3) exploration of the rapidly changing digital learning (DL) infrastructure space, which we see as a separate, complementary, emerging body of work. 
Conclusion of Accelerating Adoption of Adaptive Courseware grant
A multi-year investment to scale adaptive courseware in gateway courses at 8 large public 4-year research universities: adoption in 211 unique courses that enrolled 288,000 students; 13% increase in course pass rates and an estimated $16.6M in student tuition savings for not having to repeat gateway courses – APLU is pursuing deeper analysis of outcomes data by race and Pell status.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Optional Appendix B 03042021.pptx
This appendix slide, dated March 4, 2021, summarizes key lessons learned from the Gates Foundation's Guided Pathways initiative as part of the Postsecondary Success strategy. Guided Pathways is described as an integrating framework for whole-campus institutional transformation, packaged for intermediaries to scale to broader adoption. Nearly 400 community colleges are actively implementing the approach.

Three major lessons are highlighted. First, on transformation at scale: it is possible to make measurable progress on whole-campus redesign and student KPIs when institutions are supported by intermediaries. Scale of Adoption Assessment (SOAA) scores show maturation of implementation, and early student outcome KPIs show both absolute growth and narrowing of equity gaps across racial and ethnic groups.

Second, on cost of implementation: Guided Pathways typically requires six years to fully implement, including case-making, capacity-building, and staging. For a community college with 4,000 full-time students, total implementation cost is estimated at $7.1 million over four years ($1.8 million/year or $450 per FTE), with annual operating costs of $1.4 million ($350 per FTE). Advising — including new advisors and advising technology systems — is the costliest component. The reforms are predicted to produce net savings for students, with value exceeding a typical $400 tuition and fee increase. Outcome-based funding formulas in some states may offset a portion of institutional costs.

Third, on equitable student success: students from different backgrounds are not equally distributed across academic programs, with stratification along racial, ethnic, and socioeconomic lines. Disaggregated KPI review by administrators, faculty, and staff across all Guided Pathways pillars is essential to identify and address inequities. Completion of early milestones — such as completing 12+ college credits in the first year — approximately doubles the likelihood of transfer and bachelor's degree completion overall, with disproportionate benefits for Black students (2–3x), Latino students (4–5x), and low-income students (7–9x).

This document connects directly to the Foundation's Postsecondary and Pathways strategy areas, providing evidence-based justification for continued investment in Guided Pathways intermediaries and solutions as a scalable, equity-focused approach to community college transformation.

[Slide 1]
B - Guided Pathways: key lessons about transformation in 2020
Recap: Guided Pathways is an integrating framework for institutional transformation that we have packaged for intermediaries to scale to broader adoption. The evidence and lessons below reflect elements of success stemming from multiple portfolios (solutions, transformational resources and intermediaries)
To date, nearly 400 community colleges are actively implementing Guided Pathways. We continue to learn about its efficacy and impacts as a multi-year, whole-campus, integrated solution approach to transformation
Key Lesson: Transformation at Scale
It is possible to make progress on whole-campus redesign implementation and student KPIs for a cohort of institutions supported by an intermediary
We can observe changes to colleges’ Scale of Adoption Assessment (SOAA) scores, indicating that efforts to implement Guided Pathways have matured (and intermediaries can support this scale)
Early student outcome KPIs (right) indicate both absolute growth and equity gap narrowing 
Key Lesson: Cost of Implementation
Analyzing the cost of implementing institutional transformation is critical so leaders understand the commitment and resources necessary and make decisions around priorities, tradeoffs, and revenues and expenses accordingly.
Guided Pathways implementation typically takes 6 years, inclusive of case-making, capacity-building, and staging.
The total cost to implement Guided Pathways at a community college with 4,000 full-time students is estimated at $7.1 million over four years ($1.8 million per year) or $450 per FTE, and the annual cost to operate Guided Pathways for that same college is estimated at $1.4 million per year, or $350 per FTE. 
Advising is the costliest element of Guided Pathways implementation and operation, driven primarily by investments in people and technology (i.e., new advisors and new advising systems).
Guided Pathways reforms are predicted to result in net savings for students, with a value exceeding a typical $400 tuition and fee increase to fund a portion of implementation. 
We assume a tuition increase to cover institutional costs, but in some states, outcome-based funding formulas will offset a portion (but not all) of this investment with increase revenue
Key Lesson: Practical Implications on Equitable Student Success
Understanding where on a student’s journey inequity occurs is critical so leaders know where to focus limited resources.
Students from different backgrounds are not equally distributed across academic program enrollments and completions; they are instead stratified along racial/ethnic and socioeconomic lines.
In addition to organizing coherent academic sequences (a key activity in Guided Pathways implementation), routine review of disaggregated KPI data for each pillar (e.g., students’ selection of academic program) by administrators, faculty, and staff is critical to begin addressing racial and socioeconomic equity gaps on campus.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Optional Appendix C 03042021.pptx
This document is an appendix presentation from March 4, 2021, focused on the Postsecondary Success strategy's Public Goods portfolio. The Gates Foundation defines Postsecondary Public Goods as discrete, high-value-add items — including frameworks, tools, datasets, solutions, intermediaries, and networks — that the Foundation helps create and disseminate to the field. The intended beneficiaries of these public goods include higher education institutions, solutions providers, and network partners, with the overarching goal of enabling, supporting, and accelerating institutional transformation to close equity gaps in educational attainment and economic outcomes.

The presentation outlines how the Foundation's three Postsecondary solution areas each produce multiple public goods that work in concert to inform markets, shape demand, and support implementation. The document provides a subset of examples tied to these three solution areas and highlights four additional 'first tier' public goods, offering progress updates on each.

In strategic context, this appendix reflects the Postsecondary Success team's commitment to a public goods approach as a lever for systems-level change — moving beyond individual institutional grants toward shared resources and infrastructure that can scale impact across the higher education landscape. By investing in and disseminating frameworks, data tools, and intermediary capacity, the Foundation aims to create durable, field-wide improvements rather than one-off institutional gains.

This document is relevant to Gates Foundation staff working on Postsecondary Success strategy, particularly those focused on public goods development, market-shaping, and field-building activities. It connects to broader USP strategy by demonstrating how curated public investments can drive equity-focused systemic change at scale across U.S. higher education.

[Slide 1]
C - Public Goods Progress update
We define PS Public Goods as discrete, high value add items (frameworks, tools, datasets, solutions, intermediaries, networks) that we can help create and share with the field (higher education institutions, solutions providers, network partners) that can enable, support, and accelerate institutional transformation in order to close equity gaps in educational attainment and economic outcomes.
Our three solution areas produce multiple different public goods that work together to inform markets, shape demand and support implementation (we have provided a subset of examples related to our three solution areas). We currently have four additional first tier public goods and have provided progress updates for each.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Optional Appendix E 03042021.pptx
This appendix slide from the Postsecondary Success (PS) team documents a range of COVID-19 response investments made by or in collaboration with the Gates Foundation's Postsecondary Success strategy team, as of March 2021. It serves as a supplementary reference cataloging specific grantee activities and outcomes across four major response areas.

First, the PS team helped improve deployment of approximately $6 billion in CARES Act Emergency Aid. Key partners included: the Hope Center, which surveyed over 220,000 students and 155 colleges to build an evidence base; Edquity, a digital emergency aid distributor that onboarded 30 new institutional customers in seven months with a more affordable and tailored product; NASFAA, which engaged directly with the U.S. Department of Education to shape program guidance and hosted webinars with over 28,000 live attendees per session; and the Center for American Progress, whose congressional modeling helped shape funding formulas to better weight support for public institutions serving more low-income and part-time students.

Second, the team leveraged its existing Digital Learning (DL) network to provide quality online teaching supports. This included faculty professional development and course redesign technical assistance delivered to 14 institutions from the Frontier Set and Intermediaries for Scale cohorts, production of 78 field assets (notably a COVID-19 online instruction guide with ~8,000 downloads), and co-hosting 17 of 45 sessions at a BMGF-ASU REMOTE Virtual Teaching Conference (45,000 registrants). Partners Tyton Partners, Digital Promise, and New America conducted accompanying longitudinal surveys and policy work.

Third, investments supported transfer policy and practice reform. Co-funded with Ascendium, the Aspen Institute College Excellence Program researched how to improve credit mobility and transfer applicability for students—especially Black, Latinx, Indigenous, and low-income students disproportionately impacted by COVID-19—with policy and practice recommendations slated for Q1 release.

Fourth, the team planned and tested national-scale enterprise roles in pandemic response through grants to ASU, WGU, and SNHU for digital learning institutional and student support services, particularly for Minority-Serving Institutions (MSIs). The Sorenson Impact Center's MAPS project extended forecasting efforts to account for COVID impacts, reaching thousands through data dashboards and publications.

Finally, in collaboration with Global Health and North America teams, the Foundation provided $15 million to up to 10 HBCUs to expand COVID-19 diagnostic testing capacity, leveraging external partnerships with Microsoft, BD, SteriPack, and Operation Warp Speed. This appendix reflects the PS team's adaptive, equity-focused response to the pandemic and its disproportionate effects on underserved student populations.

[Slide 1]
E - PS Team’s Covid-19 Response Investments
Helped improve deployment of $6B in CARES Emergency Aid dollars through investments in:
The Hope Center continues to build field's evidence base with survey of over 220,000 students and 155 colleges.
Edquity (digital emergency aid distributor) advanced equitable provision of emergency aid by accelerating their product uptake, offered at a more affordable price with more tailored institutional account supports, with 30 new institutional customers in 7 months. 
National Association of Student Financial Aid Administrators (NAFSAA) has been working with the U.S. Department of Education as they developed the new programs terms and conditions. NASFAA staff regularly teed up live questions/recommendations to ED staff that led to changes to problematic guidance as the program was rolling out and informed regular communications and well-attended live webinars (28K+ live attendees per session) intended to inform their membership.
The Center for American Progress (CAP) provided modeling that the U.S. Congress and advocates used to understand the impact of different proposed formulas for institutions of higher education. This modeling support resulted in funding provisions that targeted more support to mostly public institutions of higher education based on their higher costs for transitioning to a new learning modality when compared with online-only institutions, as well as the greater weights for the number of lower-income and part-time students they serve. 


Leveraged our existing DL network to provide quality online teaching supports:
Designed and delivered faculty professional development and course redesign technical assistance to 14 institutions from the Frontier Set and Intermediaries for Scale cohorts 
Produced 78 new field assets including Delivering High Quality Instruction Online in Response to COVID-19 (~8,000 downloads)
Developed and delivered 17 of 45 sessions at the July BMGF – ASU REMOTE Virtual Teaching conference on delivering high quality remote instruction (45K registrants)
The grant also supported a longitudinal survey to monitor and measure faculty and institution response to COVID-19 (Tyton Partners) and a companion National Student Survey to measure student sentiment toward online learning (Digital Promise); also, with New America, a national Digital Learning Policy Task Force
Supported an existing partner to put forth policy and programmatic guidance to address longstanding transfer issues exacerbated by COVID-19: 
Co-funded with Ascendium, the Aspen Institute College Excellence Program conducted research and interviews with field leaders to assess how to urgently increase mobility and applicability of credits toward a degree for students transferring during COVID, including the disproportionate number of Black, Latinx, Indigenous, and low-income students impacted. Recommendations for institutional practice and state policy will be released in Q1.

The breadth of attached materials — including appendices and a summary — suggests a comprehensive strategy review rather than a routine update, likely involving decisions about grantmaking priorities, partnership strategies, or portfolio direction related to math education. This document is useful for tracing the timeline and organizational process of the February 2021 math strategy review within the K-12 Education team.

FILE: 03032022 Q1 Innovation Session USP Deep Dive - Memo and Appendics.pdf
This March 2022 pre-read document was prepared by Intentional Futures for the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation's Q1 Innovation Session, focusing on the U.S. Program's emerging K-12 math instructional materials strategy. The document is organized around four market dynamics and a proposed Product Quality Framework to guide foundation investments.

The document articulates an emerging USP math strategy anchored in the development, adoption, and enactment of high-quality, coherent, integrated instructional materials. It emphasizes that standards-aligned curricula alone are insufficient — products must translate into teacher-led, technology-aligned learning experiences that build conceptual knowledge, motivation, and persistence in math, particularly for Black, Latino, and low-income students.

Four key market dynamics are analyzed: (1) COVID-19's lasting impact on unfinished learning, accelerated edtech adoption, and ESSER funding availability ($190B federal relief); (2) the rapid growth of supplemental materials, which are projected to exceed core curriculum spending and lack shared quality standards; (3) the dominant role of teacher choice in instructional material selection, with teachers frequently modifying or replacing district-recommended materials due to inadequacy; and (4) growing demand for coherent, platform-integrated solutions across assessment, curriculum, LMS, and professional learning.

The Product Quality Framework is introduced as the foundation's working definition of high-quality math products, encompassing ten principles: appropriateness of challenge, differentiation, engagement, motivation, cultural relevance, accessibility, usability, teacher professional learning, interoperability, and data privacy. The framework is organized by student and teacher experience criteria across categories including content, facilitated learning, intervention, independent practice, assessment, and professional community.

Illustrative Mathematics (IM) is positioned as the centerpiece of the foundation's market strategy. Founded in 2011 and used in 49 states, IM offers a K-12 standards-aligned curriculum rooted in research-based practices including the Five Practices for Orchestrating Productive Mathematical Discussions. IM's partnership model — including IM Certified partners (Kendall Hunt, Imagine Learning, McGraw Hill) and ecosystem partners (Amplify, Zearn) — is seen as a scalable mechanism for moving the broader market toward quality. The foundation sees IM's open-licensing model (CC BY) as both an asset and a risk, with revenue sustainability and quality control challenges requiring strategic attention.

Other key products discussed include Zearn (K-5 core curriculum, walled-garden model, used in over half of U.S. school districts) and Eureka Math/Eureka Math2 (preK-12, Great Minds, transitioning to paid model). The document argues that walled-garden investments alone are insufficient for market-wide impact and that diversified investment across business models is essential.

RAND American Educator Panel data is extensively cited, showing persistent gaps between what teachers value in materials and what they receive, widespread material modification, inadequate professional learning, and inequitable classroom conditions — especially for students of color and those experiencing poverty. These findings underscore the urgency of the foundation's market-shaping strategy.

FILE: 03032022 Q1 Innovation Session USP Deep Dive - Presentation.pdf
This March 2022 confidential presentation prepared by Intentional Futures provides a comprehensive deep dive into the K-12 instructional materials market with a focus on math curriculum quality, teacher preferences, and supplemental products. The document was prepared to inform Gates Foundation USP (U.S. Program) strategy around high-quality instructional materials (HQIM).

The presentation analyzes teacher and district leader behavior in curriculum selection, noting that quality ratings (e.g., EdReports green ratings) are among the lowest priorities when choosing curriculum, while student engagement and standards alignment rank higher. Teachers are characterized as final arbiters of instructional content, with roughly two-thirds modifying, cobbling, or DIY-ing their curriculum rather than following a single published curriculum.

The supplemental market analysis segments instructional supplements into four categories: Augment (Khan Academy, BrainPOP), Practice (IXL, iReady), Intervene (System 44, Achieve), and Facilitate (Kahoot, Grammarly). Key market trends include growing educator prioritization of student engagement and academic acceleration, teacher autonomy in materials selection, the rise of digital supplements, and consolidation toward comprehensive solutions. Supplement share of the K-12 instructional materials market grew from 25% in 2010 to 57% by 2020. Khan Academy dominates MS and HS math supplement market share. Most top supplements lack alignment to green-rated core curricula, and roughly half have no efficacy research.

A Product Quality Framework is introduced covering student-facing dimensions (Content & Textbook, Facilitated Learning, Intervention, Independent Practice, Feedback, Summative Assessment, Math Identity & Community) and teacher-facing dimensions (including Formative Assessment, Class Community, Just-in-time Support, and Professional Community), with both student criteria and tech criteria for each dimension.

The presentation reviews key vendors—Great Minds (Eureka Math/EM2), Zearn, Illustrative Mathematics (IM), Kendall Hunt, Imagine Learning, and Amplify—with profiles on their financials, grades served, usage, and strategic positioning.

The 'Horses in the Race' section identifies strategic bets: Curriculum Associates (stable capital, studying priority student populations), Great Minds (quality product, innovation opportunity), Zearn (growth, teacher satisfaction, CIS value, A/B testing potential), and Illustrative Mathematics + Partners (landscape play, OER/open licensing, incentive infrastructure).

A deep dive on Illustrative Mathematics explores IM's potential as a content spine for the broader ecosystem. IM's CC-BY licensed curriculum is widely used, and its open licensing model positions it as a platform for other developers. Challenges include capital gaps for quality-first providers, insufficient efficacy data, and licensing complexity. IM's current model relies on semi-exclusive agreements with Kendall Hunt, Imagine Learning, and McGraw Hill. The recommended investment approach is a multi-year grant to support widespread dissemination and ongoing improvement of IM's content through its own and derivative products.

FILE: 03032022 Q1 Innovation Session USP Deep Dive - Session Placemat.pdf
This confidential placemat from the Q1 2022 Innovation Session presents a Product Quality Framework evaluating six math curriculum and edtech products across both student and teacher experience dimensions. The session was framed as a 'USP Deep Dive,' situating it within the U.S. Program's strategy around high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) in K-12 mathematics.

The framework evaluates six products — Amplify Math, Eureka Math Squared, Illustrative Mathematics, Imagine Math, Kendall Hunt, and Zearn — across seven experience categories: Content & Textbook, Facilitated Learning, Intervention, Independent Practice, Feedback, Summative Assessment, and Math Identity & Community. Each category is assessed against both student-facing criteria (pedagogical outcomes) and tech criteria (platform capabilities).

For students, the framework prioritizes: engagement with standards-aligned and culturally relevant content; active math identity development; appropriate intervention above and below grade level; differentiated independent practice; timely and actionable feedback; holistic summative assessments; and peer/teacher discourse that builds conceptual understanding.

For teachers, the framework adds three additional dimensions: Formative Assessment (using data to adjust instruction), Class Community & Learning Environment (inclusive and differentiated environments), Just-in-time Support & Ongoing Professional Learning (coaching and misconception warnings), and Professional Community (peer collaboration among teachers).

Product ratings — visualized as filled, half-filled, or empty circles — were produced by Intentional Futures and noted as approximations subject to refinement. Across the student experience rubric, Amplify Math and Imagine Math score consistently high across both student and tech criteria. Zearn also rates highly on student criteria. Illustrative Mathematics scores strongly on student criteria but has limited tech platform ratings. Eureka Math Squared and Kendall Hunt show more mixed or partial scores, particularly on tech criteria.

The teacher experience rubric reveals similar patterns: Amplify Math and Imagine Math rate strongly across most dimensions including formative assessment and intervention. Eureka Math Squared performs well on teacher criteria but less so on tech. Illustrative Mathematics and Kendall Hunt show gaps in tech platform capabilities. Zearn is moderate across teacher dimensions.

This placemat connects to the USP strategy of identifying and supporting high-quality math instructional products that serve historically underserved students. The dual student/teacher lens reflects the program's understanding that product quality must support both direct learner outcomes and educator capacity. The inclusion of math identity and culturally relevant content as explicit evaluation criteria signals alignment with equity-centered design principles central to USP's K-12 math strategy.

FILE: 062222 Postsecondary Success Strategy Update Appendix.pptx
This appendix document accompanies the June 2022 Postsecondary Success Strategy Update presented to Gates Foundation leadership. It provides supporting data, metrics, and exemplar case studies across three core solution areas within the Postsecondary Success strategy: Digital Learning, Developmental Education (DevEd) Reform, and Advising.

The appendix opens with historical sector-wide graduation rate data disaggregated by race and ethnicity, establishing the persistent equity gaps the strategy aims to close. It then highlights exemplar institutions — Georgia State University (GSU) and Sinclair Community College — that significantly exceeded national graduation rate growth, with Sinclair achieving a +15.8 percentage point gain since 2010 placing it at the 75th percentile among 2-year public institutions.

On Digital Learning, the document outlines investments in courseware and evidence-based teaching practices targeting the top 20 gateway courses, which have historically failed Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students, with failure rates of 50-60% at community colleges. Performance metrics (Availability, Awareness, Adoption, Efficacy) are reported using a 'Body of Work' (BoW) framework. Key findings include that availability of quality courseware remains below expectations (0 of top 20 courses meet highest standards), though investments in introductory statistics and chemistry are on track. Efficacy data from SRI (2019) shows small-to-moderate learning outcome effects (+0.09 to +0.47) with stronger effects for underrepresented minority students. An exemplar from Indian River State College demonstrates improved final exam scores through gateway course redesign using ALEKS courseware.

On Developmental Education Reform, the document addresses inequitable placement of Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students into remedial courses that consume financial aid without leading to gateway course completion. The strategy invests in placement reform, acceleration, and math pathway alignment. Efficacy evidence from Florida's statewide reform (251,000+ students) shows gateway math completion increased 6.81 ppt for Black students and 6.44 ppt for Hispanic students; gateway English increased 14.78 ppt and 11.02 ppt respectively. Cuyamaca College is highlighted as an exemplar implementing five tailored math pathways with multi-measure placement.

The appendix supports the broader PSS theory of change by grounding strategy investments in outcome data and institutional evidence, particularly emphasizing racial equity in completion outcomes as the central organizing principle.

FILE: 062222 Postsecondary Success Strategy Update Presentation Deck.pptx
This June 2022 presentation deck provides a strategy update on the Postsecondary Success team's work, presented to senior Gates Foundation leadership including the CEO, President of US Program, and Directors across multiple program areas. The deck covers the team's working definition of institutional transformation — defined as the realignment of an institution's structures, culture, and business model to produce dramatic and equitable increases in student outcomes — and the rationale for pursuing an Integrated Solutions Approach rather than isolated point solutions. Key strategic framing includes the argument that integrated transformation 'hard-wires' connections across the student experience, reduces initiative fatigue, and produces more sustainable outcomes than piecemeal interventions. The deck summarizes lessons learned about the transformation process, including that implementation typically takes four to six years and costs approximately $450 per student per year (roughly $7.1 million over four years, using Guided Pathways as a proxy). It identifies minimum elements required for transformation: an integrated institutional framework, committed leadership and financial readiness, strategic data use, membership in peer learning networks, and supportive policy and data enablers. The team's Proof of Scale strategy is introduced as the mechanism to operationalize these lessons at broader scale through three interconnected components: Solutions (advising, college readiness including developmental education and K-12 bridging, and digital learning), Transformational Resources (tools and methods to standardize and accelerate the transformation process), and Delivery/Intermediaries (trusted scaling partners that support institutions through the change process and broker connections to solutions expertise). The document emphasizes equity throughout, focusing on closing opportunity gaps for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students. Institutional exemplars from Completion by Design, Frontier Set, and institutions like Georgia State University and Middle Tennessee State University are referenced. The presentation reflects the team's effort to package and replicate transformation models and reduce implementation variation through targeted capacity building in leadership, data, and finance.

Our Working Definition 
Transformation is realignment of an institution’s structures, culture, and business model to create a student experience that results in dramatic and equitable increases in outcomes and educational value.
In our conversation with you about institutional transformation in June 2020, we shared why we are pursuing an Integrated Solutions Approach to Transformation:

It Produces Better and More Sustainable Outcomes than Implementing individual solutions
Student success initiatives that are implemented in the context of broader institutional transformation (versus point solutions that seek to fix a particular problem) “hard-wire” connections across the student experience while helping staff avoid “initiative fatigue.”
It’s More Packageable and Replicable than Comprehensive Transformation
GSU’s transformation, while impressive, is hard to replicate and scale. But we are learning from institutions in Completion by Design and Frontier Set and we’re unpacking their transformational journeys and identifying approaches that can be packaged and replicated.

FILE: 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix B Classroom Vision.pdf
This document is Appendix B of the Gates Foundation's K-12 Education Strategy Approval package, presenting a comprehensive vision and landscape analysis for improving math education in U.S. classrooms, with a particular focus on Black, Latino, and low-income students (referred to as 'priority students'). The document is organized around four interconnected levels: Students, Classroom (Teachers and Instructional Resources), School Community, and Ecosystem.

The Vision for the Math Classroom articulates what an ideal math learning environment looks like, emphasizing relevant and rigorous instruction, personalized supports, diverse and experienced teachers, digital tools, sustained and coherent instruction, and supportive school environments where students have agency and engagement.

The Students section provides a grade-by-grade analysis (3rd through 9th grade) of key math standards and documents significant equity gaps. Data shows that Black and Latino students are systematically underrepresented in advanced math courses such as Algebra I. Math confidence is low among 4th and 8th graders nationally, and girls face disproportionate math anxiety starting in elementary school. Student math identity — shaped by classroom environments, recognition, and access to advanced coursework — is highlighted as a critical factor in long-term achievement.

The Classroom: Teachers section articulates a vision for diverse, qualified, and supported teachers. Key findings show that priority students disproportionately lack access to qualified teachers, especially in high-minority urban schools. The teacher workforce remains predominantly white, and teachers of color are 24% more likely to leave the field annually. Professional learning is insufficient, with 22.8% of teachers receiving zero hours of PD focused on implementing instructional materials.

The Classroom: Instructional Resources section documents the gap between teacher demand for engaging, culturally relevant, standards-aligned materials and what is actually available. While availability of aligned materials has grown, quality indicators remain weak. 75% of middle and high school math teachers want culturally relevant materials but only 27% find their current materials adequate for that purpose. Supplemental materials — largely teacher-created or from platforms like Teachers Pay Teachers — dominate actual use.

The School Community section emphasizes the need for shared leadership vision, data-informed improvement, and supportive school culture. Data access and use vary widely; many teachers lack preparation to interpret student data meaningfully. Only one-third of students regard their school culture favorably, and SEL implementation lags despite high perceived importance among principals and teachers.

Overall, this appendix provides the evidentiary foundation for the K-12 strategy's focus on high-quality instructional materials (HQIM), teacher support and diversity, and equity-centered school community practices as interconnected levers to improve math outcomes for priority students.

The inclusion of interim leadership in key roles (R&D, Solutions) and multiple open senior advisor slots signals that the team was in a transitional phase aligned with the strategy refresh underway in 2022.

FILE: 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Presentation.pptx
This September 2022 presentation was delivered to Gates Foundation US Program co-chairs and CEO to seek formal approval for the K-12 Education team's updated math strategy. The presentation represents the culmination of over a year of strategy development beginning with co-chair alignment in early 2021, through team reorganization, hiring of math expertise, and strategy refinement through August 2022.

The strategy is anchored in a vision that math should not be a barrier to students' success in college and beyond. It targets priority students — specifically Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty — who faced significant instructional gaps even before COVID-19, which further exacerbated disparities. The strategy focuses on grades K-9, with Algebra 1 mastery by end of 9th grade identified as a critical milestone linked to access to advanced courses, high school graduation, and postsecondary success.

The strategy is organized around three core investment areas over a four-year period (2023–2026) with a total budget of approximately $1.135 billion: (1) Instructional Content ($424M) — improving quality and adoption of digital instructional materials to increase student outcomes, engagement, and motivation; (2) Teachers and Teaching ($144M) — improving pre-service and in-service training tied to high-quality instructional materials; and (3) Coherent Systems ($275M) — validating the impact of integrating quality materials and teaching supports at the district level. An additional $62M is reserved to transform and reimagine math education through rethinking pedagogy around digital technology. Cross-cutting investments include Critical Enablers ($135M) and Policy and Advocacy ($95M) supporting state coalitions, measurement and evaluation, and data infrastructure.

Geographically, the strategy focuses on California, Florida, New York, and Texas as focus states, with district-level demonstration sites as proof-of-concept environments. Key milestones include increased availability and adoption of high-quality digital instructional content, diverse and prepared teachers, 6–10 district partnerships per focus state implementing coherent instruction, and strong state partnerships and networks.

The presentation reflects the K-12 Education team's comparative advantage in building on existing progress in instructional content quality, teacher support systems, and state-level coalition building. It connects to the broader US Program strategy by targeting systemic improvements in math instruction for historically underserved students as a foundation for postsecondary and economic success.

FILE: 09152022_WSI Strategy Approval _ Appendix B_ Stakeholder Engagement Update and Ongoing Engagement Plan.pptx
This presentation serves as Appendix B to the Washington State Initiative (WSI) Strategy Approval package, providing an update on stakeholder engagement conducted during the strategy development process and outlining plans for ongoing engagement. The document was produced in 2022 as part of the WSI strategy approval cycle, with stakeholder engagement contracted to Education First.

The engagement process was guided by five equity principles: Representation, Transparency, Accessibility, Responsiveness, and Reciprocity. These principles were designed to center voices closest to the populations and issues addressed in WSI's strategy. Over 200 stakeholders were engaged across multiple phases, including interviews, focus groups, and three learning trips with 100+ external stakeholders. Selection criteria prioritized geographic diversity across Washington State and representation of focus populations including Black, Latino, Indigenous, low-income, and rural communities, as well as stakeholders ranging from strong allies to those with reservations about the foundation's approach.

Key strategy decisions shaped by stakeholder input include: (1) ensuring BIPOC-led organizations are at decision-making tables and can access investment opportunities; (2) building trust through broader listening before inviting deeper collaboration with regional partners; (3) defining educational pathways to reflect diverse student and family experiences rather than narrow attainment metrics; (4) investing in partners' data capacities; and (5) creating new structures for ongoing engagement—in person, virtually, and asynchronously.

Stakeholder feedback on strategy goals consistently emphasized that educational success must be holistic, encompassing mental health, cultural identity, sense of belonging, and career opportunity—not just postsecondary attainment rates. Quotes from stakeholders across Puget Sound, Kitsap, Western Washington, Spokane, and Yakima regions reflect deep concern about the mental health crisis among youth and a desire for culturally relevant, place-based, and community-driven solutions.

Feedback on trust and relationships highlighted community skepticism of the Gates Foundation's historical top-down approach, urging the foundation to listen first, trust local expertise, and avoid prescribing solutions. In response, WSI committed to broadening its range of relationships statewide and prioritizing regional expertise in solution development.

A follow-up communication plan was noted: in July 2022, all engaged stakeholders received a summary of how their input shaped strategy decisions, with a final cumulative accounting planned for October 2022. This document connects directly to WSI's broader strategy refresh by demonstrating the community-informed foundation for its investment and partnership decisions in Washington State.

[Slide 1]
RECAP: Stakeholder Engagement: Overview
GOAL: Gather stakeholder insights to center voices closest to WSI priority issues and populations in the strategy development process and to inform critical strategy decisions –  through authentic, transparent, and respectful engagement that lays the groundwork for building and strengthening longer-term, ongoing stakeholder relationships
Guiding Equity Principles
Representation: Identify and elevate the voices of stakeholders most proximate to the education inequities addressed in WSI's strategy. Maintain an asset-based lens and prioritize diversity of geography, identity, and experiences in selecting stakeholders.
Transparency: Engage in stakeholder communication before, during, and after their input is shared. Provide an honest accounting of all facts essential to ensuring an informed and equitable process, including acknowledgement of historical context
Accessibility: Enable the full and equitable participation of all stakeholders by supporting informational, digital, physical, financial, linguistic, and cultural access during the engagement process
Responsiveness: Tailor and flex the stakeholder engagement process to meet the needs of stakeholders in real time and allow stakeholders to inform both content and process, continuously evaluating the approach in response to stakeholder feedback
Reciprocity: Ensure the engagement process provides mutual benefit to stakeholders and the foundation. This means working with stakeholders to design engagement processes that support their economic, social and cultural wellbeing, acknowledge power imbalances and historical patterns of harm, and address their priorities

FILE: 09152022_WSI Strategy Approval _ Appendix C _ Regional Partnerships.pptx
This appendix to the 2022 WSI (Washington Student Initiative) Strategy Approval package details the foundation's approach to identifying, selecting, and investing in regional partnerships across Washington State to support high school-to-postsecondary (HS-to-PS) transitions, particularly for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and students experiencing poverty.

The document presents a capacity analysis of regional partnerships organized into a maturity typology with three archetypes: early-stage, developing, and mature. This analysis informed the design of a two-phase RFP process intended to build broad partnership capacity before narrowing to 3-5 high-impact regional proof points.

Phase 1, called the 'Catalytic Cohort,' targets approximately 10-15 partners and focuses on technical assistance and capacity building toward threshold competencies defined by the Ecosystem Partnership Framework (EPF). Minimum criteria are intentionally broad to ensure wide applicant reach and include a focus on postsecondary enrollment, experience serving BIPOC students and students in poverty, interest in data-driven continuous learning, and a partnership structure that includes a school district plus an intermediary or institution of higher education (IHE).

Phase 2 targets 3-5 regional proof points for deeper, 4-year investments deploying WSI's regional levers — partnership capacity, data, and solution adoption — to drive measurable postsecondary enrollment outcomes aligned with the WSI Theory of Change and Theory of Action. Learnings from these proof points are expected to inform future scale strategies.

Guiding principles emphasize fairness and equity in the RFP process, broadening the portfolio to include nascent and BIPOC-led partnerships, and maintaining transparency with applicants about expectations and resource requirements.

A high-level RFP timeline is outlined, with Phase 1 beginning in 2023 and Phase 2 spanning 2024–2028. Phase 1 partners will be invited into a Community of Practice (CoP) to sustain the broader partnership network even after proof point selection.

The approach draws on cross-USP learnings from K-12 NSI, Postsecondary IFS, and Pathways AccelerateEd RFP processes, as well as the Washington Student Achievement Council's Regional Challenge Grant process. The document notes ongoing attention to Indigenous and rural community contexts, with plans to revisit framework applicability for those populations. This appendix connects directly to WSI's broader strategy of driving regional postsecondary credential attainment outcomes in Washington State.

FILE: 09152022_WSI Strategy Approval _ Appendix D _ Lessons from WA and Other States.pptx
This appendix presentation supports the WSI (Washington State Initiative) Strategy Approval package and draws on lessons from other states to inform Washington's postsecondary strategy. The document presents four case studies examining successful state-level approaches to improving postsecondary access and completion.

The first two slides present pathway scaling case studies: one examining how a regional intermediary in Texas (The Commit Partnership) leveraged local proof points to champion state policy and funding changes, and another examining Tennessee's top-down policymaking approach driven by external actors that shaped higher education initiatives.

A central focus of the document is FAFSA completion mandates. The presentation details how Louisiana became the first state in 2017-18 to require FAFSA completion as a high school graduation condition, achieving the highest national FAFSA completion rate (over 75%). Seven additional states have since passed similar mandates. The document notes that states with mandates have seen dramatic increases in FAFSA completions (22-26% increases in the first cycle). For WSI, the document recommends supporting Washington to amend legislation to require FAFSA completion for high school graduation, paired with additional implementation supports, while flagging risks including late filing and guidance counselor burden.

Tennessee's FAFSA strategy is examined as an embedded, systemic approach. Tennessee's Drive to 55 initiative—aimed at 55% postsecondary credential attainment by 2025—integrates FAFSA completion requirements through the Tennessee Promise scholarship (free community college). The state's February 1 scholarship deadline incentivizes early FAFSA filing and features school-based FAFSA Frenzy events. For WSI, the implication is that FAFSA strategy must be cohesive and tied to multiple aligned efforts; the Foundation plans to leverage the Education Round Table to align key actors.

The Texas Student Success Council (TSSC) case study illustrates effective sector coordination, bringing together education, business, philanthropic, and nonprofit leaders to advocate for policy changes improving postsecondary pathways. Convened by Educate Texas as a neutral platform, TSSC won legislative support for four major policy areas in its first cycle, including outcomes-based funding for community colleges. Key success strategies include engaging influencers across the education continuum, identifying a small set of achievable goals, and building a broad coalition with diverse stakeholders.

Collectively, these lessons inform WSI's approach to state policy advocacy, sector alignment, and postsecondary access strategies in Washington State.

FILE: 09152022_WSI Strategy Approval _ Appendix E _Hypothetical Investment Scenario in Pierce County .pptx
This presentation appendix presents a hypothetical investment scenario for Pierce County, Washington, used to illustrate how the Gates Foundation's Washington State Initiative (WSI) regional strategy would be applied in a concrete geography. The document is structured across three slides covering current state, problem analysis, and proposed regional lever application.

The core challenge identified is that postsecondary enrollment (PSE) rates in Pierce County have stagnated despite improvements in high school graduation rates, particularly in Tacoma. Quotes from nonprofit leaders highlight that graduation alone is insufficient if students are not prepared for postsecondary success, and that non-Tacoma districts—including those with high low-income populations like Franklin Pierce School District—are often overlooked in regional partnership efforts.

Two major intermediaries are identified as coordinating cross-sector work: the Foundation for Tacoma Students (FFTS), which previously drove HS graduation improvements and is now pivoting to PSE, and the Puget Sound College & Career Network, which focuses on broader Pierce County PSE enrollment but is still building capacity. Greater county-wide coordination is identified as critical to avoiding duplication, scaling bright spots, and supporting underserved districts outside Tacoma.

Key drivers of enrollment challenges include summer melt (lack of HS-to-PS transition support), inadequate advising around college applications and financial aid, and inequitable access to college and career services—with some students receiving overlapping services while others are entirely underserved.

Data analysis shows PSE rates in Pierce County are inversely correlated with district poverty levels. While dual enrollment completion is relatively strong compared to statewide averages, FAFSA completion rates are only average, indicating multiple momentum indicators must be addressed simultaneously. Pierce County represents approximately 12-13% of Black, Latino, and Indigenous students and low-income students statewide.

The proposed investment approach frames Tacoma/Pierce County as an 'Archetype A' regional partner—the most mature and developed archetype in Washington—using a two-phase RFP process. The strategy involves leveraging USP (U.S. Program) assets alongside Washington local assets such as PSESD, FFTS, and National Student Clearinghouse data to build regional capacity and strengthen the high school-to-postsecondary transition for historically underrepresented students.
