FILE: 09152022_WSI Strategy Approval _ Appendix H _ Execution planning.pptx
This appendix to the WSI Strategy Approval package focuses on execution planning, specifically the proposed operational model for coordinating Washington State Initiative (WSI) team activities with other USP program and functional strategy teams (PSTs/FSTs) operating in Washington State. The document establishes guiding principles and a tiered engagement framework — distinguishing between 'Strategic/Committed' and 'Opportunistic' collaboration types — to ensure that no USP team initiates work in Washington State without WSI Team support, and that collective investments represent highest and best use of USP resources.

The strategic/committed tier involves aligned Bodies of Work (BOWs) and shared outcomes, with clear delineation of who leads on funding, staffing, and local coordination. WSI staff are responsible for state context, local stakeholder relations, and internal coordination, while PST/FST staff provide technical programmatic expertise. The opportunistic tier allows for selective, time-limited engagement when mutual agreement exists between teams.

A central case study illustrates the WSI–USP Data collaboration in Washington State, focused on strengthening the WA P20W (Preschool-to-Grade 20-to-Workforce) Data Ecosystem. Key outcomes include building an equity-driven coalition with unified political will for data reform, improving data access and use at the regional level, and modernizing the Education Resource Data Center (ERDC). The collaboration is characterized as 'Strategic and Mutually Driven,' with a co-developed BOW aligned to both USP Data frameworks and WSI strategy. Funding for 2023–2026 totals $13.7M (WSI contributing $12.2M, USP Data $1.5M), with .9 FTE allocated across both teams.

First moves identified include launching an RFP for regional partner selection using an Equity Partnership Framework (EPF), mapping next steps for improving state P20W data quality following feasibility studies, and initiating targeted technical assistance in regional partnerships based on capacity assessments. The document also references collaborative opportunities such as enabling automatic admissions pilots, supporting charter advocacy, and testing hybrid dual credit options in Yakima. The DACI accountability model is used to define roles across WSI and USP Data teams, supporting clarity in decision-making and execution.

FILE: 09152022_WSI Strategy Approval_ Presentation Deck.pptx
This September 2022 presentation was prepared for a Gates Foundation U.S. Program strategy approval meeting focused on the Washington State Initiative (WSI). The meeting sought formal approval of the WSI strategy, presented to USP President Allan Golston and senior leadership including representatives from the Foundation Strategy Office (FSO), USP Data, and Postsecondary Success teams.

The WSI strategy is grounded in place-based principles and was shaped by input from over 200 local stakeholders across Washington State, including partners and beneficiaries in regions such as Puget Sound, Spokane, Kitsap, Yakima, and Wenatchee. Stakeholder feedback emphasized the need for locally tailored approaches, improved data infrastructure, and better cross-sector coordination across K-12, postsecondary, and workforce systems.

The strategy's first focus area is postsecondary (PS) enrollment, identified as a critical area of concern in Washington State. Key data points include stagnant direct PS enrollment rates of approximately 60% from 2013-2019, a 19% high school non-graduation rate among 9th graders in 2019, and severe COVID-19-related enrollment declines — particularly a 19.3% drop at 2-year public institutions and disproportionate impacts on Indigenous (24.3%) and Hispanic (22.8%) students. The strategy specifically prioritizes improving outcomes for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students, aligning to USP Goal 3.

The theory of change is built around mutually reinforcing actions at the regional and state levels. Regions serve as the primary unit of change, with strengthened education partnerships tasked with convening stakeholders, using data for continuous improvement, driving uptake of quality solutions, and influencing state policy. The state level functions as a critical enabler by improving data infrastructure, advancing high-impact policies, strengthening funder collaboration, and promoting cross-regional learning.

The strategy draws on national assets and exemplars, including NCAN's FAFSA completion challenge work, Tennessee's Drive to 55 initiative, and Dallas Commit's regional intermediary model. Several existing USP assets — including the Value Commission, Pathways frameworks, and USP Data systems — are identified as applicable and were deeply integrated into WSI strategy development.

The long-term vision is to ensure every Washington State student has the opportunity to pursue a credential of value with a path to meaningful employment. The 4-year strategy goal centers on improving transition-related student outcomes through widespread adoption of effective transition solutions and an enabling environment at both regional and state levels.

FILE: 092922 Postsecondary Advising Appendix A.pptx
This appendix (Appendix A) is a market dynamics analysis supporting a postsecondary advising strategy document, produced in September 2022 in partnership with Tyton Partners. It provides a detailed landscape of the higher education advising technology market, segmented into incumbents and upstarts across core and point solution categories.

The market is characterized as complex and fragmented, with roughly 6-7 incumbent vendors holding approximately 48-52% market share in 2022 through ongoing M&A consolidation. Key consolidation events include EAB acquiring Starfish from Hobson, Blackboard merging with Anthology, EAB acquiring WISR, and Ellucian acquiring CampusLogic. Despite consolidation, fragmentation persists among new entrants, and integration/interoperability between systems remains a critical unresolved problem.

The document evaluates advising technology vendors across four dimensions: popularity (client usage share), basic functionality (provision of all four iPASS functions — Course Planning & Degree Audit, At-risk Student Identification, Advising Management, and Analytics & Reporting), satisfaction (Net Promoter Score), and affordability. The analysis covers both four-year and two-year institutions, finding that dominant vendors offer minimal satisfaction and affordability, particularly for lower-resourced institutions and community colleges.

A central strategic concern is that solutions enabling full iPASS functionality are unaffordable for lower-resourced four-year institutions and community colleges. These institutions tend to use less capable but cheaper tools (e.g., Jenzabar, Aviso), and advisors report needing an increasing number of disconnected technologies to understand student needs. Siloed data is identified as an acute barrier to effective advising.

The document defines BMGF's 1,693 'focus institutions' based on six criteria: Title IV eligibility in U.S. states, enrollment of at least 500 undergraduates, open or near-open admissions or high Pell/URM enrollment, financial stability (liquidity ratio ≥ 1.0), below-70% success rates for URM or Pell populations, and a value metric still to be determined. Among these focus institutions, the top barriers to improving advising are departmental silos and limited budgets, with minority-serving institutions (MSIs) disproportionately naming these barriers.

The document proposes segmenting the 1,693 focus institutions by highest degree level offered, enrollment size, and a third unstated metric to tailor technology and reform strategies. This market analysis directly informs Gates Foundation's postsecondary advising investment strategy by identifying where market gaps exist, which vendors serve or underserve focus institutions, and what structural barriers must be addressed to improve advising equity and student success outcomes.

FILE: 092922 Postsecondary Advising Appendix B.pptx
This document is Appendix B of a September 2022 postsecondary advising presentation prepared for the Gates Foundation's Postsecondary Success strategy area. It examines the implications of COVID-19 on postsecondary advising systems, institutions, and students — particularly those who are Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income (BLI).

The appendix is organized around three main themes: (1) core advising technologies and data infrastructure, (2) COVID-19 impacts on institutions and advisors, and (3) COVID-19 impacts on students across demographic groups.

On the institutional side, the document notes that while 70% of institutions have partial or full deployment of advising case management systems, and 78% have data governance policies, many still lack seamless data integration needed to support accurate predictive analytics. Advisors during the pandemic faced challenges including disconnection from colleagues, excessive caseloads, inadequate staffing, and insufficient financial and technological resources. Budget cuts led to staff reductions and elimination of support programs, even as virtual advising became the norm.

On the student side, 87% of students experienced disruption during the pandemic, with 84% having classes moved online. Black, Hispanic/Latino, and American Indian/Alaska Native students experienced the most enrollment disruptions yet received the least institutional technology support. Digital inequity — particularly internet access gaps — disproportionately affected students of color. Only 60% of institutions provide students access to their own academic data, and fewer than half have course or degree recommendation technologies. Only 39% of institutions have tools to refer students to social or community resources.

The document makes the case for expanded investment in: advising case management and CRM systems, data-informed early alert tools, degree planning and credit management technologies, and personalized course/program recommendation systems. It emphasizes that technology must function as an interconnected ecosystem rather than a set of standalone tools.

This document connects to the Gates Foundation's USP strategy by highlighting persistent equity gaps in advising access and technology infrastructure that disproportionately affect BLI students — a core target population. It provides evidence-based context for advising technology investments, underscoring the importance of integrated data systems, proactive outreach, and scalable support tools to drive postsecondary credential attainment.

[Slide 1]
APPENDIX B: Implications of COVID-19

[Slide 2]
Core technologies
Services are a seamless part of a student’s experience, which need to function as interconnected tools rather than standalone interventions. Institutions should include an advising case management system that tracks student interactions (70% partial or institution-wide deployment).

Data and analytics technologies and practices support managing, sharing, and decision-making 
Advising policies are increasingly data-informed and data-driven. An explosion of vendor-provided and homegrown technologies has emerged, each designed to enhance advisor/student interactions and to provide predictive analytics. These analytics help to increase accessibility to staff, faculty, and institutional support resources that can positively impact students.
In order to enable accurate analytics, institutions need to ensure access to comprehensive student data that is seamlessly integrated and easily accessed to make sure no student is overlooked or mismanaged.

Data management and governance
Although policies and practices to safeguard data are widespread (in place at 78% of institutions), fewer institutions report having the data they need, or the ability to share data across systems. This suggests that even though most institutions have the core technologies and policies in place, they still have significant progress to make in accessing and integrating needed data.

Strategies for successful remote advising
Technologies to help identify and intervene with students at academic risk or to give students social or community resource referrals are still extremely new. Even so, students appreciate them very much and might need them now in particular.[5]

FILE: 092922 Postsecondary Advising Appendix C.pptx
This appendix presentation (Appendix C) from September 2022 documents findings and equity insights from five postsecondary advising demonstration projects launched in November 2021, with a total investment of $2.2 million. The projects were designed to address three high-level integration barriers in postsecondary advising technology and to fill critical data gaps related to student lifecycle management and degree progress.

Early insights from the five projects explored integration strategies across different EdTech products, the viability of affordable comprehensive student lifecycle solutions, the challenges of building connectors to address degree progress gaps, and opportunities for creating holistic student profiles. Key sourcing included interim learning reports from The Ada Center (May 2022) and an interim equity report from Paritii (July 2022).

A central focus of this appendix is equity analysis, organized around Paritii's Eight Domain EdTech Equity Framework. This pyramid-structured model identifies hierarchical conditions that must be met to create equity-aligned EdTech solutions capable of closing gaps for Black, Latino, and low-income (BLI/LI) students. Key equity themes identified across the five projects include: trust and personalization in student-advisor relationships, the digital divide as a barrier to technology-mediated services, complex interrelationships among students, advisors, and institutions, the importance of centering BLI/LI students in holistic data collection, and the need for technology-capability alignment including data literacy and cultural responsiveness among staff.

Critical observations highlight both challenges and opportunities. A significant challenge is that none of the five demonstration projects included BLI representation in ideation, design, or development, and none conducted root cause analyses for outcome gaps. Projects also lacked sustainable technology and business plans for serving BLI/LI students, and exhibited gaps in holistic data collection, data privacy, and data governance structures. Opportunities identified include moving toward targeted universalism, prioritizing anti-racism in solution design, and ensuring race- and culturally-responsive programming is embedded across all phases of data construction, algorithm building, and interpretation.

This document is directly relevant to the Gates Foundation's Postsecondary Success strategy, particularly efforts to support equitable advising technology ecosystems at institutions serving BLI/LI students. The findings inform future investment decisions around EdTech integration, vendor selection, and institutional capacity-building for equity-aligned student advising.

FILE: 092922 Postsecondary Advising Appendix D.pptx
This appendix presentation details Maricopa County Community College District's (MCCCD) ASSIST platform, an open-source CRM-based advising and student support technology system developed to address fragmented data infrastructure across the district's 10-college system. The document is structured as a technical deep-dive appendix to a broader postsecondary advising strategy discussion, likely supporting a Gates Foundation investment or learning review.

The first section describes MCCCD's legacy state: siloed, patchwork systems with no unified data layer for advisors. Each college used PeopleSoft core systems but configured them differently, with dated Cognos reporting tools and a data lake accessible only to researchers. The result was that advisors had to navigate multiple fragmented applications, encountering incomplete, duplicative, or inaccurate student data.

The core innovation described is ASSIST — a customized extension of SuiteCRM (the largest active open-source CRM project) adapted specifically for the community college use case. ASSIST acts as a 'single pane of glass' portal that unifies prospect management, enrollment, advising, early alert, marketing automation, scheduling, and case management tools. It is vendor-agnostic and modular, allowing individual colleges to maintain workflow flexibility while accessing a shared, synchronized data layer.

The technical architecture section explains how AWS services power the data layer: AWS DMS or Lambda handles data ingestion from systems of record (SIS, LMS, ERP), AWS Aurora Serverless v2 provides scalable database storage, AWS S3 handles object storage, and AWS Athena enables data abstraction to analytics tools like Apache Superset, Power BI, or Tableau. The Student Lifecycle CRM (ASSIST) visualizes data from application through graduation.

Strategically, this document is relevant to the Gates Foundation's Postsecondary success work because it illustrates a scalable, cost-effective advising infrastructure model for large community college systems. MCCCD's approach — building on open-source technology and cloud-native AWS infrastructure — offers a potential replicable framework for other institutions seeking to unify advising data without prohibitive commercial CRM costs. The emphasis on real-time data synchronization, early alert, and holistic student presentation aligns with foundation priorities around improving advising capacity and postsecondary completion for underserved students.

FILE: 092922 Postsecondary Advising Appendix E.pptx
This document is Appendix E from a September 2022 Postsecondary Success strategy presentation focused on postsecondary advising strategy development milestones and the organizational dimensions of advising reform. It outlines a multi-year strategy development timeline spanning from December 2020 through Q2 2023, including the launch of five demonstration projects (November 2021), co-chair learning sessions featuring ASU and Maricopa, a Presidential Strategy Review, field leader and funder advisory group meetings, and a Leadership Strategy Decision Meeting planned for October 2022. Key activities include mini-research projects on integration sub-topics such as CRM systems and advising definitions, open-source lessons and assessments, decision support tool discovery research, and monthly learning documentation touchpoints with demonstration project grantees.

A central framework presented is the People, Process, and Technology (PPT) model for holistic advising, which emphasizes that institutional challenges extend beyond technology complexity to include human resource competency, process reform, and organizational culture. The document draws on CCRC research (including iPASS initiative findings) to argue that advising redesign using technology requires comprehensive organizational change across four dimensions: institutional culture (norms, mission, racial composition, decision-making), attitudes (orientations toward equity, student support, technology, and change), structures (technology products, organizational charts, project management infrastructure, policies), and process (nature of advising interactions, use of data, culturally responsive practices, and progress monitoring strategies).

Strategic implications for BMGF include potentially building institutional capacity and subsidizing IT and data staffing, covering upfront technology costs that serve as adoption barriers, and ensuring project timelines include dedicated bandwidth for planning and implementation support. The framework connects to USP's postsecondary success strategy by addressing how technology-mediated advising supports can be implemented at scale at focus institutions — particularly minority-serving and broad-access colleges — while centering equity, student experience, and institutional readiness as core design considerations.

FILE: 092922 Postsecondary Advising Strategy Presentation.pptx
This September 2022 confidential presentation was prepared for a Postsecondary (PS) Advising Learning Session convened by the Gates Foundation's U.S. Program Postsecondary Success team, led by PS Director Patrick Methvin and Deputy Director Rahim Rajan. The session brought together an Expert Advisory Group, an Expert Panel of edtech and higher education field leaders, and internal USP advisors to present findings from approximately one year of strategy development research.

The core strategic argument is that holistic advising — defined as comprehensive, personalized, and culturally relevant academic support — is a proven driver of postsecondary student success for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students, but cannot be scaled without integrated advising technology. Evidence cited includes the ASAP program (24% STEM graduation rate increase), CUNY ACE (16% graduation rate increase), Carolina Works (18% Black student retention increase), and the MAAPS Advising Experiment (0.22 GPA increase among Black students).

The presentation outlines four analytical sections: (1) defining quality holistic advising and its components, (2) diagnosing barriers to quality advising — including fragmented institutional tech stacks, data ownership challenges, decentralized procurement, and lack of interoperability — (3) presenting three strategy scenarios for market intervention differentiated by institutional segment and market lever, and (4) establishing goals and next steps.

The recommended scenario proposes building and scaling a holistic data and application 'layer cake' for focus institutions, potentially leveraging existing cloud-based incumbents or scaling a purpose-built open-source product with implementation support. The strategy targets four outcomes: increased availability of affordable holistic advising products, greater awareness of interoperability standards, broader adoption of advising technology, and improved KPI outcomes for focus student populations.

The presentation concludes with a request for approval of a $7 million investment in critical strategy development activities — including research, feasibility testing, seed funding, and market convening — with a commitment to return with a full strategy proposal in Q3 2023. This work connects to USP's broader postsecondary success theory of change by targeting technology market dynamics as a root cause limiting the scale of proven advising interventions at community colleges and other focus institutions serving historically underserved students.

FILE: 10062022 Pathways Strategy Review -Digital Binder-FINAL.pdf
This October 2022 Co-chair Strategy Review digital binder covers the Gates Foundation's Pathways strategy, which was approved in February 2021 and runs through 2024 with a $132M budget. The strategy aims to reverse declining postsecondary enrollment rates — which fell to 63% in 2020 — by helping Black, Latino, and low-income students successfully transition from high school to postsecondary credentials of value.

The strategy operates across five bodies of work: (1) Integrated Advising Solutions, targeting expanded reach to 500,000+ students through providers like OneGoal, which demonstrated 46% higher college enrollment for program participants; (2) Accelerated Postsecondary Pathways, including the Accelerate[ED] initiative supporting 12 regional intermediaries and ASU to develop career-connected AA-by-year-13 (AAx13) programs; (3) Math Pathways, aiming for 15 states to adopt new high school math course frameworks by 2024; (4) Credit Mobility Platforms, testing technology solutions including ASU's Trusted Learner Network to improve credit transfer transparency; and (5) the Texas State Exemplar, leveraging House Bill 3's CCMR bonus incentive structure to drive district uptake of pathways programs.

Strategy progress is mixed: advising model adaptation is on track but demand catalysis among districts is slower than expected; Accelerate[ED] designs are compelling but nascent; credit mobility work is slightly below expectations due to partner capacity issues in Dallas; and the National Student Clearinghouse's Student Tracker 3.0 is over a year delayed. The Texas TUC districts notably surpassed an 80% FAFSA/TASFA completion goal.

Key 2023 investment priorities include $5M incremental for Accelerated Postsecondary Pathways to fund implementation of 5-8 promising Accelerate[ED] regional models, and $3.5M for a Cross-Cutting Strategy Drivers and MLE body of work to prepare enabling conditions for scaled adoption post-2024. The team is also building a funder co-investment network of 40+ national and regional funders, with $365M collectively invested in pathways since 2019.

Policy wins in focus states include California securing $200M for dual enrollment and $500M for career pathways, Ohio expanding College Credit Plus criteria, and Florida expanding dual enrollment access. At the federal level, the Biden Administration's Department of Education launched a Pathways call to action aligned with the foundation's framework.

The post-2024 phase is expected to shift from proof-point development toward broader scaling of advising, credit mobility, math pathways, and dual enrollment solutions, with increased emphasis on state and federal policy levers. The team collaborates closely with USP Data, K-12, Postsecondary Success, and North America teams. Investment scoring highlights OneGoal and Just Equations as top performers, while the NSC Student Tracker 3.0, Frontline Solutions, and Commit!2Dallas credit mobility work were bottom performers.

FILE: 10062022 Postsecondary Strategy Review -Digital Binder-FINAL.pdf
This October 2022 Co-chair Strategy Review digital binder for the Postsecondary Success (PS) strategy presents a comprehensive annual update to foundation co-chairs and the CEO. The strategy aims to create equitable value and economic mobility by increasing postsecondary attainment rates and closing opportunity gaps for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and students from low-income backgrounds through two overarching goals.

Goal 1 (Proof of Scale) is rated 'meets expectations,' driven by reinvestment in six Intermediaries for Scale (IFS: AASCU, AIHEC, CCA, Excelencia in Education, Growing Inland Achievement, and UNCF), reinvestment in three solution delivery networks, and increased network use of transformational tools. The strategy targets approximately 250 institutions served by these intermediaries, with placeholder outcome targets including 5pp YoY increases in first-year retention and 3.5pp YoY increases in completion rates. National Scale Enterprises (ASU, WGU, SNHU) are also being tracked as complements to the IFS ecosystem, with projected equitable degree production increases through 2029.

Goal 2 (Develop Public Goods) is rated 'slightly below expectations,' primarily because solution products—courseware, advising technology, and developmental education reform—do not yet meet quality and equity thresholds. Key public goods include the Institutional Transformation Assessment (used by ~350 colleges), the NSC Postsecondary Data Partnership (676 institutions), the Equitable Value Explorer, the Value Data Collaborative (launched with three states), and the Carnegie Classification Economic Mobility Marker. The Guided Pathways Framework shows sustained evidence with 2–9% increases in key metrics and 2–10% reductions in racial equity gaps.

Major 2022 portfolio activities include the launch of exemplar courseware in Statistics (Lumen) and Chemistry (ASU/CMU), transition of Coursegateway.org to EDUCAUSE, the HBCU Digital Learning Infrastructure co-design process with six HBCU partners, and policy wins including Pell Grant increases and DevEd reform progress in California, Washington, and Ohio. The College Transparency Act passed the House but was removed from final Senate legislation.

The investment plan requests an incremental $7M for the Develop Advising body of work ($5M in 2023, $2M in 2024) to scope a larger 'big bet' advising technology play anticipated in 2024. The 4-year outlook allocation totals approximately $487M across Delivery, Enabling Context, Global Policy and Advocacy, Institutional Transformation, Solutions, and Other categories.

Key 2023 priorities include supporting IFS institutions to reach initial milestones, releasing courseware MVPs, launching HBCU Digital Learning Infrastructure investments, refining the advising technology strategy, and advancing National Scale Enterprise equity goals. Co-chair engagement is requested for Q4 2022 courseware deep dive, Q2 2023 HBCU update, Q3 2023 advising and NSE updates.

FILE: 10062022 USP Data Strategy Review -Digital Binder-FINAL.pdf
This digital binder contains materials for the October 6, 2022 USP Data Co-Chair Strategy Review meeting. The USP Data strategy aims to modernize and use education-to-workforce (E-W) data systems to identify and close disparities so that race and socioeconomic status are no longer predictors of education attainment and economic mobility. The strategy is organized around four ecosystem components: Demand, Systems, Governance, and Use (DSGU).

The strategy encompasses three main bodies of work: (1) Scale National Postsecondary-to-Workforce Data Systems ($46.8M managed budget), including the National Student Clearinghouse's Postsecondary Data Partnership (PDP) with nearly 700 institutions, the Coleridge Initiative's Administrative Data Research Facility (ADRF) expanded to 14 states, and the Value Data Collaborative; (2) Build Statewide P20W Data System Exemplars ($47.6M), focused primarily on Texas with holistic modernization across TEA, THECB, and TWC, and model components in California, Washington, South Carolina, and New Mexico; and (3) Innovate with R&D ($11.3M), proposing investments in data interoperability, privacy-enhancing technologies, and enhanced longitudinal records.

Overall strategy performance is largely meeting expectations, with strong momentum on national platform adoption and the release of the Education-to-Workforce Indicators Framework. State work is slightly below expectations due to competing COVID priorities and challenging political environments. Governance work is also slightly below expectations due to R&D strategy delays.

A major cross-cutting deliverable is the E-W Indicators Framework, which identifies 20 essential questions spanning the pre-K through workforce continuum and provides a public good for aligning field efforts. The framework has been well-received internally and externally, prompting a cross-division implementation working group.

The binder includes a joint appendix on the Texas State Exemplar, where USP Data and Pathways teams collaborate under a shared theory of action leveraging Texas's HB3 CCMR bonus incentive structure to drive district demand for pathways programs and data use. In 2020-21, districts captured only 17% of the $1.6B potential CCMR bonus, indicating significant opportunity.

The 2023-2026 total managed budget is $115.9M including funding envelopes from Postsecondary Success ($36M) and Economic Mobility and Opportunity ($7.7M) teams. Two incremental requests totaling ~$20M for 2023-24 are planned: one to execute the R&D portfolio and one to expand E-W Framework implementation. Investment scoring highlights top performers including WestEd (California C2C planning), Mathematica (E-W Indicator Framework), IHEP (Postsecondary Data and Equitable Value), and the Coleridge Initiative (ADRF), while bottom performers include Texas 2036, FSG MLE work, Harvard SDP fellowship, and University of Virginia workforce data project. Key co-chair discussion areas include balancing long-term state relationship building with short-term progress demonstration and navigating increasingly politicized data environments.

FILE: 2022 USP Division Summary Memo.pdf
This November 2021 memo summarizes the U.S. Program (USP) division's progress, priorities, and resource allocation plans for the 2022 planning cycle. It covers eight strategy teams: K-12 Education, Postsecondary Success (PS), Pathways, Economic Mobility & Opportunity (EMO), USP Data, Early Learning (EL), Washington State Initiative (WSI), and US Charters.

The document opens by contextualizing USP work within COVID-19 impacts: preschool enrollment dropped to its lowest level in 25 years, K-12 enrollment declined nearly 3%, postsecondary enrollment fell significantly (especially at 2-year publics), and math achievement scores declined 8–12 percentile points below pre-pandemic norms. Disproportionate impacts on students of color and low-income families are highlighted throughout.

Key strategic progress includes: the K-12 team advancing tutoring investments and A/B testing of digital math materials, launching the Advanced Education R&D Fund ($250M with Chan Zuckerberg and Walton); the PS team documenting graduation rate improvements at Guided Pathways colleges and launching a courseware quality challenge; the Pathways team receiving strategy approval and launching the Texas Pathways Exemplar, Credit Mobility, and AA by 13 initiatives; EMO making direct-impact investments (e.g., Code for America tax prep serving 111,000 people); USP Data advancing the Postsecondary Data Partnership (550 institutions) and state P20W systems; and Early Learning launching assessment collaborations post-strategy approval.

Priorities for 2022 center on three cross-cutting themes: accelerating next-generation digital learning, enhancing data-enabled student supports, and changing the high school-to-college paradigm. K-12 is developing a new math-focused strategy for Co-Chair approval in Q2 2022. PS is pursuing proof-of-scale at 300 institutions, operationalizing DEI, and capitalizing on Build Back Better federal funding. EMO is seeking approval for a new strategy targeting adults below 200% of the federal poverty line, with an incremental request of $236.6M.

The proposed 4-year outlook (2022–2025) totals $2.827B in direct grantee support, with K-12 as the largest allocation (41.5%, $1.172B), followed by PS (18.1%) and EMO (16.5%). A net incremental request of $277M is made, driven primarily by EMO ($236.6M) and US Charters ($30.4M for Charter School Growth Fund reinvestment). USP also requests $6.2M in rollovers across five teams.

Key risks identified include continued COVID disruptions, the digital divide, political opposition to critical race theory and charter schools, enrollment declines, partner capacity constraints amid federal funding influx, and staff burnout. Dependencies on the North America Team (NAT), FP&A, HR, and Legal are noted across all strategy teams. The memo reflects a division in active strategic transition, with EMO and K-12 in strategy refresh, WSI preparing for a 2022 refresh, and newer teams (Pathways, EL, USP Data) in early execution phases.

FILE: Pathways Co-chair presentation 10062022.pptx
This 2022 strategy review presentation deck was prepared for a co-chair meeting focused on the Gates Foundation U.S. Program's Pathways strategy. The document outlines the Pathways team structure, strategic framework, portfolio performance, and forward-looking goals for the 2021–2024 strategy period and beyond.

The Pathways strategy's North Star Goal centers on Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds obtaining credentials of value and gaining professional skills, agency, and social capital to succeed in the workplace. The strategy is organized around three pillars: (1) Shifting Enabling Conditions, (2) Driving Innovation in Pathways Solutions, and (3) Enabling Cross-Sector Delivery Models.

Key bodies of work include: integrated postsecondary advising (targeting reach of over 500,000 students), math pathways (supporting 15 states to adopt new high school math course frameworks aligned with postsecondary requirements), credit mobility platforms (in partnership with Postsecondary Success and USP Data), accelerated Associate's Degree pathways (the AccelerateED initiative for grades 11–14), and pathways intermediaries (validating and codifying the function of regional intermediary organizations). A Texas State Exemplar effort aims to create a statewide proof point for a coordinated pathways system spanning grades 11–14, with cross-agency coordination and a focus on increasing immediate postsecondary enrollment and state bonus funding tied to College, Career, and Military Readiness (CCMR) metrics.

The budget allocation for the current strategy period (2021–2024) reflects 57% toward the core Pathways bodies of work, 31% toward enabling conditions, and 9% toward cross-sector delivery. The portfolio dashboard indicates that execution in the first 18 months meets expectations broadly, though it is too early to assess likelihood of full success. Notable risks include mixed progress on catalyzing district demand for advising solutions, early-stage scaling mechanism development for AccelerateED, and inherent risk in credit mobility platform impact hypotheses.

The next phase of strategy (post-2024) envisions moving student momentum metrics at larger scale by building on proof points established in the current phase, including scaling advising, credit mobility, and math pathways solutions, and establishing system-level exemplars in key states. An example of model adaptation highlighted is OneGoal's digital enablement of its advising model, which uses daily elective class periods, peer cohorts, trained teacher-advisors, and college transition coaching.

This document is relevant to staff working across Pathways, Postsecondary Success, K-12, USP Data, and North America teams, and reflects cross-cutting coordination among multiple USP strategy areas.

FILE: Pathways Investment Plan 10062022.pdf
This document is the U.S. Program Pathways Investment Plan, generated from the Decision Support Application on September 10, 2022. It presents a comparative financial resource plan for the Pathways strategy area covering fiscal years 2022–2026, contrasting the previous plan with the updated 2023 resource plan that includes proposed incremental requests.

The previous financial resource plan (2022–2025) totaled $128.5M across four years. The largest investment category was Solutions at $77.9M (60.6%), followed by Delivery System at $27.0M (21.0%), Exemplars at $8.0M (6.2%), Global Policy & Advocacy at $6.1M (4.7%), Enablers at $5.5M (4.3%), and Other Body of Work Types at $4.0M (3.1%).

The 2023 Resource Plan, which incorporates currently proposed incremental requests, totals $135.6M across 2023–2026. Key changes include: the elimination of the Delivery System and Exemplars categories, replaced by Delivery Models ($20.8M, 15.3%); Solutions remains the dominant category at $95.5M (70.4%); Enablers at $6.0M (4.4%); Global Policy & Advocacy at $6.0M (4.4%); and Other Body of Work Types at $7.4M (5.4%). The net difference between plans shows increases of $4.0M in 2023 and $4.5M in 2024, with no difference in 2025.

The second page presents a 4-Year Outlook (4-YO) comparing allocations to existing committed funds, excluding incremental requests. The total 4-YO allocation is stable at approximately $31.7–$32.0M per year. However, committed funds drop sharply over time: $12.2M committed (38.1%) in 2023, $4.1M (12.9%) in 2024, and only $0.3M (0.8%) in both 2025 and 2026. This indicates significant uncommitted capacity in outer years, particularly in Delivery Models, Enablers, Global Policy & Advocacy, and Other Body of Work Types. Solutions carries the highest committed funding at $10.4M in 2023 (45.6% of its allocation).

Strategically, this document reflects a restructuring of how the Pathways portfolio categorizes its body of work, consolidating prior groupings and shifting proportionally more resources toward Solutions and a newly defined Delivery Models category. The large gap between allocations and commitments in 2025–2026 signals substantial grantmaking decisions yet to be made, underscoring the need for pipeline development within the Pathways strategy. This plan is relevant to USP portfolio management, budget planning cycles, and strategic prioritization within the Pathways focus area.

FILE: Pathways Investment Scoring Summary 10062022.pdf
This document is a $5M+ Investment Summary Report for the U.S. Program's Pathways strategy, created on September 12, 2022. It covers two major investments managed under the Pathways-Solutions funding model, both focused on improving postsecondary readiness and credit mobility for students.

The first investment (INV-006755, $6,687,657) was awarded to the University of Texas at Austin's Charles A. Dana Center for the 'Launch Years: Redefining High School College Readiness in Mathematics' initiative, managed by Bill Tucker. Running from October 2018 to November 2021, this fully paid-out grant aimed to build consensus among K-12, higher education, and employer stakeholders around new high school math pathways better aligned to postsecondary needs. Key outcomes included the publication of the 'Launch Years: A New Vision for the Transition from High School to Postsecondary Mathematics' report, creation of course frameworks for Modern Algebra II, Data Science, and Transition to College Mathematics, establishment of the Launch Years Math Organizations Leadership Network (11 national math organizations), and policy-level changes in Georgia aligning state math standards to Launch Years recommendations. The investment received 'Meets Expectations' ratings for both Impact and Execution Performance in 2022, with a 'Will Reinvest' designation.

The second investment (INV-030888, $6,000,000) was awarded to the Arizona State University Foundation for A New American University for 'Expand Credit Mobility: Phase II ASU Trusted Learner Network,' managed by Dale Whittaker. Running from October 2021 to September 2024, with $2.8M paid and $3.2M outstanding, this grant seeks to develop technology tools and community infrastructure to address the national credit mobility challenge with an equity focus. Three workstreams include Credit Mobility Technology Development (course equivalency tools, Dallas College as co-design partner), Trusted Learner Network Community Building (governance and architecture advisory established), and a Student-Facing Pocket tool (prototypes in development). The project received 'Too Early To Tell' for Impact Performance and 'Meets Expectations' for Execution Performance in 2022, with reinvestment prospects also listed as 'Too Early To Tell.' The managing team noted the project is on schedule but flagged weak coordination among the three ASU internal teams as a risk.

Together, these investments reflect the Pathways strategy's dual focus on improving K-12 to postsecondary math alignment and enabling seamless credit transfer for learners, with an emphasis on equity and systemic change. The report includes performance scoring across 2020, 2021, and 2022, and covers both managed and managed-and-funded investment types within the U.S. Program.

FILE: Pathways Investment Scoring Top 5 Bottom 5 10062022.pdf
This document is a confidential Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation report dated September 12, 2022, presenting the top five and bottom five performing investments within the U.S. Program Pathways strategy. It provides detailed performance scoring for ten investments across impact performance, execution performance, and reinvestment prospects for 2022.

Top performing investments include: (1) Community Partners' 'Advancing a Mathematics of Opportunity' ($749,999), which exceeded expectations by expanding math equity ecosystems, publishing six reports against a target of two, and growing national partnerships; (2) OneGoal's 'New Delivery Model' ($4.5M), which demonstrated strong evaluation results showing Fellows are 46% more likely to enroll in college and secured a $7.5M state-level contract with the Illinois State Board of Education; (3) New York University's 'Intermediary-led pathway - Here to Here' ($1.5M), which scaled from a Bronx-based to citywide intermediary influencing NYC Department of Education policy and expanding work-based learning to 2,500+ students; (4) Commit!2Dallas's 'Leveraging Federal Funding with Texas Urban Council of Superintendents' ($1.1M), achieving 83% collective FAFSA/TAFSA completion and supporting 838,000 students across Texas's ten largest urban districts; and (5) Foundation for California Community Colleges' 'Dual Enrollment for Equitable Completion in California' ($375,000), which influenced state policy and helped remove institutional barriers for underrepresented students.

Bottom performing investments include: (1) National Student Clearinghouse Research Center's 'High School Tracker 3.0' ($3.38M), which is over a year behind schedule with platform launch now projected through early 2024; (2) Frontline Solutions International's 'AA by Year 13 Organizing Partner' ($401,007), terminated early due to poor leadership, disorganized execution, and failure to meet any primary outcomes; (3) Commit!2Dallas's 'Credit Mobility in Dallas: Phase I' ($461,234), where the primary partner was disengaged and outcomes were only achieved through AACRAO and foundation intervention, with Commit excluded from Phase 2; (4) University of California, Irvine's 'Occupational Identity Research + Innovation Network Development' ($2.2M), delayed by COVID and characterized by an imbalance between academic and practitioner perspectives; and (5) Jobs for the Future's 'Building Equitable Pathways Phase II Community of Practice' ($1.775M), which struggled with learning arc design and member engagement but showed improvement by mid-2022.

The document reflects the Pathways strategy's focus on reducing equity gaps for Black, Latino, and low-income students in high school-to-postsecondary transitions, with investments spanning math equity, college access, dual enrollment, career-connected learning, data infrastructure, and intermediary capacity building. It provides actionable reinvestment guidance, with some investments flagged for reinvestment and others explicitly closed out.

FILE: Pathways Optional Appendix A 10062022.pdf
This document is Appendix A from the USP Data and Pathways 2022 Strategy Review, presenting Texas as a state exemplar for collaborative Data and Pathways investments. It provides strategic context, grantmaking history, outcome baselines, and future investment priorities for Texas through 2024 and beyond.

Texas offers a unique policy environment anchored by House Bill 3 (2019), which created a College, Career, and Military Readiness (CCMR) outcomes bonus incentive structure rewarding school districts when graduates successfully transition to postsecondary, military, or career pathways. In FY2021, districts captured only 17% ($270M) of the potential $1.6B CCMR bonus, largely due to low district awareness and limited data use. A tri-agency structure linking TEA, THECB, and TWC provides a governance framework, and a new master data sharing agreement in 2022 enables greater cross-agency coordination.

The foundation's investments operate on two tracks: (1) Pathways investments through the Texas Impact Network (TIN) and Texas Urban Council of Superintendents (TUC), supported by Contigo Ed, ESG, and E3 Alliance, to help districts adopt quality pathways programs, use data for improvement, and capture CCMR bonus funding; and (2) Data investments to modernize state-level P20W infrastructure at THECB, TEA, and TWC, and to scale regional tools like 'CCMR Insights' via Region 10 Education Service Center and the Texas Education Exchange (TxEdx).

Early results show TUC districts surpassing the 80% FAFSA/TASFA completion goal by June 2022, and Brownsville ISD serves as an exemplar, increasing college enrollment by 10 points and industry-based certifications by 35 points between 2015-2020. The document tracks six outcome areas through 2024: state-level coordination, regional delivery system capacity, model P20W data system, research/evaluation ecosystem, system-wide uptake of learner-centered programs, and longer-term momentum metrics (2025+).

Key challenges include political headwinds around 'culture wars' in K-12, legislative privacy concerns about data linkage, and anticipated legislative turnover. The foundation is following rather than leading state priorities in the current political climate and has recalibrated speed and breadth of legislative engagement, deferring aggressive policy change to 2025. The long-term 2026 goal is a fully linked P20W data system enabling seamless data sharing across K-12, postsecondary, and workforce sectors.

The document emphasizes equity by tracking outcomes for Black, Latino, American Indian, and low-income students, and frames Texas as a model that can be replicated in other states. Baseline data show statewide immediate postsecondary enrollment at 41% (down from ~50% pre-COVID), with significant racial disparities in CCMR metrics across TUC districts.

FILE: Pathways Optional Appendix B 10062022.pptx
This appendix (Appendix B) accompanies the 2022 Pathways Strategy Review and presents trend data on state-level 'momentum metrics' — a set of indicators predictive of student success along the high school to postsecondary continuum. The core finding is a gap of up to 40 percentage points between high school graduation rates and immediate postsecondary enrollment rates across Gates Foundation focus states, a disparity that worsened during the COVID-19 pandemic. The document maps a sequence of momentum metrics across three phases — Preparing, Applying, and Enrolling — encompassing indicators such as FAFSA completion, success in early college courses, college application rates, well-matched postsecondary selection, immediate postsecondary enrollment, and certificate or degree completion. On the workforce side, it also tracks work-based learning experiences, CTE course concentration, industry credential attainment, and employment in a quality job. A key strategic challenge identified is that most states and districts do not yet measure or publicly report these momentum metrics consistently, limiting the field's ability to monitor and act on student progress. The Foundation is working with USP Data partners, including Mathematica (which conducted a Momentum Metrics Analysis in July 2022), to develop a field-wide, uniform approach to the collection and reporting of Pathways student outcome indicators. This effort reflects the broader Pathways strategy goal of expanding state and district measurement and monitoring systems beyond high school graduation to encompass a cross-system definition of student success. The data presented is drawn from publicly available state-level sources. This appendix supports the Foundation's investment in data infrastructure and policy advocacy to close equity gaps in postsecondary access and completion, particularly for students from low-income backgrounds and students of color in focus states.

FILE: Pathways Optional Appendix C 10062022.pptx
This document is Appendix C from the 2022 Pathways Strategy Review, focused on building actionable pathways policies to close equity gaps in postsecondary preparation, access, and success. Working with partners, the Gates Foundation U.S. Program Pathways team has developed five Policy Pillars that describe high-impact, equity-driven state policy actions. A federal policy framework was noted as under development at the time of this document.

The immediate policy priorities highlighted in the document center on two of the five pillars: (1) Facilitating Seamless Transitions and (2) Connecting Education and the Workforce. These priorities are identified in green within the framework, while yellow-highlighted items represent next-phase areas for future policy exploration, signaling a phased and deliberate approach to policy adoption.

The document notes that advancing pathways policy priorities in the Foundation's focus states is a multi-year effort, and the ability to advance any particular policy depends on a variety of on-the-ground factors within each state. This reflects the Foundation's recognition that state political context, capacity, and stakeholder readiness vary significantly and must be accounted for in strategy execution.

Notably, the document flags that Aligning Data Systems policies are shared with the USP Data team, indicating cross-team coordination between the Pathways strategy area and the broader U.S. Program data infrastructure work. This cross-cutting element underscores the integrated nature of the Foundation's approach to postsecondary pathways, where data alignment is seen as foundational to policy effectiveness.

This appendix serves as a policy map and priority-setting tool within the broader 2022 Pathways strategy review, giving Foundation staff and partners a structured view of where to focus near-term advocacy and investment in state policy environments. It connects directly to USP strategy by operationalizing the Pathways team's equity-focused theory of change through concrete, tiered policy actions at the state level, with a longer-term federal dimension being built out in parallel.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Strategy Review Appendix 10062022.pptx
This confidential September 2022 appendix document supports a Postsecondary Success Strategy Review co-chair meeting. It provides detailed analysis and strategic framing around the foundation's systems approach to improving outcomes in gateway courses—specifically Introductory Statistics and General Chemistry—at open access postsecondary institutions.

The document draws on analysis of 340 Postsecondary Data Partnership (PDP) institutions encompassing over 800,000 unique student records across 301 unique Statistics courses. Key findings show significant variation in course success rates, with top-quintile open access institutions achieving roughly 79% course success rates compared to an average of 66% across all open access institutions—a gap of approximately 13 percentage points. Racial equity gaps are prominently highlighted: Black students average a 53% course success rate versus 68% for White students (a 15-point gap), and Latinx students average 64% versus 70% for White students (a 6-point gap).

Performance targets are anchored to these findings. The strategy aspires for institutions implementing exemplar courseware and evidence-informed teaching practices to achieve either a greater than 80% course success rate or a 10 percentage point increase at their institution. A 50% reduction in course success gaps between Black and Latinx students and the highest-performing group is also targeted, informed by outcomes observed across 12 ELE Lighthouse institutions. Both goals are expected to be achievable by the third semester of implementation.

The foundation's 'systems approach' is central to the strategy, encompassing: (1) course design aligned to modern, field-aligned learning outcomes; (2) development of exemplar courseware informed by student ethnographic research and a Teaching and Product Profile (TPP); (3) best-in-class instructional practices and professional development, particularly targeting adjunct faculty; and (4) market steering mechanisms to improve faculty discovery and adoption of high-quality courseware.

Investments highlighted include work with OpenStax for scalable OER content development, codification of learning science on motivation, engagement, and metacognition, and an additional R&D investment with Outlier to evaluate cinematic-quality lecture video combined with interactive learning supports. The document also references a research agenda examining subject area mastery, with preliminary results expected in 2024 and summary outcomes in 2026.

This document connects directly to USP Postsecondary strategy by operationalizing the gateway course improvement theory of change, setting measurable equity-focused targets, and detailing how the foundation's cross-sector investments in courseware, faculty practice, and research infrastructure work together to drive scaled, measurable improvements in student outcomes at open access institutions serving Black, Latinx, and low-income students.

Distribution of results
We analyzed 340 PDP institutions with over 800,000 unique student records across 301 unique Statistics courses and found significant variation in course success rates1 to inform our approach to setting target outcome goals for courseware (and in some cases, is helping us identify additional traits of what good looks like).
In terms of targets, we see the positive outliers (Top 20th percentile institutions) have a course success rate above 80%. These top-quintile rates were higher at more selective institutions (91%), than at the open access colleges (79%) that better mirror the selectivity and resourcing of our focus institutions.  
The average course success rates across these open access institutions is 66% (roughly 13ppt below the Top 20th percentile threshold).
We also analyzed course success rates at open access institutions by race/ethnicity and socioeconomic experience In this analysis we found gaps in the average course success for Black (53%) and White (68%) students (a 15% gap); and for Latinx (64%) and White (70%) students (a gap of 6%). These gaps will vary based on the institutions enrolled in the efficacy trial, so our target in the next column focuses on reduction of the broadest gaps between the success of our priority subpopulations and other populations.
Solution Systems Approach
We believe we can reach these ambitious targets because our systems approach uniquely focuses on improving  the components of learning experience design that drive measurable, scaled improvements in faculty practice, and student engagement, persistence, and mastery including:
Course design aligned to modern, field-aligned learning outcomes that define the content, concepts, and competencies that students should know and be able to do upon course completion
Novel, exemplar courseware embodying product characteristics, design considerations, and unmet needs of faculty teaching statistics and students (particularly our focus students informed by rich student ethnographic research) articulated in our TPP
Best-in-class Instructional Practices & Implementation to advance the adoption and scaled use of evidence-informed teaching practices, high-quality implementation of courseware, and digital pedagogy (particularly among adjunct faculty)
Market steering mechanisms including new decision-support tools to improve faculty discovery and adoption of high-quality courseware
Our exemplar courseware platforms will be instrumented for research and continuous improvement, informed by insights from the discovery phase, leading discipline experts, feedback from students and faculty (particularly those focus students and faculty using the courseware), technology developers, and educational researchers (see next page).

FILE: Postsecondary Success Strategy Review Co-chair Presentation 10062022.pptx
This September 2022 confidential co-chair presentation covers the Postsecondary Success (PS) strategy review for the 2022 cycle at the Gates Foundation's U.S. Programs. The document outlines the PS team's two primary goals for the 2023-2026 strategy period and provides ratings on current performance against each.

Goal 1, 'Proof of Scale,' is rated 'Meets Expectations.' It aims to demonstrate that approximately 250 institutions can use an integrated solution approach to transformation—increasing postsecondary attainment rates and closing opportunity gaps by race and income. Progress is driven by finalizing six Intermediaries for Scale (IfS) partners, reinvesting in three Solution Delivery partners, and increased network usage of transformational tools. Key risks include continued disruption to higher education enrollment and the need for implementation approaches with strong evidence of closing equity gaps, not just improving graduation rates. The strategy tracks system components including intermediary support, solution networks, transformational resources, National Scale Enterprises (ASU, WGU, SNHU), and enabling policy environments. Consequential strategy decisions are planned for Q3 2023, Q2 2025, and 2027 based on operational and student outcome progress.

Goal 2, 'Public Goods,' is rated 'Slightly Below Expectations,' reflecting that solution offerings do not yet meet quality and equity bars needed to eliminate race and income as predictors of postsecondary success and economic mobility. Public goods are at various stages of maturity, and annual targets for availability, awareness, adoption, and efficacy are being developed for 2023.

The 2023-2026 budget allocation distributes resources across Institution Transformation Networks (23%), Delivery/Intermediaries for Scale (33%), Transformational Resources (23%), Solutions (17%), and 3% to Strategy Portfolio Management. National Scale Enterprises (ASU, WGU, SNHU) sit within the Transformational Resources budget, with projected combined degree awards to focus students modeled through 2029.

Participants include senior leadership across USP, including directors for North America, Policy & Communications, K-12 Education, USP Data, WA State, and Early Learning & Pathways, along with PS SPOs covering SPM, Transformation, Solutions, and MLE portfolios. The presentation connects directly to USP's overarching postsecondary attainment strategy targeting Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Strategy Review Investment Plan 10062022.pdf
This Investment Plan document, dated September 8, 2022, presents the financial resource planning for the Gates Foundation U.S. Program's Postsecondary Success strategy area. It provides a comparative analysis across two planning cycles and a forward-looking commitment outlook through 2026.

The document presents three primary data views. First, the Previous Financial Resource Plan (2022–2025) shows a total four-year allocation of $504.9M across six body-of-work groups: Delivery ($121.0M, 24.0%), Solutions ($119.7M, 23.7%), Global Policy & Advocacy ($93.1M, 18.5%), Institutional Transformation ($87.0M, 17.2%), Enabling Context ($69.0M, 13.7%), and Other Body of Work Types ($15.0M, 3.0%).

Second, the 2023 Resource Plan—which includes currently proposed incremental requests—shows a revised total of $487.4M across the same period, representing a reduction of approximately $17.5M from the prior plan. Notable shifts include Solutions decreasing from $119.7M to $110.6M and Delivery decreasing from $121.0M to $116.0M, while Other Body of Work Types increased slightly from $15.0M to $16.4M. The percentage distributions remain broadly consistent across categories.

The Difference table highlights year-by-year changes between the two plans. In 2023, the 2023 Resource Plan is $6.3M higher than the previous plan, driven largely by increases in Solutions (+$6.1M) and Institutional Transformation (+$2.2M), offset by a decrease in Delivery (-$4.4M). In 2024, the new plan is $7.1M higher. By 2025, the plan is $1.5M lower, reflecting declining commitments in Institutional Transformation (-$0.6M) and Solutions (-$0.9M).

The second page presents a 4-Year Outlook Allocations comparison (not including incremental requests) against existing committed funds. Total 4-YO allocations decline modestly from $127.6M in 2023 to $114.9M in 2026. Committed funds drop significantly over the same period: $48.9M (38.3%) in 2023, $29.1M (23.8%) in 2024, $18.5M (16.1%) in 2025, and $14.8M (12.8%) in 2026, leaving increasing uncommitted balances ($78.8M through $100.2M) available for new investments.

By body-of-work group, Delivery has the highest commitment rate across all years (up to 84.6% in 2023), while categories such as Enabling Context, Global Policy & Advocacy, Institutional Transformation, and Solutions show very low or zero commitment rates beyond 2023, indicating significant grantmaking capacity yet to be deployed. This document is central to USP Postsecondary portfolio management discussions and supports strategic decision-making around pacing, prioritization, and incremental investment requests.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Strategy Review Investment Scoring - Top 5 Bottom 5 10062022.pdf
This document is a confidential Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation U.S. Program report dated September 12, 2022, presenting the top five and bottom five performing investments within the Postsecondary Success strategy. It provides performance scoring across impact, execution, and reinvestment prospects for ten grantees.

Top performing investments include: (1) American Indian Higher Education Consortium (INV-003706, $1.84M), which exceeded expectations for impact by building intermediary capacity for tribal college networks and achieving the highest score in the IFS Next Stage investing process; (2) Institute for Higher Education Policy (INV-000896, $4.29M), which exceeded impact expectations through the widely influential Postsecondary Value Commission and Equitable Value Explorer data tool; (3) American Association of State Colleges and Universities (INV-029894, $175K), which exceeded execution expectations by developing a white-label virtual platform for institutional transformation curriculum delivery; (4) Complete College America (INV-031883, $3.26M), which met expectations for its participatory co-design process for Digital Learning Infrastructure strategy with HBCUs as part of the Racial Equity Initiative; and (5) Education Design Lab (INV-031157, $436K), which met expectations for equity-centered stakeholder research on faculty professional development in gateway courses.

Bottom performing investments include: (1) Jobs for the Future (INV-006093, $4.78M), rated below expectations on execution and slightly below on impact due to inability to integrate learnings, weak equity alignment, and a business plan lacking metrics and KPIs; (2) Agnes Scott College (INV-016446, $147K), slightly below expectations due to COVID-related capacity challenges including PI and business partner changes; (3) DXtera Institute (INV-036242, $500K), below expectations on execution due to significant data access agreement delays undermining a degree planning technology integration project; (4) University of Southern California Race & Equity Center (INV-005973, $1.25M), below expectations on execution due to chronic lateness on deliverables despite high-quality equity coaching and learning sessions; and (5) Association of Public and Land-Grant Universities (INV-003711, $3.02M), slightly below expectations due to insufficient equity proficiency and weak relationships with non-PWI institutions.

Strategically, this report reflects the foundation's focus on scaling institutional transformation through equity-centered intermediaries, with particular attention to serving Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students. The document highlights the importance of racial equity capacity as a prerequisite for effective execution and long-term reinvestment decisions.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Strategy Review Investment Scoring Summary Report 10062022.pdf
This confidential Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation document is a $5M+ Investment Summary Report for the U.S. Program Postsecondary Success strategy, created September 12, 2022. It covers 22 investments across multiple strategy buckets including Delivery, Institutional Transformation, Enabling Context, Solutions, and Policy & Finance.

The largest cluster of investments falls under the 'Develop Intermediaries for Scale' bucket (B00034), reflecting a strategic pivot toward building organizational capacity among intermediary organizations. Six IFS Reinvestment grants totaling approximately $88M were awarded in August 2022 to Complete College America ($24M), United Negro College Fund ($17.9M), American Indian Higher Education Consortium ($17M), AASCU ($11M), Growing Inland Achievement ($9.7M), and Excelencia in Education ($9.2M). All are rated 'Too Early to Tell' given their recent activation, and all aim to improve outcomes for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students.

A major cross-cutting investment is INV-010248 with Rockefeller Philanthropy Advisors ($7.46M), the backbone for the Service Design and Delivery ecosystem linking institutional intermediaries and solution partners. It scores 'Meets Expectations' in 2022 but will not be reinvested as the foundation transitions to an equity-forward backbone partner (Promise 54).

The Frontier Set (FS) portfolio is largely concluding. Key FS investments include The Aspen Institute ($14.4M, community colleges), American Institutes for Research ($10.8M, evaluator), AASCU FS sites ($7.7M), Arizona State/University Innovation Alliance ($8.2M), APLU ($6.9M), and VentureWell ($6.6M). Most score 'Meets Expectations' on impact and execution in 2022. The UIA and VentureWell will not be reinvested; APLU and AASCU are rated 'May Reinvest.'

In the Solutions area, NASPA manages two advising network investments totaling ~$18M, both performing well and expected for reinvestment. Western Interstate Commission for Higher Education holds two digital learning investments ($13.3M and $8M) performing at or slightly below expectations. Tyton Partners ($8.1M) provided longitudinal market measurement across advising, DevEd, and digital learning solutions with mixed impact performance.

The Institute for Higher Education Policy ($6.8M, shared) supports postsecondary data infrastructure and the Equitable Value Reform movement, scoring 'Meets Expectations' with reinvestment expected. The National Student Clearinghouse Research Center ($6.8M) supports the Postsecondary Data Partnership (PDP), scoring slightly below expectations due to adoption slowdowns but reinvestment is planned.

Reach Fund II (INV-029385, $10M) is an EdTech seed fund that has fallen into breach of PRI requirements and is being withdrawn — the first such withdrawal in SIF's history.

Overall, the document reflects USP's strategic transition from the Frontier Set learning phase toward a Proof of Scale phase anchored by equity-focused intermediaries, solution networks, and postsecondary data infrastructure.

FILE: USP Data Co-Chair Presentation 10062022.pptx
This October 2022 presentation was prepared for the USP Data 2022 Strategy Review co-chair meeting, convening senior leaders across the U.S. Program including directors from K-12, Postsecondary, Early Learning & Pathways, Washington State Initiative, Economic Mobility & Opportunity, and the North America Team. The document provides a comprehensive overview of the USP Data team's strategy, portfolio, and performance.

The USP Data strategy is framed around a goal of addressing gaps in the education-to-workforce (E-W) data ecosystem so that practitioners and policymakers can implement evidence-based reforms that equitably improve outcomes for Black, Latino, and low-income learners. The strategy is organized into four major bodies of work (BOWs): (1) Build State Exemplars ($46.4M Data; $1.2M EMO), focused on modernizing Texas as a P20W data system exemplar and supporting California, Washington, and New Mexico; (2) Scale National Postsecondary-to-Workforce Data Systems ($36M Postsecondary; $5.8M Data; $5M EMO); (3) Innovate with R&D ($9.8M Data; $1.5M EMO), targeting interoperability, privacy, and enriched learner records; and (4) Field Coordination & Alignment ($2.7M core data budget), supporting dissemination of an E-W Indicators Framework and funder coordination.

The strategy's theory of change centers on four outcome domains: Demand, Systems, Governance, and Use. Desired outcomes include modernized systems collecting disaggregated data aligned to the E-W indicators framework, equitable governance structures, and regular use of E-W data by policymakers, researchers, and practitioners.

State-level work highlighted includes: Texas as the primary P20W modernization exemplar; California's planning of a Cradle-to-Career (C2C) system integrator RFP and P20W modernization playbook; Washington State's enhancement of ERDC data marts and dashboards for HS-to-postsecondary transition metrics (co-funded with WSI); and New Mexico's longitudinal data system planning with Google partnership and Strategic Data Project (SDP) fellow placement. Cross-state efforts include a Community of Innovation, SDP fellows at Harvard, SHEEO state data surveys, and support for the State Chief Data Officers Network at Georgetown's Beeck Center.

The USP Collaboration Framework is referenced as a mechanism for aligning roles, resources, and accountability across USP teams. The presentation also references a Data Policy, Advocacy & Communications workstream led by the North America Team with a $6.7M data envelope. The document is confidential and intended for internal strategic review purposes.
