FILE: USP Data Investment Plan 10062022.pdf
This document is the Investment Plan for USP Data, produced as part of the 2022 Strategy Review and dated September 11, 2022. It provides a detailed financial resource plan for the USP Data portfolio covering the 2022–2026 period.

The Previous Financial Resource Plan (2022–2025) showed a total investment of $78.3M, with USP Data comprising the dominant share at $70.8M (90.5%), alongside Global Policy & Advocacy at $6.7M (8.5%) and Other Body of Work Types at $0.8M (1.0%). The 2022 payout year was notably higher at $24.2M due to a large USP Data allocation of $22.4M.

The 2023 Resource Plan restructures the portfolio into six Body of Work (BoW) groups with a flat annual allocation of $18.0M from 2023–2026, totaling $72.1M. The new structure breaks out investments as follows: Building State Exemplars leads at $46.4M (64.3%), Accelerating Innovation at $9.8M (13.6%), Global Policy & Advocacy at $6.7M (9.3%), Scaling National Systems at $5.8M (8.0%), Leveraging Cross-Cutting Enablers at $2.7M (3.7%), and Other Body of Work Types at $0.8M (1.1%). The Difference table confirms zero net change year-over-year, with the prior USP Data group allocation of $16.1M/year redistributed into the new BoW structure.

The second section compares 4-Year Outlook (4-YO) Allocations against existing committed funds, excluding any proposed incremental requests. Total allocation is $18.0M per year across 2023–2026. Committed funds are concentrated in near-term years: $6.6M in 2023 (36.4% committed) and $2.7M in 2024 (15.2% committed), with 2025 and 2026 showing zero committed funds, indicating significant flexibility for new investments in those years.

At the BoW group level, Scaling National Systems is notably over-committed in 2023 at 111.0% ($1.6M committed against $1.4M allocated), signaling a potential resource tension. Global Policy & Advocacy and Leveraging Cross-Cutting Enablers are also highly committed in 2023 at 56.7% and 56.5% respectively. Building State Exemplars, the largest allocation, is 27.3% committed in 2023 and 16.3% in 2024, leaving substantial room for new grant-making.

This plan provides the financial backbone for the USP Data 2022 Strategy Review, illustrating how the portfolio is transitioning from a single large bucket (USP Data) into a differentiated set of strategic investment areas. The document is directly relevant to USP program planning and resource stewardship decisions.

FILE: USP Data Investment Scoring Summary 10062022.pdf
This confidential 2022 strategy review document from the Gates Foundation's U.S. Program (USP Data) summarizes investment scoring for two major grants exceeding $5M, evaluated on impact performance, execution performance, and reinvestment prospects.

The first investment (INV-002696) was awarded to the National Student Clearinghouse Research Center (NSC) for $6,819,847 to sustain the Postsecondary Data Collective (PDP) from June 2020 through December 2022. This Phase III grant focused on sustainability, dashboard enhancements, and release of the first PDP Insights Report. Performance ratings for 2022 were 'Slightly Below Expectations' for both impact and execution. Key challenges included higher-than-expected institutional attrition (33% vs. 20% projected), delays in technical enhancements due to difficulty hiring tech talent, and an antiquated organizational structure separating data and tech teams. Positive developments included meeting the 550-institution adoption goal after pricing was introduced, securing a multi-year contract with the CA Community College Chancellor's Office, and new intermediary partnerships. The managing team recommends reinvestment but emphasizes the need for organization-wide capacity building and tech infrastructure improvements including cloud migration.

The second investment (INV-031405) was awarded to the Institute for Higher Education Policy (IHEP) for $6,780,648 (shared across two funding teams) to advance postsecondary data governance and equitable value work from October 2021 through October 2024. The grant supports amplification of the Postsecondary Value Commission's findings, the launch of the Value Data Collaborative (VDC), and continued leadership of the PostsecData Collaborative. Performance ratings for 2022 were 'Meets Expectations' for both impact and execution, with reinvestment prospects classified as 'Expect To Reinvest.' Key results included the College Transparency Act (CTA) passing the U.S. House of Representatives as an amendment to the COMPETES Act, updates to the College Scorecard reflecting IHEP's recommendations, and the launch of the VDC with three pilot states leveraging the Coleridge Initiative's Administrative Data Research Facility for equity-centric analyses.

Together, these investments reflect USP Data's strategy to strengthen postsecondary data infrastructure, expand data access and use through governance reform, and advance federal student-level data networks. Both grantees are seen as critical to the foundation's Postsecondary Metrics Framework and equitable value agenda, with reinvestment anticipated for both organizations despite execution challenges at NSC.

FILE: USP Data Investment Scoring Top 5-Bottom 5 10062022.pdf
This confidential document from the USP Data 2022 Strategy Review presents performance scoring for the top five and bottom five investments managed by the USP Data team at the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation. Each investment is evaluated on Impact Performance, Execution Performance, and Reinvestment Prospects for 2022, with managing team rationale and key results provided.

Top Performing Investments: (1) WestEd (INV-033870, $475K) supported California's P-20W Cradle to Career data system planning, producing a modernization playbook and conceptual data model; rated Meets Expectations on both dimensions with plans to reinvest. (2) Mathematica Inc. (INV-029820, $1.18M) developed the equity-focused Education-to-Workforce (E-W) Indicator Framework, a first-of-its-kind consensus metrics framework spanning the pre-K-to-workforce continuum; adopted by NSC and NWCCU/SACSCOC and named a USP priority by USLT. (3) Institute for Higher Education Policy (INV-031405, $4.77M allocation) advanced postsecondary data equity through the PostsecData Collaborative and Value Data Collaborative, achieving a major milestone when the College Transparency Act passed the U.S. House. (4) National Student Clearinghouse Research Center (INV-031838, $400K) underwent a Partner Organization Effectiveness assessment via Slalom Consulting, catalyzing significant organizational restructuring and strategy refocusing. (5) The Coleridge Initiative (INV-000866, $4.42M) exceeded all grant objectives by building the ADRF data platform, training 800+ state agency staff, and launching the Democratizing our Data Challenge; now serving as data partner for the Value Data Collaborative.

Bottom Performing Investments: (1) Texas 2036 (INV-001480, $741K) faced significant delays in ERC data access due to FERPA concerns, failing to deliver the planned researcher data platform on time; rated Below Expectations with no reinvestment planned. (2) FSG, Inc. (INV-035006, $1.425M) supported Texas State Exemplar measurement and evaluation but was rated Slightly Below Expectations due to insufficient insight generation on regional intermediary data needs and weak connections between USP Data and Pathways strategies. (3) Harvard Graduate School of Education (INV-021324, $600K) failed to place federal education data fellows successfully due to insufficient capacity and overly complex legal structures; grant wound down and funds redirected to the Day One Project. (4) National Skills Coalition (INV-001137, $400K) produced on-time but tangential deliverables that did not replicate the impact of the former Workforce Data Quality Campaign; rated Slightly Below Expectations. (5) University of Virginia (INV-024835, $703K) completed a thorough 50-state scan of UI and postsecondary data infrastructure but could not deliver the planned outreach toolkit due to limited capacity to manage state agency relationships; rated Below Expectations with no reinvestment.

The document reflects USP Data's strategic focus on building state longitudinal data systems, scaling national data infrastructure, advancing education-to-workforce data linkages, and ensuring equity-centered data frameworks. Reinvestment decisions are clearly tied to milestone achievement, organizational capacity, and alignment with evolving strategy priorities.

FILE: USP Data Optional Appendix A 10062022.pdf
This 2022 strategy review appendix presents Texas as a state exemplar for integrated USP Data and Pathways investments. The document outlines the rationale, current state, investment priorities, outcomes framework, and key challenges for the foundation's work in Texas.

Texas is uniquely positioned due to its ambitious 60% postsecondary credential attainment goal by 2030 and its House Bill 3 (HB3) College, Career, and Military Readiness (CCMR) outcomes bonus structure, which incentivizes school districts financially when students successfully transition to postsecondary pathways. Despite this incentive, in 2020-21 districts captured only 17% ($270M) of the $1.6B potential CCMR funding, largely due to limited data use and low awareness. Immediate postsecondary enrollment dropped from ~50% pre-COVID to 41% in recent years.

The foundation's strategy leverages two interconnected investment streams: (1) Pathways investments supporting regional intermediary networks — specifically the Texas Impact Network (TIN) and Texas Urban Council of Superintendents (TUC), with partners Contigo Ed, Education Strategy Group, and E3 Alliance — to help districts improve student outcomes and maximize CCMR bonus earnings; and (2) Data investments to modernize state P20W (PreK-20-Workforce) data infrastructure across three agencies: Texas Education Agency (TEA), Texas Higher Education Coordinating Board (THECB), and Texas Workforce Commission (TWC).

Early results show promise: TUC districts surpassed an 80% FAFSA/TASFA completion goal by June 2022, and Brownsville ISD serves as a peer exemplar having increased college enrollment by 10 percentage points and industry-based certifications by 35 points. Regional tools like CCMR Insights and E3 Alliance dashboards are enabling data-driven decision-making where statewide infrastructure remains insufficient.

On the data modernization side, THECB is migrating to cloud infrastructure with Microsoft, creating a new labor market earnings portal for institutions, and releasing public outcome dashboards. A new tri-agency master data sharing agreement was completed in 2022, replacing 20-year-old agreements. Regional efforts include piloting the CCMR Insights tool on TEA's Texas Education Exchange (TxEdx) hub using Ed-Fi standards, in partnership with the Dell Foundation.

Six outcome areas are tracked through 2024: state-level coordination, regional delivery system capacity, model P20W data system, research and evaluation ecosystem, system-wide uptake of learner-centered supports, and longer-term momentum metrics (2025+). A forthcoming P20W System Modernization Rubric will assess maturity thresholds across demand, systems, governance, and use dimensions.

Key risks include political headwinds around 'culture wars' in K-12, legislative privacy concerns about data sharing, and high legislator turnover. The foundation is responding by following rather than leading state priorities, deferring aggressive legislative activity until 2025, and investing in scalable regional tools in the near term while maintaining long-term state infrastructure goals for 2026.

FILE: USP Data Optional Appendix C 10062022.pptx
This document is Appendix C of the USP Data 2022 Strategy Review, focusing on the Measurement, Learning, and Evaluation (MLE) approach for scaling national data systems and accelerating state P20W (Preschool through Workforce) data modernization. It outlines two primary evaluation efforts: a new PDP (Postsecondary Data Partnership) and ADRF (Administrative Data Research Facility) Performance and Sustainability Assessment, and a P20W System Modernization Rubric.

The PDP and ADRF Performance and Sustainability Assessment is designed to codify clear KPIs, benchmarks, and impact criteria to objectively measure progress and inform future investments. It notes that prior targets set five years ago have largely been met, and this new evaluation will set targets for the next phase, including determining when and how to reduce investment as platforms achieve sustainability. The framework assesses essential platform capabilities such as data collection, data quality, data linkages, technical infrastructure, privacy and security, field adoption, and financial sustainability. Expected insights include measuring the impact of platform updates on utility for postsecondary and pathways partners, the efficacy of complementary investments through intermediaries, and technical audit results assessing platform readiness for potential reuse as federal infrastructure.

The P20W System Modernization Rubric introduces maturity thresholds across four performance stages for key features of a fully linked P20W system. The rubric is organized around the DSGU (Data Systems for Good Use) framework and evaluates state progress across four domains: Demand (stakeholder engagement, shared research agenda), Systems (data coverage, quality, usability, interoperability), Governance (formal governance structures, continuous improvement), and Use (researcher data access, tools for individual progress tracking). A baseline assessment using this rubric will establish performance thresholds to monitor impact over time.

The document references cross-cutting evaluations spanning DSGU that will inform strategy impact timelines across key investment areas, and directs readers to the Texas Data/Pathways Appendix for related content. This MLE approach is integral to the USP Data strategy, providing an objective framework for assessing the foundation's investments in national and state-level data infrastructure that supports postsecondary success and workforce outcomes.

FILE: 031323 USP Roundtable on Math Pathways & Instructional Transformation Presentation Deck.pdf
This March 2022 confidential presentation deck was prepared for a USP roundtable bringing together Gates Foundation staff and external math experts to discuss the evolution of grades 6–12 mathematics content, pedagogy, and technology, and how high school math courses align to the national scaling of postsecondary math pathways. The two-hour meeting covered two primary topics: advances in what math is taught (curriculum and pathways alignment) and advances in how math is taught (pedagogy and digital tools).

Key external experts included Dan Meyer (Desmos/Amplify), Dash Young-Saver (Skew The Script), Dave King (Charles A. Dana Center), Ji Yun Son (CourseKata), Ken Koedinger (Carnegie Mellon), and Pam Burdman (Just Equations). BMGF participants included leadership from K-12 Education, Postsecondary Success, and Early Learning & Pathways teams, including Allan Golston (President, US Program), Patrick Methvin, Sara Allan, and Bob Hughes.

The deck showcases several edtech tools and pedagogical approaches. CourseKata is highlighted as a fully instrumented interactive statistics textbook featuring over 1,200 formative assessments, integrated R programming exercises, AI-powered Q&A (promoted as curiosity and metacognition support), and differentiated data flows for instructors versus researchers. Skew The Script is presented as an example of culturally relevant high school AP Statistics curriculum. Desmos is featured as an interactive graphing and activity tool that fosters student engagement through visual, exploratory challenges, with teacher dashboards showing real-time student progress.

Khan Academy is presented as a case study in adaptive learning, demonstrating step-by-step hint scaffolding, mastery-based progression, and teacher assignment score dashboards. Research from Ken Koedinger (Carnegie Mellon/PSLC DataShop) is included on AI-driven cognitive modeling, showing that more granular knowledge component models produce smoother learning curves and more effective educational technology. His work also presents a multi-level tutoring framework (PLUS system) distinguishing Level 1 (self-initiated remote tutoring for motivated students), Level 2 (AI dashboard-initiated tutoring for all students, showing 3x learning gains), and Level 3 (high-dosage small-group in-person tutoring for high-needs students, showing 2x learning gains).

Strategically, this roundtable reflects USP's interest in connecting K-12 math transformation—particularly at the high school level—with postsecondary math pathways reform, examining how curricular content choices and instructional technology investments can reduce remediation needs and expand equitable access to college-level mathematics. The discussion of debate areas around new high school math pathways and their alignment with postsecondary pathways (e.g., statistics, data science, quantitative reasoning as alternatives to calculus) is central to the foundation's pathways strategy.

FILE: 062823_USP Innovation Session on Motivation, Engagement, Persistence_Appendix 1.pdf
This document is a June 2023 Substack essay by Dan Meyer titled 'If I'm Wrong About AI in Education, Here's Why,' included as Appendix 1 to a USP Innovation Session on Motivation, Engagement, and Persistence. The author pre-registers three falsifiable bets against prevailing AI-in-education optimism, offering a skeptical counterpoint to claims made by figures such as Sal Khan, Marc Andreessen, and Luis von Ahn.

Bet #1 asserts that no peer-reviewed study will replicate Bloom's two-sigma tutoring effect using AI chatbot tutors within five years. Meyer argues that AI chatbots fail on two critical dimensions: they hallucinate at unacceptable rates, and even if accuracy improved, research shows it is already difficult to get students to engage consistently with human tutors — making AI engagement even less likely. He contends that meaningful learning gains require consistent, relational tutoring that AI cannot provide.

Bet #2 predicts that no national survey will find a plurality of K-12 students — given access to both a classroom teacher and a chatbot — preferring the chatbot. Meyer grounds this in the primacy of human relationship in learning, noting that student preference for AI would only emerge in contexts lacking a human teacher entirely.

Bet #3 argues that AI chatbots will not measurably affect teacher hiring or attrition in any of the ten largest school districts within five years. He dismisses the 'AI as teaching assistant' thesis by pointing to research showing teacher satisfaction is driven by resource availability, professional respect, disciplinary climate, self-efficacy, and collaboration — factors unaffected by AI tools.

The pre-mortem section acknowledges that these bets could be wrong if AI becomes indistinguishable from humans, or if students come to accept AI as relational partners. Meyer's most optimistic scenario is that AI functions as a quality-of-life tool for teachers and students — akin to a grammar checker — without transforming core learning dynamics.

The essay closes with a pointed critique of maximalist edtech claims as funding-driven rhetoric disconnected from classroom reality, echoing a historical pattern across radio, TV, VHS, and the internet. For USP strategy, this document is relevant as a critical framing resource for discussions about AI's realistic role in student motivation, engagement, and persistence — cautioning against over-investment in AI tutoring as a substitute for relational, teacher-driven instruction.

FILE: 062823_USP Innovation Session on Motivation, Engagement, Persistence_Pre-read Deck.pptx
This June 2023 pre-read deck prepares USP staff for an innovation learning session on Motivation, Engagement, and Persistence (MEP) as proximate drivers of student learning outcomes across K-12 and postsecondary contexts. The document recaps prior MEP conversations and advances the strategic framing for how the Gates Foundation's U.S. Program can invest to improve student MEP at scale.

The core argument is that MEP are measurable, research-backed constructs that directly precede learning gains, and that USP can influence them through three interconnected levers: (1) products and content (curriculum and instructional tools), (2) teacher and faculty practices, and (3) learning environments. The strategy prioritizes products and content as an early focus because of their potential for scale and their ability to indirectly shape teaching practices and classroom norms.

Motivation is organized through expectancy-value theory, which synthesizes related frameworks including ability mindset, mastery and performance orientation, interest and identity, and belonging. Engagement is categorized into three research-supported types, with the team evaluating which are most addressable through products and most measurable. Persistence is framed as productive engagement over time that enables knowledge transfer and proficiency.

A significant portion of the deck addresses context — both personal and systemic — as a critical moderating factor. Two student personas illustrate how contextual factors (e.g., race, disability status, family situation, prior knowledge) can either support or detract from MEP. Student personas include Nicole Davis, an 11-year-old Black girl with an IEP who likes math but struggles with literacy, and SheKinah McClellan, a Black single mother and military veteran in a postsecondary social work program. These personas are used to ground the design of products and teaching strategies in lived student experience.

The deck also maps key contextual factors relevant to K-12 and postsecondary teaching and learning strategies, drawing on sources such as TNTP, the Student Experience Research Network, and peer-reviewed journals. USP acknowledges it cannot address all contextual factors but can advance products and guidance that help educators engage student context meaningfully.

The product/content strategy has three prongs: building a validated supply of MEP-equipped math products, developing quality signals and guidance for the field, and establishing measurement infrastructure. This framing connects directly to USP's broader math strategy and supports future grantmaking decisions by clarifying what 'MEP-equipped' means and how it can be assessed. Grantee examples (e.g., Zearn, 'X In a Box') are referenced to illustrate current approaches being tested.

FILE: 062823_USP Innovation Session on Motivation, Engagement, Persistence_Presentation Deck.pptx
This June 2023 presentation deck was prepared for a USP Innovation Session focused on supporting student Motivation, Engagement, and Persistence (MEP). The session brought together multiple partners and featured live video demonstrations alongside slide content, covering both K-12 and postsecondary contexts.

Key content areas included: Digital Promise's measurement framework for affective engagement, which distinguishes between retrospective and real-time measures (surveys, learning system data, physiological sensors, facial/posture detectors). Their partnership with Lumen Learning focuses on retrospective reports and learning system data as currently the most feasible and cost-effective approaches.

Bank Street's contributions centered on learner variability and whole-learner design principles, emphasizing the importance of designing for social context, individual needs, and historically excluded populations rather than the 'average' learner. Their Learner Factor Map is grounded in literature review and expert consultation. Practices highlighted include student mathographies and empathy interviews as tools for building math identity through strengths-based approaches.

PERTS contributed content on fostering growth mindset through structured routines that normalize mistakes (e.g., 'my favorite no'), supported by the PERTS Student Voice Survey as a feedback tool for teachers.

Amplify/Desmos was featured via live demo (led by Dan Meyer), showcasing their edtech model for promoting student motivation through relevance, emotional engagement via self-expression, and cognitive engagement through peer connection — with an emphasis on helping teachers respond to early student thinking to build confidence and belonging.

Lumen Learning (via Kim Thanos) also presented live video content, likely focused on postsecondary engagement strategies given their known work in that space.

Strategically, this session reflects USP's interest in innovation at the intersection of social-emotional learning, learner-centered design, and technology-enabled engagement — spanning K-12 and postsecondary. It connects to broader USP priorities around equity, whole-learner approaches, and the role of edtech in supporting diverse student populations.

FILE: 082523 Q3 Accelerator Update - Future medicine_from molecular pathways_Levin.pdf
This peer-reviewed feature article from Trends in Molecular Medicine (2023), authored by Eric Lagasse and Michael Levin, presents a theoretical and translational framework for a next-generation approach to biomedicine grounded in the collective intelligence of living tissues. The document was shared as a Q3 2023 accelerator update, likely as background reading for a Gates Foundation U.S. Program session on emerging science relevant to health innovation.

The central argument is that the human body functions as a multiscale, information-processing system whose cells and tissues possess innate problem-solving, memory, and goal-directed capacities. Rather than continuing to focus solely on bottom-up molecular interventions (CRISPR, gene therapy, protein engineering), the authors propose that biomedicine should shift toward 'top-down' strategies that communicate desired outcomes to cellular collectives — exploiting their native competencies for anatomical homeostasis and repair.

Key scientific concepts include: (1) Multiscale Competency Architecture — each biological layer from molecules to organs solves problems in its own problem space; (2) Developmental Bioelectricity — non-neural bioelectric networks store and transmit pattern memories that guide morphogenesis, offering tractable targets for intervention via ion channel drugs (electroceuticals) and optogenetics; (3) The Hepatostat — a clinically grounded example where hepatocytes transplanted into lymph nodes regenerate functional ectopic livers precisely tuned to replace lost liver mass, driven by 'need of function'; and (4) Somatic Psychiatry — a proposed framework that applies tools from behavioral neuroscience and cybernetics to reprogram tissue-level goal states, analogous to psychiatric intervention in the nervous system.

Preclinical evidence is cited for bioelectric induction of organ regeneration, cancer suppression, and birth defect repair using FDA-approved compounds. The authors describe an 'anatomical compiler' concept — software that translates desired anatomical outcomes into cellular signals — as the long-term vision for regenerative medicine.

For Gates Foundation U.S. Program staff, the relevance of this document is indirect but potentially significant for health innovation strategy discussions. While not directly addressing K-12 education, postsecondary access, or workforce pathways, the document exemplifies emerging science that could inform long-term thinking about biomedical innovation, health equity, and the future of disease treatment — particularly for chronic conditions disproportionately affecting underserved communities. The hepatocyte-lymph node transplantation approach has an active clinical trial (NCT04496479) and is being commercialized through LyGenesis, a company co-founded by co-author Lagasse. This translational pathway from basic science to clinical application may be of interest to program staff tracking health innovation adjacencies to the foundation's broader equity and opportunity agenda.

FILE: 2023 USP Division Summary Memo (Nov.16.2023 Updated).pdf
This November 2023 memo provides a comprehensive annual summary of the Gates Foundation U.S. Program (USP) division, covering progress, challenges, priorities, resource allocation, dependencies, and risks across all strategy teams.

The document opens with a landscape assessment highlighting ongoing COVID-19 aftereffects: K-12 math scores at their lowest since 1990, deteriorating attendance, declining postsecondary enrollment (down ~1M students from pre-pandemic), a growing Some College No Credential (SCNC) population of 40.4M, and declining public confidence in higher education. Racial equity challenges are prominent, including overrepresentation of Black, Latino, and Native American students in the SCNC population and the chilling effects of legislation in Texas and Florida following the SCOTUS admissions ruling. Despite headwinds, digital instructional materials and AI represent key tailwinds, with ~25% of K-12 materials now digital and expected to double by 2025.

Key team highlights include: K-12 launching partnerships with five high-quality math providers (Khan Academy, Zearn, Illustrative Mathematics, Carnegie Learning, Math Nation), awarding 28 AI pilot investments, and developing state-specific plans for California, Florida, New York, and Texas. Postsecondary Success (PS) is pursuing scaled transformation through six intermediaries (nearly 100 MOUs executed) and three National Scale Enterprises (ASU, SNHU, WGU), while advancing AI-embedded courseware and Digital Holistic Student Supports (DHSS). The Pathways team is building credit mobility tools, early college pathways, and advising solutions. The Economic Mobility & Opportunity (EMO) team launched six anchor partners stewarding ~$100M and helped low-income families access $480M in public benefits. USP Data is expanding the Administrative Data Research Facility (ADRF) to 14 states and advancing statewide longitudinal data systems. Early Learning launched R&D for child assessments, and the Assessment Initiative was separated from K-12 into its own initiative. US Charters received CEO approval for a WA state three-year strategy, and the Washington State Initiative (WSI) began full implementation.

On resource allocation, the division proposes a net increase of $234M and 16 FTE over baseline, including $21.2M in rollovers and $212.8M in net incremental requests. Major incremental requests are concentrated in PS (courseware, DHSS, NSEs totaling $187.8M/14 FTE), Assessment Initiative ($15M/1 FTE), and USP Data Analytics ($10M/1 FTE). Significant internal reallocations include $43M from US Charters (winding down the Students with Disabilities BOW) and $84.7M within PS to sharpen focus.

Key risks include lingering COVID effects, political polarization and education politicization, technology adoption concerns, data availability constraints, anchor partner execution challenges (notably National Student Clearinghouse delays), internal grantmaking timing issues, and leadership transitions across Early Learning, Pathways, and USP Data teams in 2024.

FILE: 2023 USP Division Summary Memo.pdf
This November 2022 U.S. Program Division Summary Memo provides a comprehensive overview of progress, priorities, resource allocation, dependencies, and risks across all USP strategy teams for the 2022-23 planning cycle. It was written against a backdrop of historic math score declines, declining postsecondary enrollment, political polarization, and ongoing COVID-19 impacts.

Key programmatic highlights include: K-12 Education's Middle Years Math work showing measurable effects on math knowledge for Black, Latino, and low-income students; Teacher Preparation exceeding 2022 benchmarks in CA and TX; and new math strategy approval secured in September 2022. Economic Mobility & Opportunity (EMO) completed landmark social capital research via Opportunity Insights, unlocked $834M in tax credits through Code for America, and is consolidating from ~65 partners to 25 by 2025 under a new anchor partner model. Postsecondary Success (PS) advanced Intermediaries for Scale investments, digital learning delivery, and National Scale Enterprises equity goals. Pathways launched investments in advising models, credit mobility tools, and 12 regional employer-K12-PS partnerships. USP Data expanded the Postsecondary Data Partnership to 675 institutions and launched the Education-to-Workforce (E-W) Indicator Framework. Early Learning completed Target Product Profiles for child assessments with 40+ suppliers expressing interest. The Washington State Initiative received CEO approval for a new credential attainment strategy targeting Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students.

Resource allocation reflects a proposed 4-year direct grantee support total of ~$2.9B (2023-2026), with K-12 Education as the largest strategy at 40.1% ($1.17B). A net incremental request of $54.6M covers: PS Advising Technology ($7M), Pathways MLE and Accelerated Pathways ($11.8M), USP Data scale and R&D ($28.8M), and Early Learning child assessments ($7M). A $19M rollover request reflects COVID-related slowdowns and strategy transition timing.

Key risks include ongoing COVID impacts on school and partner capacity, political polarization affecting K-12 curriculum work in TX and FL, inadequate federal stimulus for learning recovery, ramp-up and execution risk for newly approved strategies (EMO, K-12, WSI), and staff burnout. Dependencies span NAT collaboration on advocacy and policy, cross-team alignment on the E-W Indicator Framework, and Pathways-PS coordination on dual enrollment and National Scale Enterprises. The memo signals strategic maturation across the division with a shift toward fewer, deeper anchor partnerships, stronger measurement frameworks, and clearer equity goals.

FILE: K-12 - IFI_PRI_10242023.pdf
This document is a summary spreadsheet of Program-Related Investments (PRIs) and Impact Finance Investments (IFIs) active in 2023 under the Gates Foundation U.S. Program's K-12 Education funding strategy. It lists investments with end years of 2023 or later, capturing their names, public descriptions, status, projected end years, and total investment amounts.

Three active PRI investments are documented, all within the K-12 Education strategy area:

1. **Reach Fund II** – A $7,710,000 PRI investment in an education technology seed fund, active through 2027. This is the longest-horizon investment in the set, suggesting an early-stage edtech focus with an extended runway for portfolio company development.

2. **PRI Investment in an Education Technology Equity Fund (2025, $6,000,000)** – Described as supporting an education technology fund focused on the U.S. K-12 market. This limited partnership investment is active through 2025.

3. **PRI Investment in an Education Technology Equity Fund (2025, $15,000,000)** – A larger limited partnership interest PRI, also focused on education technology for the U.S. K-12 market, active through 2025. The formal public description emphasizes its structure as a limited partnership interest program-related investment.

Collectively, these three investments represent a total committed capital of approximately $28,710,000 in the K-12 edtech space via PRI mechanisms. All investments are currently active as of 2023.

The use of PRIs reflects the Foundation's strategy of deploying mission-aligned capital in markets where philanthropic grants alone may be insufficient to drive systemic change — particularly in the education technology sector, where private capital markets can be leveraged to scale promising tools and platforms for K-12 students and educators. By investing in edtech funds (both seed-stage and equity), the Foundation is seeking to catalyze a broader ecosystem of technology-enabled learning solutions aligned with U.S. K-12 needs.

This document is relevant to Gates Foundation staff tracking the foundation's financial instruments portfolio within U.S. K-12 education, particularly those working at the intersection of investment strategy, edtech ecosystem development, and program-related finance. FILE: K-12 Education Presentation Deck 10242023.pptx
This October 2023 K-12 Education Strategy Review presentation was delivered to USP leadership including the President of US Program, directors of Postsecondary Success, Washington State, US Policy & Communications, Assessments Initiative, and other senior leaders. The document outlines the K-12 Education team's strategy, progress, and investment priorities centered on improving math instruction quality to drive Algebra 1 completion among priority students.

The core math classroom vision is built around three pillars: Effective Personalization, Interaction & Communication, and Relevant & Meaningful Application. These are intended to drive motivation, engagement, and persistence, ultimately improving learning outcomes and Algebra 1 proficiency. Key targets include increasing teacher use of high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) from 48% to 60%, increasing the share of priority students with diverse trained teachers from 22% to 30%, and raising the share of students engaged in math class from 43% to 75%.

The strategy's top priority for its first 3-4 years is improving instructional materials quality. The team is pursuing this through three levers: (A) strengthening research and data infrastructure, (B) testing features supporting personalization, interaction, and relevance, and (C) investing in high-quality providers to incorporate validated features and scale reach.

Generative AI is identified as a rapidly accelerating force in the innovation landscape. Specific R&D investments include AI video chatbots based on real teachers, gamified personalized learning paths, large language model-powered AI tutors, spatial/VR learning experiences, AI vision models for grading student work, and speech recognition tools for measuring student engagement. The team is working with 16 partners to build digital infrastructure and collaborating with the National Science Foundation to direct more R&D funding toward priority students.

Five core instructional materials partners are highlighted as 'big bets' where investments are made to improve product quality and scale. All R&D partners are required to share findings with the broader field through publications and biannual convenings. California is noted as a key state opportunity for improving outcomes for priority students. The presentation includes a portfolio dashboard and strategy progress assessment, with notes on cross-cutting and global policy work comprising a portion of the portfolio.

FILE: 02 - K-12 Education Acronym List 10092024.pdf
This document is a reference glossary of acronyms used within the Gates Foundation's K-12 Education program area, dated October 2024 and marked CONFIDENTIAL. It serves as an internal decoding tool for staff navigating strategy documents, grant materials, and program communications related to K-12 education initiatives.

The acronym list spans a wide range of organizational partners, programmatic concepts, and policy frameworks relevant to the U.S. K-12 education landscape. Key thematic clusters include:

**Instructional Quality & Curriculum:** Acronyms such as HQIM (High-quality instructional materials), HQPL (High-quality professional learning), CBPL (Curriculum-Based Professional Learning), IM (Illustrative Mathematics), and OER (Open education resources) reflect the foundation's sustained focus on improving instructional quality through evidence-based materials and teacher support.

**Education Technology & AI:** References to AI2 (Allen Institute for AI), NLP (Natural Language Processing), KAD (Khan Academy District), ISTE, and PERTS signal the program's engagement with ed-tech innovation and AI-enabled learning tools.

**School Improvement & Networks:** NSI (Networks for School Improvement), MTSS (Multi-Tiered System of Support), NIRN (National Implementation Research Network), CI (Continuous improvement), and PNI (Partners for Network Improvement) reflect systems-level improvement strategies.

**Student Milestones & Equity:** Acronyms like 8GOT (Eighth Grade On-Track), CROT (College Ready On-Track), CRSE (Culturally relevant and sustaining education), MLL (Multi-lingual learners), EOS (Equal Opportunity Schools), and FAFSA point to equity-centered approaches and student trajectory monitoring.

**Key Partner Organizations:** The list includes major grantees and partners such as AERDF, ANet, CASEL, CZI, CEMD, TNTP, NASSP, NCLD, TEA, NYCDOE/NYCPS, LAUSD, and YPS — reflecting the geographic and organizational breadth of foundation investments.

**Policy & Data Frameworks:** ESSA, ESSER, CRDC, MCAP, NAEP, SBOE, and RFT point to federal policy, assessment, and accountability structures that contextualize the foundation's work.

**Financial & Grant Terms:** DPI, MOIC, and PRI reflect investment and portfolio management language used in grant and financial reporting contexts.

This reference document does not itself contain strategic decisions or investment narratives, but provides essential vocabulary infrastructure for staff interpreting K-12 program documents. It is particularly useful for onboarding new team members or for cross-team readers engaging with K-12 strategy memos, investment plans, and results frameworks.

This document is a reference glossary of acronyms used within the Gates Foundation's Postsecondary Success (PS) strategy team as of 2024. It serves as an internal terminology guide to support consistent communication across strategy documents, grant materials, and team discussions. The acronyms span institutional partners (e.g., ASU, CMU, SNHU, WGU, UMGC, NAU), higher education associations (AASCU, AIHEC, HBCU, UNCF), federal programs and policy references (FAFSA, TRIO, ED, SCOTUS, CA Prop. 209), and Gates Foundation-specific strategy constructs (SG1/SG2, IFS, SIT, TAS, DHSS, AKP, SNI, NSE, ITA, ICA, TPP, BoW, SoE, UDTS, PDP, EVE). Key strategy concepts encoded in the acronyms include: equitable value (EV, EVE), intermediaries for scale (IFS), supports for institutional transformation (SIT), digital holistic student supports (DHSS), transformation at scale (TAS), developmental education (DevEd), and solution network intermediaries (SNI). The document reflects the breadth of the PS team's portfolio, which encompasses work on college completion, racial equity in higher education, institutional transformation, courseware, data infrastructure (IPEDS, NSC, PDP), and policy engagement. References to HBCU, AIHEC, UNCF, SEAL of Excelencia, and CA Prop. 209 signal the team's attention to equity-centered partnerships and the legal/policy landscape affecting minoritized students. The inclusion of terms like CMEP (Context, Motivation, Engagement, and Persistence), DFWI, and NPS indicates attention to student outcome measurement and learning science frameworks. The Washington State Initiative (WSI) and Growing Inland Achievement (GIA) point to place-based strategy efforts within the broader US Program. This reference document supports staff onboarding, cross-team communication, and consistent use of terminology in strategy reviews and investment documents within the Postsecondary Success portfolio.

FILE: 03 - K-12 Education Strategy Memo 10092024.pdf
This October 2024 confidential strategy memo outlines the Gates Foundation's K-12 Education strategy, which shifted three years prior to focus on mathematics—specifically increasing Algebra 1 proficiency. The 2032 goal is to raise the number of students in CA, FL, TX, and NY demonstrating Algebra 1 proficiency by 10 percentage points on state exams, reaching approximately 1 million additional students. The memo notes stark racial disparities: only 9% of Black students and 14% of Latino students show proficiency on 8th grade NAEP tests.

The strategy operates across three interconnected pillars. Strategy 1 (46% of 2025-27 budget) focuses on improving the quality, comprehensiveness, and appeal of digital instructional materials. Key investments include a Product Quality Framework (PQF) tracking 20 fundamental characteristics and 49 features, R&D Infrastructure (RDI) grants enabling faster product testing, and a pivot of the Modernizing Math body of work toward AI R&D. AI investments include tutoring and teacher coaching platforms with partners Khan Academy, Carnegie Learning, University of Florida, CourseMojo, and Carnegie Mellon University. Public goods investments include open-access datasets (Maryland, Harvard, Stanford, Cornell, MIT), AI benchmarks developed with Allen Institute for AI, and model development partnerships. Gates' $163M in K-12 R&D has been matched with $523M from NSF, CZI, Schmidt Futures, and Walton. Strategy 2 (11% of budget) focuses on aggregating demand and supporting HQIM adoption in state adoption cycles—CA and FL are prioritized for place-based adoption investments, while TX and NY focus on enactment. CA Senate Bill 153 provides $20M for high-quality math instruction; FL presents political challenges given conservative leadership and prior rejection of math textbooks. Strategy 3 (22% of budget) supports high-quality curriculum enactment through aligned professional learning. TX House Bill 1605 allocates $324M annually to districts adopting SBOE-approved materials. NYC is a key enactment site following its mandate of Illustrative Mathematics.

The overall portfolio is rated slightly off track due to slower-than-expected commercialization of R&D insights, delayed state plan development, and unfinalized investments with Khan Academy and Kiddom. The memo requests leadership alignment on overall strategy and measurement, commercialization best practices, AI full-stack player risks, and policy options. Emerging policy priorities include defining HQIM quality standards, incentivizing adoption through procurement and financial mechanisms, requiring evidence-based math interventions, advancing opt-out advanced coursework enrollment policies, and exploring AI policy frameworks. The memo also references ongoing work on high school math pathways including statistics, data science, and calculus courses aligned to the broader USP Math vision.

FILE: 03 - Postsecondary Success Strategy Memo 10092024.pdf
This confidential strategy memo from the Gates Foundation's Postsecondary (PS) team provides a comprehensive update on the PS strategy as of late 2024. The memo addresses declining public confidence in higher education value, rising student debt ($1.75T), and persistent equity gaps in completion rates by race and income. It frames the next phase of reform as moving beyond access and completion toward equitable value — economic mobility for students and public benefits from broader credential attainment.

The strategy operates through two primary goals. First, Generate Proof of Scaled Transformation, pursued via (A) six Intermediary organizations working with 200+ institutions on evidence-based practices targeting credit completion, first-year retention, and degree completion, and (B) National Scale Enterprises (NSEs) — ASU, SNHU, WGU, UMGC, and Purdue Global — guided toward equitable growth and R&D innovation. The Intermediary approach meets expectations, with 174 signed MOUs, 168 institutions completing the Institutional Transformation Assessment (ITA), and 145 requesting services. NSEs also meet expectations, showing enrollment and degree growth for Black, Latino, and Indigenous students exceeding overall rates.

Second, Develop, Share, and Scale Public Goods and Solutions encompasses three areas: Courseware (exceeds expectations), including successful launches of Lumen Stats and REAL Chem with strong faculty adoption and student success rates, and preparation for a Math Big Bet operating entity; Digital Holistic Student Supports (DHSS, slightly below expectations), experiencing minor delays after restructuring partner roles between Ed Advancement and Canyon GBS for the System of Engagement and Unified Data and Technology System; and Transformation Resources, including the ITA, DevEd reform, Guided Pathways framework, and PS Admissions work — the last of which is being sunset as an independent body of work.

Key external context includes FAFSA rollout disruptions causing a 6.5pp decline in completions, anti-DEI legislation in 28 states threatening enrollment for focus students, and growing AI adoption in higher education (49% student use, 22% faculty use). The memo highlights co-funder leverage, including $12M on ACE Carnegie Classifications, $124M from Blue Meridian to UNCF, and $38M from Lilly to AIHEC.

2025 priorities include advancing the Equitable Value reform agenda, launching the Math Big Bet operating entity and MVP Calculus course, completing DHSS anchor partner investments, evaluating the Intermediary for Scale approach, expanding NSE learnings, and integrating inclusive excellence into operations. The memo connects to broader USP Division work in Pathways, Math Pathways, CMEP, and AI.

FILE: 04 - K-12 Education Presentation Deck 10092024.pdf
This October 2024 Strategy Review presentation covers the Gates Foundation's K-12 Education strategy, focused on improving math outcomes for Black students, Latino students, and students from low-income backgrounds ('priority students'). The overarching goal is for priority students to demonstrate proficiency in Algebra 1 by the end of 9th grade.

The strategy operates along two primary pillars: (1) improving the quality of digital instructional materials and teacher supports, and (2) supporting effective adoption and use of those materials. Classrooms and tools are designed to be Personalized, Interactive, and Relevant (PIR), driving student motivation, engagement, and persistence.

The external context highlights historically low math achievement post-pandemic (2022 NAEP), with proficiency lagging in Algebra 1 end-of-course exams across focus states (FL: 53%, NY: 65%, TX: 45%). Low-quality materials remain pervasive, with teachers frequently relying on self-created content and Teachers Pay Teachers. ESSER funding expiration poses additional headwinds.

The portfolio dashboard shows most bodies of work rated 'Slightly Below Expectations,' with focus state enactment work (CA, FL, NY, TX) rated 'Too Early to Tell.' Key bodies of work include Instructional Materials R&D, MLE for Improve Quality, Demand Aggregation & Adoption, and state-specific Enactment investments.

R&D priorities include feature testing with solutions partners (Carnegie Learning, Khan Academy, Kiddom, Math Nation, Zearn, Imagine Learning), convening partners around measurement, and AI-driven tutoring investments. Notable findings include: Carnegie Learning's AI feedback tools yielded a 20% increase in teachers' use of focusing questions; CU Boulder/Saga Education high-dosage tutoring showed 35% weighted average learning improvement; University of Florida's AI chatbot showed 1.56x math knowledge growth.

Partner deep dives cover Khan Academy (rated off-track due to unwillingness to shift from supplemental to integrated platform; 53M users; Khanmigo AI advancing) and Carnegie Learning (rated on-track; leading AI cognitive model; strong TX presence; expanding to FL and CA). A Product Quality Framework (PQF) benchmarks partners across 28 features.

State-level adoption goals target 28–61% of students in districts adopting HQIM by 2027, with enactment goals focused on increases in curriculum-based professional learning (CBPL). Investment approaches vary by state: CA focuses on awareness and use networks; FL on momentum-building pre-adoption; NY on NYC IM implementation; TX on teacher use networks and TEA support.

Discussion topics include strategy and measurement plan alignment, commercialization of innovation, concerns about AI-enabled full-stack players like Khan Academy, and policy options to support the math strategy.

FILE: 04 - Postsecondary Success Presentation Deck 10092024.pdf
This October 2024 strategy review presentation covers the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation's Postsecondary Success (PS) strategy, providing an overview of higher education context, portfolio performance, and deep dives into key Bodies of Work (BoWs). The strategy aims to increase attainment and close opportunity gaps for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students through two complementary paths: Transformation at Scale (TAS) and National Scale Enterprises (NSEs), together reaching approximately 10% of U.S. undergraduates across roughly 200 institutions.

The higher education context highlights significant headwinds including declining enrollments (down 7% from 2018-2022 nationally), shrinking budgets, rising costs, FAFSA rollout issues, anti-DEI movements, and stagnant graduation rates. The national six-year completion rate stands at 62.2%, essentially flat for three cohorts, with the largest declines among Black and Indigenous students. First-year retention nationally reached 68.2%, the highest in a decade.

The PS strategy's FY24-27 budget is $216M (32% of USP allocation), structured across five primary BoWs: (1) Develop and Scale Courseware ($140M projected), focused on new introductory Math courseware (Objective A) and scaling existing Statistics and Chemistry courseware (Objective B), with a key presidential decision due March 2026 on future investment levels; (2) Develop and Scale Digital Holistic Student Supports (DHSS, $97M), developing a Unified Data Tech System and System of Engagement bundle for lighthouse institutions, with director and presidential decisions in 2025 and 2027; (3) Transformation at Scale ($153.2M), supporting intermediaries and 200+ institutions with evidence-based practices targeting improvements in credit completion ratios, first-year retention, and six-year completion rates; (4) Shape National Scale Enterprises ($32.8M), steering five NSEs including Arizona State University, Western Governors University, Southern New Hampshire University, Purdue Global, and University of Maryland Global Campus toward equitable outcomes and accelerating R&D through an Innovation Fund; and (5) Refine and Sustain Equitable Value Reform ($13M), advancing equitable value as a driver of public policy and institutional behavior through public goods including the Equitable Value Explorer and Equitable Value Framework.

Portfolio performance ratings show the Courseware and NSE BoWs rated as 'Exceeds' on execution, while DHSS and TAS intermediary work are rated 'Slightly Below' or 'Meets.' The strategy leverages three key advantages: data and technology, influential voice and convening power, and the ability to connect R&D and delivery ecosystems. Anticipated tailwinds include increasing persistence rates and advances in Generative AI. Improved completion rates are projected to yield approximately 171,900 additional degrees above baseline trends from 2026-2029, including 27,800 for Black students and 37,100 for Latino students across TAS and NSE institutions combined.

FILE: 04162024 K-12 Courseware Feature Map Appendix A Placemat Product Quality Framework.pdf
This document is part of the Gates Foundation K-12 Product Quality Framework and serves as Appendix A to a courseware feature map. It presents a two-page placemat covering 'Preferred Features' for K-12 digital math products — first as an overview of feature categories and their descriptions, then as a comparative ratings table scoring several leading edtech products against those features.

Page one outlines ten feature categories considered desirable (but not foundational requirements) for high-quality K-12 digital courseware: Digital Lessons, Practice (e.g., homework), Assessment, Student Collaboration Tools, Data Dashboard, Teacher Instructional Tools, Professional Development Library, Technical Supports, Personalization, and Exploratory Technologies. Each category includes a description and a list of specific features. The document hypothesizes that integration of these features will increase student motivation and engagement, save teachers time, and increase student success, while acknowledging that the evidence base is sparse and effect sizes are modest compared to foundational requirements.

Page two provides a comparative scoring rubric (0=Not present, 1=Developing, 2=Present, 3=Exemplary) and applies it across six products: Carnegie Learning Middle School Math Solution (MATHia + MATHstream), Zearn, Math Nation, Illustrative Math Kiddom, Illustrative Math Imagine Learning, and Khan Academy District Offering & Khanmigo. Products are rated across all features within each category.

Key findings from the ratings include: Carnegie Learning scores highly on adaptive feedback, real-time monitoring, professional development resources, and AI enhancements. Zearn performs strongly on multimedia, interactive manipulatives, and real-time feedback. Khan Academy's district offering shows strength in lesson plans and AI enhancements via Khanmigo. Math Nation and Illustrative Math variants show more moderate or developing scores across most preferred features, particularly in collaboration tools and personalization.

Strategically, this document connects to the Gates Foundation's U.S. Program efforts to identify and promote high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) in K-12 math. It supports the foundation's work to evaluate edtech products not just on content quality but on their digital platform features, teacher support infrastructure, and capacity for personalization and data use. The framework is explicitly presented as evolving — features will be updated as technology advances and testing occurs. This comparative tool is likely used to inform grantmaking, product partnerships, or recommendations to districts adopting digital math curricula.

FILE: 04162024 K-12 Courseware Feature Map Presentation.pptx
This April 2024 presentation was delivered to Gates Foundation US Program K-12 Education leadership, including USP President Allan Golston and senior K-12 staff, along with Carnegie Learning executives (CEO Barry Malkin, Chief Strategy Officer Peter Lacasse, and Co-Founder Steve Ritter). The session also reviewed products from Kiddom and Math Nation. The presentation centers on a K-12 Courseware Feature Map and Product Quality Framework designed to guide the Foundation's investments in digital math instructional materials.

The Foundation's math education vision focuses on three transformative concepts: Effective Personalization (differentiated instruction and feedback), Interaction and Communication (student-led mathematical discourse), and Motivation, Engagement, and Persistence (gamified and relatable learning). Technology is positioned as a tool to relieve teacher time burdens — noting teachers work ~54 hours per week with under 50% spent on direct instruction — by enabling real-time data, AI tutoring, and personalized content delivery.

The Product Quality Framework establishes two tiers: Foundational Requirements (grade-level standards-aligned content, English learner language supports, interoperability, and R&D infrastructure) and Preferred Features (both student-facing, such as multimedia video, and teacher-facing, such as AI-assisted lesson planning). The Foundation states it will not invest in products that do not meet foundational requirements, and will prioritize features hypothesized to increase student motivation, engagement, and outcomes.

Coherence across digital products is framed around four dimensions: Pedagogy, Content, Data, and User Experience, all of which must be aligned across core, supplemental, assessment, and practice products.

Carnegie Learning's portfolio was demoed against the preferred features framework. The company has 20+ years of research, over 250K students using MATHia in BMGF priority states, a Texas Education Agency endorsement for grades 6–12, and active research partnerships including a RAND/DOE study. Kiddom and Math Nation were also demoed and mapped against the same feature framework. The session concluded with open discussion. This document is directly relevant to USP K-12 strategy around high-quality instructional materials (HQIM), technology-enabled learning, and evidence-based investment in digital courseware platforms.

Interaction and Communication​

Motivation, Engagement and Persistence

Meaningful Application​
Connect math content to students’ interests and assets.
Students learn at their own pace depending on learning proficiencies and are more likely to stage engaged and motivated

Real-time data for teachers indicates when a student or group of students may need redirecting or reteaching

FILE: 05 - K-12 Education Investment Narrative 10092024.pdf
This confidential investment narrative outlines the K-12 Education team's 2025 Annual Plan (AP) budget allocation and strategic priorities for the 2025–2028 period. The strategy centers on mathematics and is organized around three core investment pillars: (1) Improving the Quality of High-Quality Instructional Materials (HQIM) and teacher supports, (2) Increasing awareness and adoption of high-quality materials (HQM), and (3) Supporting effective enactment by teachers through aligned professional learning. Additional investment areas include Networks for School Improvement (NSI), Cross-Cutting Supports, and Global Policy and Advocacy.

The total four-year budget is approximately $1.085 billion (revised to $1.173.8M in the comparative table), with the largest share — 46% ($500.7M) — directed toward improving the quality of HQIM and teacher supports. Awareness and adoption of HQM accounts for 11% ($118.5M), effective use of HQM 23% ($246.3M), NSI 5% ($49M), Cross-Cutting Supports 8% ($83.2M), and Global Policy and Advocacy 8% ($88M).

A key strategic shift noted in the document is the team's move over the past three years away from delivery-focused portfolios (notably NSI) toward R&D, digital products, and curriculum — now representing 46% of the portfolio. The document acknowledges the rapidly evolving AI-in-education landscape as a factor that may require significant trade-offs or additional resources over the next two years.

The 2025–28 budget revisions reflect several structural decisions: the standalone Capacity Building body of work was closed in 2024, with its funding redistributed into the Scale Quality Products and Aligned Services (+$4.1M) and System and Stakeholder Engagement (+$3.6M) bodies of work. The Effective Use portfolio saw a minor administrative reorganization, realigning general funds into four state-specific enactment bodies of work for California, Texas, Florida, and New York. NSI funding decreases by $13M over four years to support a newly co-funded body of work with the Assessments team focused on 9th-grade on-track data capabilities. Cross-Cutting Supports decrease by $7.6M due to the closure of the capacity building body of work.

This document is relevant to USP strategy as it reflects the foundation's evolving approach to systemic math education improvement, the integration of digital and AI-enabled tools, and a shift toward more concentrated, evidence-based investment in curriculum quality and adoption rather than broad school improvement networks.

FILE: 05 - Postsecondary Success Investment Narrative 10092024.pdf
This document is the Postsecondary Success Investment Plan Narrative for FY 2025-2028, proposing a total budget of $655.5M. It outlines several key shifts from the previous plan, primarily driven by more refined implementation timelines, updated costing models, and changes in investment mechanisms for certain bodies of work.

The most significant changes involve the Tech-Enabled Solutions portfolio, which encompasses two major bodies of work: Develop & Scale Courseware Solution and Develop & Scale Digital Holistic Student Supports (DHSS). For Courseware, $10M is shifted from 2025 into 2026 and 2027. For DHSS, $15M is shifted from 2025 and 2026 into 2027. Notably, DHSS investments have transitioned from grant to contract mechanisms, affecting payment timing. The overall envelope for Tech-Enabled Solutions remains unchanged, representing the largest sub-portfolio at 37% ($239.5M) of total investment.

The Intermediaries for Scale portfolio also reflects a shift: budget was pulled forward into 2024 to make larger payments to key partners, reducing the 2025-2028 allocation. This portfolio totals $135.1M (21%) over the four-year period.

Two budget reductions are noted: $3.0M allocated in 2028 to the US Programs central reserve, and $14M reallocated from the Policy & Advocacy budget (within Strengthen Impact Through Critical Enablers) to the Pathways team to support policy alignment for scaling pathways work. This reallocation is treated as a line-item reduction of ($3.5M) per year across 2025-2028.

The budget allocation by strategic goal is: 43% ($278.9M) toward SG2: Develop and Scale Solutions and Public Goods; 32% ($207.0M) toward SG1: Generate Proof of Scaled Transformation; and 26% net (after Pathways reallocation) toward Strengthen Impact Through Critical Enablers ($183.5M gross, 28%).

Year-over-year, the budget increases by $17M (10%) from 2024 to 2025, decreases by $7M (4%) in 2026, remains relatively flat in 2027, and decreases by $24M (14%) in 2028. The team expresses confidence in execution capacity given these adjusted timelines and mechanisms.

This document connects to the broader USP Postsecondary strategy by demonstrating how the team is managing large, complex investments in technology-enabled postsecondary supports and courseware, while also channeling resources toward policy alignment with the Pathways team — reflecting cross-portfolio coordination within US Programs.

FILE: 06 - K-12 Education Investment Plan 10092024.pdf
This document is the U.S. Program K-12 Education Investment Plan, dated September 12, 2024, presenting financial resource allocations for the 2025 planning cycle. It compares the previous financial resource plan (2024–2027) with the updated 2025 resource plan (2025–2027, with 2028 to be finalized after board endorsement), and provides a four-year outlook showing allocations versus existing committed funding.

The previous plan (2024–2027) totaled $1,095.6M across six body-of-work groups, with the largest shares going to 'Improve the Quality and Scale of Instructional Materials' (42.8%, $469.3M) and 'Support Quality Enactment in Classrooms' (29.4%, $322.0M). The 2025 resource plan restructures these groups, notably renaming and refining categories: 'Improve the Quality of Instructional Materials and Teacher Supports' becomes the largest group at 46.1% ($375.9M over three years), and 'Support the Effective Use of High-Quality Materials' replaces the classroom enactment group at 22.5% ($183.3M). A new group, 'Networks for School Improvement,' appears at 4.8% ($39.0M), and 'Other Body of Work Types' now carries $25.7M (3.2%), reflecting a reorganization of strategic priorities.

The difference table confirms the structural shift: new body-of-work group categories account for a ($229.6M) offset against legacy group totals, netting to zero change in total annual spend, confirming budget neutrality in the restructuring. Annual totals remain approximately $270–$274M across 2025–2027.

The four-year outlook section compares 4-year allocations against existing committed funding. For 2025, $146.7M is committed against a $273.7M allocation (53.6% committed). Commitment rates drop sharply for outer years: 25.9% ($70.1M) for 2026, 9.7% ($26.1M) for 2027, and $10.3M for 2028 (percentage not calculated pending board targets). This pattern reflects normal multi-year grant pipelining, with significant uncommitted headroom in 2026–2028 for new investments.

Notably, 'Networks for School Improvement' is over-committed at 124.7% for 2025 and 121.2% for 2026, signaling existing grants exceed current allocation targets and may require reconciliation. 'Increase the Awareness and Adoption of High-Quality Materials' is also highly committed at 71.6% for 2025 and 67.9% for 2026.

Strategically, this plan reflects the K-12 team's sharpened focus on high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) and teacher supports as the primary investment thesis, with meaningful allocations toward effective classroom use, policy and advocacy, and school improvement networks. The restructuring of body-of-work groups suggests an evolving theory of change that integrates teacher professional learning more explicitly with materials quality.

FILE: 06 - Postsecondary Success Investment Plan 10092024.pdf
This confidential Investment Plan document, dated September 13, 2024, provides a financial resource planning comparison for the Gates Foundation U.S. Program: Postsecondary Success initiative. It compares the Previous Financial Resource Plan (2024–2027) against the 2025 Resource Plan (which includes currently proposed incremental requests), and shows the resulting differences across five body-of-work groups.

The Previous Financial Resource Plan (2024–2027) totaled $678.9M across four years, with SG2: Develop and Scale Solutions and Public Goods representing the largest share at 41.0% ($278.1M), followed by SG1: Generate Proof of Scaled Transformation at 31.8% ($216.2M), Strengthen Impact Through Critical Enablers at 13.7% ($92.8M), and Global Policy & Advocacy at 13.5% ($91.8M).

The 2025 Resource Plan (2025–2027, with 2028 pending board endorsement) totals $510.5M over three years. SG2 remains the largest category at 44.6% ($227.9M), followed by SG1 at 30.2% ($153.9M), Strengthen Impact Through Critical Enablers at 13.8% ($70.4M), and Global Policy & Advocacy at 11.4% ($58.4M). Year-over-year differences show significant reductions in 2025 (total -$22.1M) and 2026 (-$5.9M), with a net positive shift in 2027 (+$17.4M), driven primarily by changes in SG2 allocations.

The second page presents a 4-Year Outlook (4-YO) Allocations compared to Existing Commitments for payout years 2025–2028. Total 4-YO allocations are $174.3M (2025), $167.4M (2026), and $168.8M (2027), with 2028 pending. Committed funds as a percentage of allocation decline steeply over time: 29.8% committed for 2025 ($51.9M), 14.9% for 2026 ($24.9M), and 2.3% for 2027 ($3.8M), indicating substantial uncommitted resources available for future grantmaking decisions.

At the body-of-work level, SG1 has the highest commitment rate for 2025 at 52.6% ($25.1M committed of $47.8M allocated), while SG2 has the lowest at 18.6% ($15.3M of $82.3M). Global Policy & Advocacy shows 26.3% committed for 2025. These figures reflect the pipeline of active and planned grants versus available allocation headroom.

Strategically, this document is relevant for portfolio management decisions within USP Postsecondary Success, particularly for grant officers and strategy leads assessing pacing, reallocation opportunities, and planning for new investments through 2028. The note that 2028 figures will be updated post-board endorsement signals an active strategic planning cycle.

FILE: 07 - K-12 Education Inv Scoring Top5-Bottom5 10092024.pdf
This document is a confidential Gates Foundation U.S. Program report dated September 6, 2024, presenting the top five and bottom five performing investments within the K-12 Education strategy. All investments use a 'Managed and Funded' model.

Top Performing Investments: (1) INV-010487, The California Education Partners — $12M investment in a Network for School Improvement focused on improving on-track rates for Black, Latino, and low-income students in 8th and 9th grade. Scored 'Exceeds Expectations' in both impact and execution in 2024, with reinvestment confirmed. Key success factors include a strong theory of improvement, data-driven decision-making, and sustainable district relationships. (2) INV-046922, EdLight PBC — $3M research partnership developing an AI vision model to diagnose math misconceptions in student written work. Scored 'Exceeds Expectations' on impact in 2024; cost remains a challenge. (3) INV-026574, Goodwin Simon Strategic Research — $1.63M math identity formation research project generating messaging guides, webinars, and a website reaching 2,000+ unique users. Scored 'Exceeds Expectations' on both dimensions in 2024; follow-on investment being developed. (4) INV-055364, Council of Chief State School Officers — $5.67M investment in HQIM and professional development networks (Phase 4), focused on helping SEAs build capacity for curriculum-based professional learning. Scored 'Meets Expectations' in its first year. (5) INV-055177, English Learners Success Forum — $8.2M investment to improve multilingual learner access to quality math instructional materials in California and Texas. Scored 'Meets Expectations' in 2024 after early 'Too Early To Tell' ratings.

Bottom Performing Investments: (1) INV-018505, Education Analytics — $4.84M NSI data and analytics support investment declining in utilization; scored 'Below Expectations' in 2024, will not reinvest. (2) INV-031956, National Opinion Research Center — $2.52M OER math content quality research partnership that failed to establish stable district partnerships and delivered a reduced MVP; will not reinvest. (3) INV-017562, University of Southern California — $249K Teacher Education Racial Equity Academy that did not meet its goals of recruiting educator teams or producing evidence of applied learning; will not reinvest. (4) INV-003665, Arizona State University Foundation — $8.67M Arizona NSI and postsecondary alignment investment hampered by leadership turnover and inconsistent project management; consistently below expectations, will not reinvest. (5) INV-003940, Camelback Ventures — $999K pre-seed pilot for entrepreneurs of color in edtech; mixed results across three ventures, only one showed measurable student impact; will not reinvest.

Strategically, the document reflects USP K-12 priorities around HQIM adoption, Networks for School Improvement, math quality, and equity-centered instruction. The top performers generally demonstrate strong partner relationships, data use, and scalable models, while bottom performers faced challenges with partnership development, leadership stability, and execution fidelity.

FILE: 07 - Postsecondary Success Inv Scoring Top5-Bottom5 10092024.pdf
This confidential Gates Foundation document, dated July 27, 2024, presents the top five and bottom five performing investments within the U.S. Program's Postsecondary Success strategy. Top Performing Investments: Western Governors University (INV-027955, $4.9M) is advancing equity-centered R&D, exceeding Black and Indigenous enrollment targets through tools like the Transcript Broker Service. Trustees of Indiana University (INV-035235, $171K) completed an ITA rubric review with positive outputs but recurring execution delays required ongoing managing team intervention, resulting in no reinvestment.

Across the portfolio, the document highlights recurring themes: the importance of leadership stability at grantee organizations, the centrality of racial equity (with focus on Black, Latino, and Indigenous students), and the strategic tension between supporting incumbent partners versus developing proprietary solutions. Investments are funded under SG1 (Generate Proof of Scaled Transformation) and SG2 (Develop and Scale Solutions and Public Goods) funding streams.

Collectively, these follow-ups reflect the K-12 Education strategy's emphasis on evidence-based development of high-quality digital instructional materials, rigorous R&D infrastructure through partnerships like Digital Promise, and a deliberate, adaptive approach to AI integration in educational products. The document demonstrates accountability to foundation leadership and progress toward a comprehensive, digitally-enabled instructional materials ecosystem.

FILE: 102824 Pathways Strategy Update Presentation Deck.pdf
This October 2024 presentation deck outlines the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation's updated Pathways strategy for 2025–2028, presented to US Program leadership including the President and Chief Strategy Officer. The document articulates a transition from discrete solution investments to an integrated pathways approach designed to improve postsecondary enrollment, completion, and ROI for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students.

The core strategic framework — the Pathways Integrated Framework (IF) — combines High-Quality Advising with Accelerated Course Sequences (Dual Enrollment, Math Pathways, Credit Mobility), Career-Connected Learning, and Mindset/Relationship building. The minimum viable set requires Advising and Dual Enrollment as entry points. The document uses Texas as a data anchor, showing that only 1 in 4 eighth graders complete a postsecondary credential, with stark disparities for Black (1 in 6) and Latino (1 in 5) students.

The strategy is organized around three portfolios: (1) Pathways Integrated Framework and Solutions — refining solutions via AI and data-enabled technology and creating public goods; (2) Proof Point Delivery — generating 15–20 diverse implementation sites in California, Texas, and Washington to validate integrated framework impact on student KPIs including FAFSA completion, dual enrollment, college application, immediate PS enrollment, and credit transfer; and (3) Enablers — building a Strong National Pathways Field with shared definitions, infrastructure, and aligned funding, including a co-funded entity seeded by a $10M national funder commitment.

Key solution deep-dives include: Advising, where a two-horizon AI strategy (AI-enabled now; AI-centric future) aims to address the 385:1 student-to-counselor ratio and scale quality advising; Dual Enrollment, where 50% inequity in access for Black and Hispanic students drives investment in public goods and quality standards aligned to the DEEP framework; Math Pathways, focused on modernizing Algebra II and expanding quantitative literacy aligned across K-12 and PS; and Credit Mobility Technology, targeting interoperability among three CMT platforms to recover the $3B per cohort lost to credit transfer inefficiencies.

The Texas State Exemplar proof point involves the Texas Impact Network supporting four regional intermediaries across 8 colleges, 19 districts, and 16,632 students annually, leveraging HB3 and HB8 public funding. A sample performance dashboard tracks partnership strength, IF implementation maturity, and student outcome metrics across three student cohorts through 2029.

The budget proposal presents three scenarios for the 2025–28 period: Core ($270M), Low ($233.9M), and High ($284.5M). The Core scenario includes a $124M incremental request, with 55% allocated to solutions (emphasizing upstream R&D and tech from ~40% to ~75% of solutions spend), 20% to proof points, 20% to enablers, and 5% to strategy and learning. All scenarios include $35M in performance-based unlock funding. Data investments (~$25M+) span all three portfolios, supporting P20W data systems, advising platforms, and research ecosystems in focus states.
