FILE: 102824 Pathways Strategy Update_Appendix C.pptx
This document is Appendix C of the October 2024 Pathways Strategy Update, presenting detailed outcomes, targets, and milestones across the Pathways bodies of work (BoW). It covers three primary areas: (1) Strong National Pathways Field outcomes, (2) Proof Point Portfolio targets, and (3) Solutions and Integrated Framework phases including Credit Mobility Tools (CMTs) and CRED.

For the Strong National Pathways Field (Strategy Outcome #3), the document outlines 2028 targets focused on field alignment around a shared North Star and Integrated Framework, common metrics, equity principles, a unified field agenda, increased aligned funding, and unsubsidized demand for shared public goods.

The Proof Point Portfolio section references targets across Texas, Washington (specifically Southwest Washington/ESD 112), and Innovative Delivery Models, to be refined with partners by June 2025. It introduces rubrics for measuring K-12/postsecondary partnership strength, data capacity, and backbone infrastructure, with annual targets tracking district progress toward defined thresholds.

The Solutions and Integrated Framework section maps R&D cycles across phases (Discovery, Prototype, Testing/Validation, Targeted Release, General Launch) and applies them to key BoW areas including advising, dual enrollment, credit mobility, math pathways, and AI-enabled tools. Horizon 1 focuses on place-based AI-enabled solutions; Horizon 2 on AI-centric approaches.

For Credit Mobility Tools (CMTs), the document details Phase 2 (Testing & Validation) and Phases 3–4 (Targeted Release through General Launch) milestones and outcomes. Key milestones include: onboarding state systems to Universal Transfer Explorer (Ithaka), AI-Assisted Credit Articulation Platform (CAHL/NASH), and ASU's TLN and Triangulator tools by Q4 2025. Outcomes track CMT adoption rates, API integration, student access, and student use growth, with targets to be set by Q2 2025. Efficacy targets include increases in credit evaluations completed, transfer volume, and credentials awarded.

The CRED section (Course Reference and Equivalency Database) outlines a phased development roadmap: completing a scenario/recommendation report (Q1 2025), identifying a governance plan for an independent open-access database (Q2 2025), partner selection (Q3 2025), MVP launch (Q4 2026), and Beta launch (Q1 2028). Outcomes include interoperability targets, data onboarding from at least 20 institutions, and improving credit evaluation and transfer efficacy metrics year-over-year.

This appendix connects directly to USP's Pathways strategy by operationalizing the theory of change through measurable milestones, field-building investments, and technology infrastructure that supports equitable credit mobility and transfer outcomes for target student populations.

FILE: 102824 Pathways Strategy Update_Appendix D.pdf
This October 2024 presentation (Appendix D to a Pathways Strategy Update) outlines the Gates Foundation's strategy for investing in technology-enabled advising to improve postsecondary (PS) outcomes for students. It is structured around a nine-component 'Desired Student Experience' (DSE) framework for high-quality advising, covering dedicated CCR time, support from caring adults, PS options guidance, career and financial aid navigation, identity formation, SEL, and academic readiness.

The document establishes that quality advising drives a 1.4x lift in PS enrollment and generates an estimated $3.8B in additional lifetime earnings, but is difficult to deliver at scale given an average student-to-counselor ratio of 385:1 and post-pandemic budget pressures on tech-enabled solutions.

The strategy proposes investment across two horizons: Horizon 1 (AI-Enabled) focuses on building a place-based exemplar of high-quality advising — initially in New York City with ~80K high school students — using R&D partnerships with CCR platform players (Xello, OverGrad, SCOIR, OneGoal) to make incremental AI improvements across five priority areas: more capacity for caring adults, data on PS options, cost and financial aid tools, streamlined PS application processes, and improved visibility into advising delivery. Horizon 2 (AI-Centric) envisions a transformative future where students are more self-sufficient through integration of multiple data sources, agentic AI workflows, hyper-personalized PS recommendations, and automated application support.

Foundational enablers cut across both horizons and include: defining high-quality advising (anchored in Washington State), developing measurement approaches (piloted with OneGoal in WA and NYC), establishing state-level enabling conditions (via NCAN and other partners), and building high-quality data repositories for non-degree pathways, student outcomes, and program value (including Texas).

The document identifies three market scenarios for the AI-centric future: (1) LLM providers expand to deliver full CCR functionality; (2) existing CCR players move toward AI-centricity; or (3) dominant enterprise players (e.g., PowerSchool, Google Classroom) dominate the market. The Foundation's role differs by scenario but consistently involves public goods development, R&D partnerships, and equity benchmarking.

The budget for the core scenario is $48M, broken down as: $9.5M in foundational enablers, $8.5M in high-quality data development, $20M for Horizon 1 place-based R&D, and $10M for Horizon 2 learning agenda. An additional $25M in contingent funding is available to 'go big' on AI-centric development pending 18-month decision gates around articulated vision, partner match, field endorsement, data availability, and technology benchmarks. Total potential investment reaches $73M.

FILE: 2024-25 USP Division Resource Memo.pdf
This November 2024 memo outlines the U.S. Program (USP) division's proposed resource allocations for the 2025-2028 planning cycle, totaling $3,306.8M across all strategy teams. It reflects four years of strategic shifts and ~$675M in prior trade-offs, with 22 priority investment areas covering approximately 68% of USP managed payout. Resources are organized across four key drivers: Instructional Design R&D and Solutions ($842.6M, 29%), Advising and Navigational Supports ($425.9M, 15%), Scaled Use/Impact ($563.1M, 19%), and Education to Workforce Data ($132.4M, 5%).

The memo details strategy-level allocations and trade-offs: K-12 Education focuses on AI-enabled instructional materials and HQIM adoption in Texas, California, New York, and Florida, with Khan Academy and partners like Carnegie Learning and Zearn as key investments. Postsecondary Success maintains stable resourcing, reallocating $14M to Pathways and $3M to USP Reserves. Early Learning is emerging from its learning phase and submitting a Leadership Directed Allocation (LDA) request for $78.4M and 2 FTE to invest in child assessments, PreK curricula, K-12 curriculum partnerships, and AI-enabled discovery tools. Pathways is proposing a significant expansion with a $124M incremental request and novel performance-based funding unlock mechanisms to support AI-driven credit mobility and advising at national scale. Economic Mobility & Opportunity reallocates $42.6M from the discontinued Mobility Alliance to the Make Lives Better Now digital navigator platform. USP Data is emphasizing stability ahead of a 2025 strategy refresh and a planned 2026 LDA request.

The memo identifies $118.6M and 3 FTE in internal trade-offs for 2025. Two LDA requests are submitted in 2025 Annual Planning (Early Learning and Pathways), with a third planned for USP Data in 2026. The division holds $29.9M in reserves for emergent needs. Key risks include data access challenges, AI adoption barriers, political and economic headwinds in focus states, leadership transitions in OOP/Data/Pathways, and lingering COVID-19 impacts on K-12 attendance and postsecondary enrollment. Critical dependencies include the North America Team (NAT) for policy and communications, FP&A and HR for financial stewardship and staffing, and cross-team collaboration on credit mobility, data infrastructure, and public goods adoption. K-12 Instructional Design and Solutions remains a watch item for potential future incremental resource needs.

FILE: K-12 Education Meeting Notes 10092024.pdf
These are confidential co-chair meeting notes from the K-12 Strategy Review held on October 9, 2024, attended by Bill Gates, Mark Suzman, and a large cross-functional team including K-12 leadership and program staff. The meeting covered follow-up action items, key strategic takeaways from co-chairs, and broader discussion topics related to the foundation's K-12 education portfolio.

On AI and product R&D: Bill Gates expressed optimism about AI progress in education, specifically citing Allen AI's Molmo model as an example of technology capable of listening into classrooms and making sense of student and teacher experiences. There was broad support for building AI platforms and datasets tailored to the target student population. Given the scale of foundation investment in product R&D, Bill emphasized the need for high expectations and measurable progress from these investments. The team also flagged a need to assess K-12 product partners' AI strategies and evaluate whether client-side models are sufficient for partner use cases.

On Khan Academy: Bill noted the strategic value of Khan emphasizing its flexibility as a supplement to different core curricula while identifying which high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) are most compatible with its offerings. An e-mail exchange prior to the meeting explored the overlapping and distinct roles of Khan Academy and Google Classroom, including their evolving features and approaches to integrating instructional content and AI tools.

On adoption targets and focus states: Mark Suzman questioned whether the foundation's adoption targets in focus states were sufficiently ambitious, particularly in Texas given the likelihood of partner adoption there. The meeting surfaced that strategies vary meaningfully by state: California and Florida are focused on preparing districts for adoption; Texas is concentrating on enactment driven by regulatory incentives; and New York, lacking a formal adoption timeline, is focused on district-level implementation of HQIM.

On NAT and state policy: The Networks and Advocacy Team (NAT) presented their ongoing work using state policy levers to support HQIM adoption and curriculum-based professional learning in focus states. Co-chairs requested greater clarity on how NAT grants (including NAC) align with broader K-12 goals and how specific state-level efforts connect to K-12 strategy priorities. A follow-up was requested on NAT's current work within each K-12 priority state.

Other follow-ups include a meeting with AERDF leadership by Q4 2025 (with an email update expected when AERDF's next program launches by Q1 2025), and a completed review of Khan Academy's annual budget. This meeting reflects the foundation's focus on coherent, state-tailored strategies for HQIM adoption and implementation, combined with a growing emphasis on AI-enabled edtech products as a lever for improving student outcomes.

FILE: K-12 Education Strategy Review_Sept2024_FINAL.pdf
This 2024 Strategy Review digital binder for K-12 Education, dated October 9, 2024, presents a comprehensive overview of the foundation's K-12 math strategy, investment portfolio, and progress toward a 2032 goal of increasing Algebra 1 proficiency by 10 percentage points across California, Florida, Texas, and New York — reaching 1 million additional students. The strategy operates through three interconnected pillars: (1) improving the quality, comprehensiveness, and appeal of digital instructional materials (46% of 2025–27 budget); (2) aggregating demand and supporting HQIM adoption in upcoming state adoptions in CA (2027), TX (2026), FL (2027), and NY (11%); and (3) supporting high-quality enactment by teachers through aligned professional learning (22%). The overall portfolio is rated slightly off track due to slower-than-expected commercialization of R&D insights, delays in building state plans, and pending finalization of major investments (Khan Academy, Kiddom). The strategy memo highlights a significant pivot toward AI-driven product development, with 25% of budget now focused on AI-centric platforms including digital tutoring and teacher coaching tools. Key AI investments include partnerships with Khan Academy (Khanmigo), Carnegie Learning, University of Florida, Allen Institute for AI (AI2) for open-source benchmarks, and MIT's multimodal student math mind model. A Product Quality Framework (PQF) was adopted in 2024 as a vision and grant management tool tracking 49 product features across partner platforms. Partner deep dives cover Carnegie Learning (18/28 priority features, on-track) and Khan Academy (10/28 features, rated off-track for refusing to commit to a full-stack integrated platform). The investment plan shows a four-year allocation of approximately $1.085–1.174 billion across portfolio areas. State strategies are differentiated: CA and FL focus on adoption field-building; NY (especially NYC) and TX focus on enactment following state-level mandates for Illustrative Mathematics. The Networks for School Improvement portfolio (5% of budget) is transitioning, with learning dissemination continuing and a new co-funded data capabilities body of work with the Assessments team. Investment scoring highlights top performers including Cal Ed Partners (NSI), EdLight (AI vision model for math misconceptions), GSSR (math narrative research), and ELSF (multilingual learner supports); bottom performers include Education Analytics NSI data support, National Opinion Research Center, and Arizona State University NSI. The document includes a full $5M+ investment summary report, team org chart, and 2023 meeting follow-up tracking showing completion of the Product Quality Framework and AI architecture sharing sessions with foundation leadership.

These are the official meeting notes from the Postsecondary Success (PS) Strategy Review held on October 9, 2024, attended by senior Gates Foundation leadership including Allan Golston, Patrick Methvin, and others both in-person and virtually. The document captures follow-up action items, key takeaways from co-chairs, and additional discussion points across four major strategic areas.

On Courseware, the Math Big Bet is progressing with the operating entity to be seated at Carnegie Mellon University (CMU), with a planning grant underway. A dinner meeting between Bill Gates and CMU president Farnam Jahanian is planned for Q2 2025. Lumen Stats and Real Chem courseware products are performing well in beta.

Data quality at many institutions remains a challenge even with AI advances.

On Proof of Scale, National Scale Enterprises are on track, though the pipeline of qualifying partners may be nearing its limit at 5–6. The intermediary approach supports incremental institutional transformation (e.g., improving graduation rates by ~5%), but deeper questions remain about how to help institutions adapt more rapidly to disruptions such as AI and demographic enrollment declines.

The meeting also surfaced two significant strategic questions for future analysis: (1) how to navigate equity-related challenges in higher education amid political backlash in some states, with a perspective to be shared with Bill and Mark by end of Q2 2025; and (2) a broader meta-strategy analysis of disruptive shocks to higher education (technology, policy, funding, demographics) and their implications for the foundation's goal of higher education as an engine of equitable economic mobility, to be delivered in Q3 2025 and informing potential 2026 strategic pivots.

Additional items included expected FAFSA simplification improvements in 2025 and a note that value indices for higher education are directionally aligned, with plans to consolidate around one or two indices by 2026. This document is directly relevant to USP Postsecondary strategy and reflects active deliberation on technology, AI, equity, and institutional transformation.

FILE: Postsecondary Success Strategy Review_Sept2024_FINAL.pdf
This 2024 Strategy Review digital binder for the Postsecondary Success (PS) strategy, presented October 9, 2024, provides a comprehensive assessment of the foundation's higher education reform work. The strategy is organized around two primary goals: (1) Generate Proof of Scaled Transformation through Intermediaries for Scale (IFS) supporting 200+ institutions and five National Scale Enterprises (NSEs); and (2) Develop, Share, and Scale Public Goods and Solutions, including tech-enabled courseware and Digital Holistic Student Supports (DHSS).

Key external context includes declining public confidence in higher education value, rising student loan debt ($1.75T), FAFSA rollout failures causing a 20pp drop in completions, anti-DEI legislative trends in 28 states, and growing generative AI use among students and institutions. Persistent gaps in persistence, retention, and 6-year completion rates for Black, Latino, and Indigenous students affirm the equity focus.

Overall strategy performance meets expectations. The IFS approach (174 MOUs signed, 168 institutions completing assessments, 145 with services requested) and NSE work (ASU, SNHU, WGU, UMGC, Purdue Global) both meet expectations. Courseware exceeds expectations, with Lumen Stats and REAL Chem outperforming adoption targets and a Math Big Bet operating entity in development for Q1 2025. DHSS is slightly below expectations due to a partner role restructuring but remains on track with four lighthouse institutions onboarded. Transformation Resources BoWs are evenly split between meets and slightly below expectations; the Admissions BoW is being sunset.

The proposed FY2025–2028 Investment Plan totals $655.5M: 43% toward solutions and public goods, 32% toward scaled transformation, and 26% toward critical enablers (net of a $14M reallocation to Pathways). Notable budget shifts include moving $10M in Courseware from 2025 to 2026–2027 and $15M in DHSS from 2025–2026 to 2027.

Top-performing investments include Complete College America (IFS-CCA, $24M), Western Governors University ($4.9M), and the ASU/CMU Courseware collaboration ($4.6M). Bottom-performing investments include AIHEC ($17M, impacted by leadership transitions), Education Commission of the States Dev Ed Reinvestment ($5.1M), and Educause CourseGateway.org ($4.9M, not being reinvested).

2025 priorities include advancing the Equitable Value reform agenda, launching the Math Big Bet MVP Calculus course, completing DHSS anchor partner investments, making intermediary scaling decisions, integrating NSE learnings, and supporting cross-USP work in Pathways, Math Pathways, CMEP, and AI. The strategy aims to influence roughly 10% of U.S. undergraduates through TAS and NSE institutions, projecting over 171,000 additional degrees for focus students by 2026–2029.

FILE: WSI Investment Narrative 04152024.pdf
This document is the Washington State Initiative (WSI) FY 2024-27 Investment Narrative, produced during the 2023 Annual Planning cycle. WSI began its first full year of implementation following strategy approval in September 2022, and this narrative captures key financial and strategic decisions made to ensure proper resourcing of Bodies of Work (BOWs) while maintaining flexibility for learning and opportunistic investments.

The team reallocated $7.3M from the Reserve within Other Body of Work Types — $1.4M in 2023 and $5.9M between 2024-27 — across three priority areas: (1) Data Systems & Use BOW received $1.4M in 2023 and $1.6M for 2024-27 to support ERDC modernization and data capability upgrades at key feeder agencies, bringing the four-year outlook (4YO) to $17.1M; (2) Structures & Solutions BOW within Regional Levers received $3.05M for 2024-27 to support solutions implementation in deep dive focus regions, bringing the 4YO to $13.9M; and (3) the North America Team within Global Policy & Advocacy received $1.2M for 2024-27 to fund a communications envelope, bringing the 4YO to $3.6M with an estimated $1M annual spend. A reserve of approximately $2.4M (3% of total budget) was maintained for unplanned or opportunistic investments.

In terms of BOW consolidation, the Learning and Indigenous BOWs ($2.8M total) were closed and merged into the MLE BOW to center student voice and pilot new research methods, particularly for the Indigenous student ecosystem. The Knowledge Mobilization BOW ($2.7M) was dissolved and its work embedded across Data, Regional Partnerships, Solutions, PAC, and MLE BOWs.

Three programmatic pillars were identified as central to WSI's strategy launch: selection of three deep dive focus regions (expected December 2023); establishment of a statewide learning network for cross-sector collaboration and evidence-based practice uptake; and convening an Education Roundtable and Funders' Table to improve coordination around postsecondary transitions.

A rollover request of $1M from 2023 was submitted, citing two strategic delays: (1) a decision to delay selection of deep dive focus regions in response to field feedback aligned with place-based principles, which slowed implementation in related BOWs such as Solutions; and (2) a commitment to authentic engagement with the Indigenous community that deferred partner selection for Indigenous student ecosystem coordination to Q1 2024. The rollover is intended to accelerate implementation during the remainder of the strategy period. This document is directly relevant to WSI's broader goals of improving postsecondary outcomes for Washington State students, with particular attention to equity, data infrastructure, and regional partnership strategies.

This indicates substantial uncommitted capacity, particularly in years 2025–2027.

FILE: WSI Presentation Deck 04152024.pptx
This April 2024 CEO Review presentation provides a comprehensive strategy progress update for the Washington State Initiative (WSI), covering the first 18 months of implementation. WSI is structured around two primary levers — state-level systems building and regional (Horizons) engagement — designed to close the high school to postsecondary (PS) transition gap, particularly for Latino and Black students in Washington State.

At the state level, WSI is focused on strengthening the data ecosystem, advancing policy and advocacy, and building sector coordination infrastructure. Key state-level accomplishments include establishing an Education Roundtable and Funders' Table, renegotiating the National Student Clearinghouse (NSC) contract to improve PSE and completion data accessibility for districts, and aligning advising framework and FAFSA completion indicator definitions across partners. WA STEM serves as the primary intermediary, with Sankofa supporting measurement, learning, and evaluation (MLE).

At the regional level, WSI is engaging Horizons regions — Olympic/Kitsap, Vancouver, Walla Walla, and South King County — each selected for distinct demographic and partnership rationale. The delayed selection of Horizons regions is cited as the reason some bodies of work (BOWs) scored slightly below expectations, though the initiative reports being on track for 2024 milestones.

The measurement framework tracks Early Student Momentum Outcomes including FAFSA completion, effective advising, and non-CTE and CTE dual credit enrollment, with long-term outcomes focused on postsecondary direct enrollment and completion rates. Baseline assessments are expected by June 2024. The presentation notes nuanced dual credit tracking: non-CTE dual credit is treated as a leading indicator of PS enrollment likelihood, while CTE dual credit is a graduation requirement and not used as such. Racial disaggregation of dual credit data reveals that Latino students have lower Running Start enrollment and higher College in the High School rates, while Black students are underrepresented across all dual credit types except CiHS.

Significant headwinds include the Better FAFSA rollout delays, which have depressed WA completion rates to 26.8% as of March 2024, well below the national average of 33.7% and last year's 37.5% figure. A newly released data point shows PSE in WA dropped from 61% to 50% in 2020, and Latino student PSE declined 14% to 40% between 2019 and 2021. WSI has responded by providing direct technical assistance to partners, amplifying WSAC's financial aid awareness campaigns, and successfully advocating to delay four-year institution enrollment commitment deadlines. The presentation reflects WSI's cross-cutting connections to national Pathways and Data teams, and its integration of targeted universalism as a framework for intervention design.

FILE: 09172025 USP Data Strategy Update Presentation Deck.pptx
This September 2025 presentation deck outlines the proposed 2026–2030 USP Data strategy, presented to senior Gates Foundation leadership for approval ahead of annual planning and LDA discussions. The deck situates USP Data's evolution from its 2017 origins as a 'Data + MLE' body of work within Postsecondary, through strategy approval and execution phases, to a forward-looking vision anchored in AI-enabled learning and advising at scale.

The overarching ambition — explicitly tied to Ambition 2045 — is that by 2045, all learners, especially those historically underserved, are empowered by safe, evidence-based, AI-enabled solutions delivering personalized experiences. Connected, actionable data is envisioned to ensure universally recognized skills and credentials, enabling learners to navigate pathways, move across institutions, and thrive in meaningful work.

The strategy is organized around three core pillars: (1) Enable AI Solutions for Learning — leveraging personalization to improve efficacy through AI-enabled tutoring and navigation tools; (2) Build Trusted Evidence for AI in Education — improving quality of AI-enabled instruction to drive adoption; and (3) Accelerate Speed and Efficacy of System Feedback Loops — reducing the time from data generation to actionable insights for those closest to learners. These pillars are supported by three cross-cutting enablers: a Data and AI Enablement Hub for internal USP capacity building, key field partnerships to amplify gains, and integrated data and AI work in specific geographies tied to place-based strategy team goals.

A significant focus of the deck is the 'motivation' gap in current EdTech: AI-enabled solutions lack persistent memory and context across tools, creating a fragmented ecosystem. Research cited indicates that only 5% of students meet required usage thresholds on tools like Khan Academy, with benefits skewing toward higher-income students. Districts use an average of 45–49 unique EdTech tools per year, and 88% cite interoperability as their top procurement concern. The proposed solution is open agentic infrastructure that enables portable memory and context across EdTech solutions, which would address the 'cold start problem,' improve early-interaction accuracy, and spur ecosystem-wide innovation rather than entrenching walled-garden approaches.

The deck references engagement with major AI infrastructure players (Anthropic, OpenAI) around semantic standards, interoperability, and guardrails within memory frameworks and MCP protocol updates. Impact projections include a 0.3–0.4 SD increase in learning outcomes per year if AI tutoring is effectively scaled, halving achievement gaps for approximately 5 million Black and Hispanic learners, and approximately 1.1 million more low-income and minority students completing college over five years through scaled AI advising.

Presented to a broad cross-divisional audience including directors of K-12, Pathways, WSI, EL&A, and other USP verticals, this document represents a pivotal strategy alignment moment for the USP Data team under Director Matt Gee.

FILE: 103125 K-12 Education Learning Session Appendix A.pdf
This Gates Foundation confidential document, dated October 2025, presents a comparative framework of three AI-integrated school models intended to guide K-12 education strategy and investment thinking. The document was prepared by Intentional Futures and structures the landscape of AI adoption in schools across three progressive models: Model 1 (Minimal/Targeted AI), Model 2 (Intermediate AI-Integrated), and Model 3 (Full AI-Native Design).

Each model is evaluated across six dimensions organized under three categories: Learning Experience (Learning Design, Student Role and Agency, Assessment and Skills, SEL/Well-Being/Metacognition), Teaching Experience (Teacher Role, AI Use in Learning), and Systems & Support (Equity/Access/Governance, Investment and System Requirements).

Model 1 preserves existing structures — standards-aligned, age-based courses with fixed periods — while using AI for targeted practice, feedback, and administrative efficiency. Teachers remain primary instructors, and governance relies on emerging privacy safeguards and existing policy frameworks.

Model 2 introduces hybrid learning design with interdisciplinary blocks and flexible scheduling. AI acts as an embedded co-pilot personalizing playlists and managing mastery pathways, while teachers shift to designer/facilitator roles. This model requires select policy waivers and robust interoperability across rostering, assessment, and student information systems.

Model 3 represents a full reimagining of schooling around competency-based, project-driven learning. AI functions as persistent personal tutor and specialist agent, co-planning and adapting pacing with human guardrails. Students co-create learning pathways with mentors and AI agents, and assessment relies on continuous multimodal evidence including portfolios and simulations. This model requires district and state-level waivers for assessment, graduation requirements, and data governance, along with new accountability models and learner record wallets.

A consistent thread across all three models is the preservation of human relationships — teachers and mentors remain central to belonging, SEL, and ethical judgment, while AI handles orchestration, routine tutoring, and logistics. The framework explicitly addresses equity, access, and governance at each level, signaling the Foundation's commitment to ensuring AI integration does not exacerbate existing disparities.

Strategically, this document appears to serve as a learning session appendix, likely informing Foundation staff and partners about the range of viable AI school models and the conditions — policy, infrastructure, investment — required to support each. It is directly relevant to the Foundation's K-12 education strategy, particularly around personalized learning, instructional quality, and the evolving role of technology in supporting student outcomes at scale.

FILE: 103125 K-12 Education Learning Session Presentation Deck.pdf
This October 2025 Gates Foundation K-12 learning session presentation deck, titled 'Reimagining Schools for the Age of AI,' compiles perspectives from three external organizations — Teaching Lab, LearnerStudio, and Google — on how artificial intelligence can transform K-12 teaching and learning. The session included senior Foundation leadership across K-12, Postsecondary, Pathways, Data, and the US Program office.

Teaching Lab, a nonprofit serving 17,000 teachers annually across 50+ school systems, presented its Teaching Lab Studio initiative, which invents, tests, and commercializes AI-enabled tools for educators. They identified persistent problems in K-12 math — static curriculum, low edtech dosage, incoherent systems, and low-quality AI tools (e.g., MagicSchool, GPT) — and proposed two solutions: (1) scalable dynamic curriculum platforms (e.g., Kiddom) with AI-powered lesson customization and student grouping, and (2) R&D to build coherent AI-enabled classroom models combining launch, independent practice, small group, inquiry, mastery checks, and reflection. Pilot results from NYC middle schools showed significant learning gains, including a jump from 0% to 29% on-grade-level in 8 weeks and 35% proficiency vs. 11% for comparable peers. R&D priorities include classroom instrumentation via audio/video AI, platform co-pilots tracking student actions in real time, and motivation interventions.

LearnerStudio presented eight lessons from two years of investing in AI-enabled education innovation. Key lessons include: (1) AI has arrived to a system in crisis marked by widening achievement gaps, youth mental health issues, learner disengagement, and rapidly changing workforce demands; (2) there is an opportunity to leverage over $1 trillion in private AI investment, though gaps remain in quality, equity, coherence, and human-centeredness; (3) AI changes both what the system must achieve (future-ready competencies) and how it can deliver; (4) there is emerging consensus to shift from 'schooling' to 'learning' via competency-based, learner-centered models; (5) a cross-sector innovation blueprint is needed spanning modernized content, competency frameworks, and enabling conditions; (6) scale will come through both new 'lighthouse' school models and accelerated pivots of existing networks; and (7) a proactively designed digital backbone — including skills frameworks, learner wallets, measurement, and AI-powered interfaces — is essential for equity and coherence.

The session raised foundational strategic questions: whether one unified platform or AI interoperability across tools is more effective at scale; what AI-enabled models lower implementation barriers for underprepared teachers; how to move 'beyond the screen' using audio/video AI for holistic learning assessment; how philanthropy should balance investment between optimizing the current system and building future-ready models; and what cross-sector partnerships are needed to move at entrepreneurial pace. These questions are highly relevant to the Foundation's K-12 strategy on high-quality instructional materials, teacher development, edtech adoption, and data systems.

FILE: 2025-26 USP Division Resource Memo.pdf
This November 2025 U.S. Program (USP) Division Resource Memo outlines proposed resource allocations for the 2026-2029 planning cycle, covering all major USP strategy areas. The division manages 19 priority investment areas across four impact drivers: Instructional Design R&D and Solutions, Advising and Navigational Supports, Scaled Use/Impact, and Education to Workforce Data. Total USP managed payout for 2026-2029 is approximately $2.4B, with priority areas covering ~68% of managed payout.

The memo's central ask is a USP Data Leadership Directed Allocation (LDA) of $174M (high scenario) or $142M (medium scenario) to fund a refreshed data strategy focused on AI enablement. The three core pillars of the USP Data strategy are: Enable AI Solutions for Learning ($28M), Build Trusted AI Evidence ($39M), and Accelerate System Feedback Loops ($88M), supported by cross-cutting enablers including a Data & AI Enablement Hub ($20M), Integrate Data & AI in Place ($48M), and Leverage Key Partnerships ($4M) including an AI Hyperscaler Consortium with OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Microsoft, Salesforce, and AWS.

Key allocations by driver: Instructional Design R&D and Solutions receives $698M, including continued investment in Khan Academy, HQIM partners (Carnegie Learning, Kiddom, Illustrative Math), and the Learnvia postsecondary platform. Advising and Navigational Supports receives $214M, supporting the Digital Holistic Student Supports strategy, AI-enabled System of Engagement MVP, and Credit Mobility initiatives. Scaled Use/Impact receives $420M, with strong enrollment momentum at NSE partners (ASU, SNHU, WGU) and K-12 adoption progress in Texas and California. Education to Workforce Data receives $308M including the $174M incremental LDA request.

Strategies in wind-down include Early Learning (on track through 2028) and Economic Mobility & Opportunity (EMO), which returns $80M to foundation reserves and transitions remaining activity to NextLadder Ventures (~$441M commitment through 2029). The Postsecondary team reallocates $43.5M to a PS Reserve to fund future strategic needs, and the Pathways team contributes $1.4M to USP Reserves. The Assessment Initiative shifts funds toward an Accelerating Discovery Body of Work focused on AI public goods for next-gen assessment.

Key risks identified include data availability and access, AI advancement and hyperscaler concentration, adoption skepticism among educators and parents, political polarization, federal funding instability (particularly ESSER cliff impacts on high-poverty districts), and leadership transition risks for newly onboarded directors in USP Data and Pathways. The memo also flags dependencies on the North America Team (NAT), FP&A, HR, and cross-team collaboration within USP. The document connects directly to the division's Ambition 2045 goal-setting process, noting that out-year resource allocations will be further refined as that effort progresses.

This email must address three substantive areas: (1) a summary of how current measurement work monitors infrastructure and market components; (2) a response to Mark's question about incorporating external context into the foundation's modeling approach; and (3) a budget breakdown covering areas outside the Strategy Review (e.g., school networks and other initiatives) and an explanation for a $7 million increase in the NSI (New School Infrastructure or similar) budget line in 2025.

This includes an assessment of whether the current approach is sufficiently ambitious and an evaluation of the projected timeline to impact — indicating an intent to stress-test the boldness of infrastructure investments.

The fifth action item ties to the 2026 body of work (BOW) decision-making cycle, requiring a review of the R&D budget and overall funding mix to ensure it is optimized for AI product development and infrastructure work. This suggests a strategic reorientation of resources toward AI-enabled education solutions.

The sixth action item links explicitly to USP's Ambition 45 (Amb45) initiative, asking the team to develop a clear perspective on budget allocation across key categories consistent with the division's long-term vision. This connects K-12 strategy directly to the foundation's broader 2045 ambition framework.

Collectively, these action items reflect USP's K-12 team navigating a pivotal strategic moment: integrating AI into school models, setting bold 2030 infrastructure targets, ensuring measurement systems capture market dynamics, and aligning budget decisions with long-term division goals under Ambition 45.

FILE: K-12 Education Inv Scoring Summary 10092025.pdf
This document is a $5M+ Investment Summary Report for the Gates Foundation U.S. Program K-12 Education strategy, created September 10, 2025. It covers 86 investments organized across multiple strategy buckets including Cross-Cutting Supports, Instructional Materials R&D, Scale Quality Products and Aligned Services, Demand Aggregation and Adoption, Networks for School Improvement, and California/Texas/Florida Enactment work.

The report provides investment-level detail including approved and allocation amounts, impact and execution performance ratings (on a scale from Below Expectations to Exceeds Expectations), INV owners, funding models, and start/end dates. Key organizations funded include RAND Corporation (educator surveys, NSI evaluation), Texas Tech University (US PREP teacher preparation cohorts), Partnership for Los Angeles Schools (IM implementation in LAUSD), RTI International (curriculum-based professional learning), Khan Academy, Zearn, Illustrative Mathematics, Carnegie Learning, NewSchools Venture Fund, AERDF, and numerous NSI intermediaries.

Strategically, the portfolio reflects a concentrated focus on high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) adoption and enactment, particularly in mathematics. Major themes include scaling Illustrative Mathematics and other OER curricula, building professional learning provider capacity, supporting teacher residency programs (US PREP), and improving on-track student outcomes through networked continuous improvement. The Foundation is investing heavily in California (LA Unified), Texas, and Florida as focus states for math enactment.

Performance is mixed across the portfolio. Strong performers include Texas Tech US PREP cohorts (exceeding expectations on execution), Johns Hopkins GRAD Partnership, CORE Districts NSI, Zearn, Carnegie Learning, and CCSSO HQIM network. Investments with concerns include Unbounded Learning (below expectations, organizational instability), Arizona State NSI (below expectations on both dimensions), City Year NSI (persistent below expectations on impact), and Impact Florida (below expectations on execution). Several newer investments are rated Too Early to Tell.

The NSI portfolio shows statistically significant positive effects from the Mathematica summative evaluation, with 9th grade on-track NSI schools showing higher GPAs, more courses passed, and more credits earned. The report also highlights RTI International's curriculum-based professional learning initiative as a complex but high-potential bundled partnership model. The Foundation is winding down several NSI investments as the strategy shifts toward math-focused work. AERDF continues to show promise in R&D but remains slightly below expectations on measurable student outcomes at scale. The report reflects active portfolio management with reinvestment decisions, grant reductions (UnboundEd), and strategic pivots toward AI-enabled math tutoring and OER ecosystem sustainability.

This document is a Gates Foundation U.S. Program K-12 Education investment scoring report dated September 10, 2025, presenting the top five and bottom five performing investments within the K-12 Education strategy portfolio. Top Performing Investments: (1) INV-043668 — RAND Corporation's Math Strategy Educator Survey ($11.5M), which conducts annual teacher and principal surveys to benchmark math instruction and curriculum use nationally; rated Exceeds Expectations for impact in 2025. (2) INV-069894 — Civilytics Consulting's Math Strategy Impact Modeling Support ($1.03M), building a curriculum-adoption sub-model to inform program goal-setting and scenario planning; rated Exceeds Expectations for execution. (3) INV-057661 — California Mathematics Council's HQIM communications initiative ($499K), developing messaging tools for system- and building-level stakeholders; rated Exceeds Expectations for impact. (4) INV-070474 — Texas Tech University/US PREP's District Support for Strategic Staffing ($7.68M), which trained ESC fellows, enabled 2,100+ paid residencies, and shaped state policy including the Texas Residency Certification Pathway. (5) INV-055693 — University of Chicago's Data Science 4 Everyone ($3M), catalyzing K-12 data science policy adoption across 29 states and training 2,571 teachers.

Bottom Performing Investments: (1) INV-055699 — UnboundEd Professional Learning Provider Capacity & Scale ($14M), which has missed curriculum implementation and math product goals, faces severe leadership turnover and financial instability, and is rated Below Expectations in 2025. (2) INV-068437 — Carnegie Foundation's Future of Improvement ($4.5M), experiencing staff departures and missed milestones related to a student-impact award and journal publication; rated Below Expectations and Will Not Reinvest. (3) INV-017887 — AIR's Research Partner for Algebra 1 Grand Challenge ($4.7M), which faced COVID disruptions and design flaws limiting cross-project comparison; rated Below Expectations for impact, Will Not Reinvest. (4) INV-001873 — AIR's Florida Network for School Improvement ($8.9M), showing persistently Slightly Below Expectations ratings due to lack of district-level traction and leadership turnover. (5) INV-069846 — Consortium of Florida Education Foundations' Workforce Management Models ($300K), where most districts pursued initiatives misaligned with strategic staffing frameworks.

Strategically, the report reflects the foundation's focus on math instruction quality, HQIM adoption, educator preparation, and data-informed strategy. Strong performers tend to combine robust data infrastructure with policy influence. Underperformers share common challenges: organizational instability, leadership turnover, and misalignment between grantee capacity and initiative scope. All investments use a Managed and Funded model.

FILE: K-12 Education Investment Plan 10092025.pdf
This document is the U.S. Program K-12 Education Investment Plan, presenting a 2026 Resource Plan with a four-year outlook (2026–2029) generated from the Decision Support Application on September 8, 2025. The plan outlines total planned investments of approximately $1.08 billion across the four-year period, with annual allocations of roughly $270M per year.

The largest investment area is 'Improve the Quality of Instructional Materials and Teacher Supports,' which accounts for $504.0M (46.7% of total), reflecting the foundation's core commitment to high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) development and educator capacity building. The second largest area is 'Cross-Cutting Supports' at $186.6M (17.3%), followed by 'Support the Effective Use of High-Quality Materials' at $150.5M (13.9%), and 'Increase the Awareness and Adoption of High-Quality Materials' at $104.3M (9.7%). Global Policy & Advocacy receives $88.0M (8.1%) and Networks for School Improvement receives $46.9M (4.3%).

The document also presents a detailed comparison of four-year outlook (4-YO) allocations versus existing committed funds, revealing significant uncommitted capacity across the planning horizon. For 2026, 54.3% ($146.9M of $270.7M) is already committed, leaving $123.8M available for new investments. Commitment rates drop sharply in outer years: 23.5% committed in 2027, 9.4% in 2028, and just 4.3% in 2029, indicating substantial flexibility for future grantmaking decisions.

Notably, the Networks for School Improvement group shows a 2026 commitment of 125.7% ($21.4M against a $17.0M allocation), indicating over-commitment relative to the current year allocation, likely due to multi-year grants made in prior cycles. The 'Increase the Awareness and Adoption of High-Quality Materials' group has the highest 2026 commitment rate among other groups at 64.3%.

Strategically, this plan reflects the K-12 team's sustained focus on the full HQIM ecosystem: developing quality materials, increasing awareness and adoption, supporting effective use, and building enabling infrastructure through cross-cutting supports and policy advocacy. The investment structure signals a deliberate sequencing strategy where foundational commitments are high in near-term years and capacity is preserved for adaptive decision-making in 2028–2029. This plan is relevant to Gates Foundation staff managing K-12 portfolio strategy, grant pipeline planning, and resource allocation discussions within the U.S. Program.

FILE: K-12 Education Meeting Notes 10092025.pdf
These are confidential meeting notes from the K-12 Education Team's Strategy Review held on October 9, 2025, attended by Bill Gates, Mark Suzman, and a broad cross-functional group of foundation staff. The document captures follow-up action items, key takeaways from the Chair and CEO, and other discussion topics central to K-12 education strategy.

A central theme is the role of AI infrastructure as a long-term lever for impact. Bill Gates emphasized building and open-sourcing foundational AI assets — datasets, benchmarks, and knowledge graphs — over focusing solely on product adoption. The team was directed to set measurable goals for AI infrastructure, align with co-funders, and evaluate whether the division's approach is sufficiently bold in the context of 2030 target-setting. Potential alignment with CZI's Learning Commons knowledge graph initiative was flagged as worth pursuing.

The notes highlight a strategic tension between horizontal AI platforms (e.g., hyperscalers like Google and Microsoft) and vertical AI products tailored for education. The team is encouraged to influence hyperscalers to incorporate the AI infrastructure being developed, while continuing to weigh near-term tradeoffs. A follow-up meeting on October 31 will address AI and the future of school models, and a Q1 2026 deliverable will outline a vision for supporting below-grade-level students using classroom and AI tools.

The core math strategy remains focused on PreK–9, with Algebra passage by 8th grade (no later than 9th) as the key milestone. Discussion noted that most priority students are not engaging with math outside of school, reinforcing the importance of maximizing in-school learning quality. Early implementation results in NYC and LAUSD are positive, with no evidence of the typical implementation dip following curriculum adoption.

Teacher confidence and adoption are flagged as critical. Confidence is most strongly linked to consistent professional learning (more than monthly) and sustained use of materials over multiple years, and is lowest when teaching below-grade-level students. NYC's approach of offering middle schools a choice among Illustrative Math options is cited as a model for building teacher buy-in. District-wide adoption can take up to two years, and coercive approaches are noted as ineffective.

The supplemental market (~$4–5B, expanded post-COVID) is being addressed through curated partnerships aligning high-quality supplementals with core products like Illustrative Mathematics. R&D features validated by the foundation are being incorporated into partner products, with further quality gains expected by 2026.

Action items include budget analysis (including a $7M NSI increase in 2025), R&D and funding mix review for the 2026 body of work, and developing a budget allocation perspective aligned with USP's Ambition 45 work.

FILE: K-12 Education Presentation Deck 10092025.pptx
This October 2025 presentation deck covers the annual strategy review for the K-12 Education portfolio within the Gates Foundation US Program (USP). The document outlines a $1.08 billion budget for 2026–2029 organized across four primary strategies: (1) Improving Quality of Products and Teacher Supports ($504M, 47%), focused on AI-enabled digital core and supplemental instructional materials reaching efficacy benchmarks by 2032; (2) Shaping the Product and Service Markets ($104.3M, 10%), aimed at increasing adoption of high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) in focus states; (3) Demonstrating and Scaling District-Level Adoption and Use ($150.5M, 14%), targeting teachers in schools with the largest gaps in California, Florida, New York, and Texas; and (4) Cross-Cutting Enablers ($159.5M, 15%). Additional bodies of work include Networks for School Improvement, Data Capabilities, Business Operations, and Global Policy & Advocacy.

The overarching vision is for all students to graduate high school with math skills needed for higher education and workforce success, with a specific milestone of all students in focus states passing Algebra I by end of 9th grade and a target of a 10 percentage point proficiency increase by 2032. Priority populations include Black students, Latino students, and students from low-income backgrounds.

The external environment section highlights several significant challenges: erosion of federal funding (including cancellation of IES grants, NSF education spending cuts from $280M to ~$56M, and delays in releasing $6B in funds), shifts in the core and supplemental curriculum markets (supplemental market growing to $5B with fragmentation risks), near-universal digital learning adoption but expiring federal broadband support, and rapid AI adoption in schools (50% of districts training teachers in generative AI as of 2024). In response, the team has proactively funded seven AI infrastructure partnerships, launched a $41M co-funded AI infrastructure initiative with USP Data, engaged data aggregators (Education Data Center/ZELMA) to replace potentially lost federal data, and strengthened state advocacy capacity through national grantees.

Impact accounting is a key analytical feature: an Impact Model estimates the strategy's effect on Algebra I proficiency through 2032, initially built for California in 2025 with Texas expansion planned by year-end and Florida/New York in 2026. Early modeling shows an estimated 5 percentage point gain in California from strategy impact versus business-as-usual, though a 2pp gap remains between projected impact (40%) and the 2032 target (42%), indicating opportunity for further investment or course correction. The presentation also introduces team leadership across USP and Global Policy & Advocacy, and references critical long-term partners central to strategy execution.

Throughout the course of the strategy, we have identified cross-cutting supports that provide enabling conditions and accelerate scale-up of learnings to reach more students.

FILE: K-12 Education_Oct2025-FINAL.pdf
This is the 2025 Strategy Review digital binder for the K-12 Education strategy, presented October 9, 2025. The document covers the Gates Foundation's comprehensive K-12 math education strategy focused on improving Algebra 1 proficiency by 10 percentage points across California, Florida, New York, and Texas by 2032, targeting approximately 1.71 million students—particularly Black students, Latino students, and students from low-income backgrounds.

The strategy is organized around three pillars: (1) Improving Quality of Instructional Materials and Teacher Supports ($504M, 47% of budget), including R&D infrastructure, AI use cases such as tutoring, grading, lesson planning, and adaptive practice; (2) Shaping the Product and Service Markets ($104.3M, 10%), driving district adoption of HQIM to reach 50%+ of students in focus states; and (3) Demonstrating and Scaling District-Level Adoption and Use ($150.5M, 14%), with implementation networks in LAUSD, NYCPS, and Texas districts. Total 2026–2029 budget is $1.08 billion.

Key strategic developments include: a $41M cross-funder K-12 AI Infrastructure Program with CZI, Walton, Overdeck, and others, engaging OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google to embed education-specific capabilities into frontier models; anchor partner investments in Carnegie Learning (integrated MATHbook/MATHia platform), Khan Academy (Khanmigo, 1.4M district licenses), and Kiddom (AI-centric platform combining IM, ANet/EEDI, and analytics); and curated ecosystem partnerships such as Great Minds + Zearn (Eureka ecosystem) and Carnegie Learning + Illustrative Mathematics.

The strategy memo highlights external pressures including federal funding cuts ($2.3B reduction in FY25/26 including $774M from IES and NSF STEM education), teacher shortages in 47 states, post-pandemic stagnation (only 28% of 8th graders proficient per NAEP 2024), and accelerating AI adoption. Strategic responses include proactive state-level advocacy, outcomes-based contracting pilots in Arkansas, California, Florida, and Texas, and alignment of professional learning with HQIM adoption.

An Impact Model (completed for CA, expanding to TX) projects a 5pp gain from current strategy versus BAU in California, with a 2pp gap to close toward the 42% 2032 target. Early implementation data from LAUSD and NYCPS show 8+ percentage point gains in schools with strong implementation support, though teacher confidence in teaching below-grade-level students remains a persistent challenge (46% in LAUSD, 45% in NYCPS).

The binder also includes investment scoring with top performers (RAND Math Strategy Educator Survey, Texas Tech/US PREP, Civilytics impact modeling) and bottom performers (UnboundEd, Carnegie Foundation for Teaching, AIR Algebra 1 Grand Challenge). The appendix outlines a vision for AI-centric school models progressing from AI-Assisted (Model 1, current) through AI-Integrated (Model 2) to AI-Native (Model 3) school redesign.

FILE: USP division Priorities 10062025.pptx
This presentation provides a high-level overview of USP Division Priorities, summarizing how the division's budget is allocated across its major strategic drivers and assessing progress against those priorities. The document indicates that the priorities covered represent 65% of the USP budget (approximately $1,482M), with the denominator explicitly excluding $533M in EMO, $210M in NAT, and $203M in Scholarships portfolios. The four primary strategic drivers highlighted are: (1) Instructional Design R&D/Solutions, covering curriculum, assessment, and teacher/instructor professional development; (2) Advising and Navigational Supports; (3) Scaled Use/Impact; and (4) Education to Workforce Data. Progress ratings across these drivers range from 'Too Early To Tell' to 'Exceeds,' 'Meets,' 'Slightly Below,' and 'Below,' indicating a mixed performance picture across the portfolio. Notable annotations flag that $13M in WA Structures and Solutions BOW is managed by WSI, and $17M in WA Data Systems and Use BOW is also managed by WSI, reflecting cross-divisional coordination. A significant strategic note concerns the USP Data strategy, which was still in development at the time of this document, with Chair approval scheduled for September 17. The document explicitly states that following approval, priorities would be reframed to align with the new data strategy and articulate intended impact, signaling that the Data driver's rating and rationale reflect only the past year's progress rather than a fully established strategic framework. The presentation also raises questions around the need for additional resources to achieve greater impact, suggesting that the current budget allocation may be insufficient to fully meet the division's ambitions. This document serves as a strategic snapshot for USP leadership, enabling assessment of resource deployment, identification of underperforming areas, and prioritization of future investments across K-12, Postsecondary, Pathways, WSI-Charters, and Data strategy areas.

FILE: 022426 K-12 Education Presentation Deck.pptx
This February 2026 K-12 Education Strategy Update presentation outlines three major strategic pivots proposed by the Gates Foundation K-12 Education Team, with significant implications for the broader U.S. Program strategy through 2030 and toward Ambition 2045.

The presentation begins by framing a 'Student Experience Hypothesis' — articulating that AI can transform learning by providing personalized content, dynamic gap diagnosis, real-time multimodal feedback, AI tutoring in and out of school, and motivating learning experiences. The target outcome is 1.6x student learning acceleration.

Three strategic pivots are proposed:

1. Pivot 1 (WHAT — Product Strategy): Shift from AI-informed to AI-centric digital instructional materials, centering human-level AI tutoring and AI teacher assistance. HQIM remains foundational, with continued scaling toward 7.4M elementary and 4.4M middle school students, but AI tutoring and full-stack coherence across instruction, homework, assessment, and intervention become the new core.

2. Pivot 2 (HOW — R&D Approach): Move from prioritizing scale to proving impact first. This involves shaping frontier AI models with education-specific datasets and benchmarks, identifying which AI capabilities produce acceleration through targeted product R&D, and studying general-purpose AI use outside of school. Approximately 20 diverse demonstration school sites are planned to test AI tutoring under real conditions.

3. Pivot 3 (WHY — Focus through 2030): Explicitly tied to the Ambition 2045 vision, the strategy emphasizes the rare opportunity to shape how AI is developed and used in education while trajectories remain fluid.

The presentation also details investments in AI Infrastructure — defined by Gates as K-12-specific data, models, and evaluation tools across five priority use cases. Key infrastructure investments include gold-standard tutoring datasets (e.g., Million Tutoring Moves Dataset), math classroom benchmarks, knowledge graphs of misconceptions, and multimodal models of math learning. Frontier AI lab partnerships are identified as a key pathway to scale.

An AI Tutor is defined as a student-facing, HQIM-aligned system delivering personalized practice and feedback, while an AI Teaching Assistant supports teachers with lesson planning, student work analysis, and professional learning. Both are framed as pathways to expert-level instructional performance at scale.

This document is directly relevant to K-12, WSI-Charters, USP-Data, and Amb45 strategy areas and reflects a significant directional shift in how the foundation intends to invest in educational AI through 2030.

FILE: 022526 Lessons for Personalization in K-12 Appendix A.pdf
This March 2021 presentation, prepared for the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation, provides a demo overview of the Summit Learning Platform, a K-12 personalized learning platform. The document outlines the platform's core architecture, student and teacher experiences, equity pillars, and a sample virtual school weekly schedule for 7th-grade students.

The platform is organized around three Commencement Level Outcomes: Cognitive Skills (assessed across 7 domains and 36 dimensions for grades 3–12), Content Knowledge (aligned to Common Core or state standards), and Habits of Success (building blocks for non-cognitive development). Each outcome area is supported by specific design elements, platform features, and defined teacher roles. Key design elements include project-based learning, applied learning, rubric-based formative and summative assessment, mastery/spiraling, competency-based learning, personalized learning, 1:1 mentoring, trauma-informed practices, and community groups.

The platform's student-facing interface ('Bill's Playlist') includes a Year Page, Projects, Focus Areas, Week Page, and Progress Page. Teacher-facing tools include a Course Page and Mentoring Page, supporting data-driven interventions, personalized coaching, and differentiated scaffolds.

A Sample Virtual School Student Weekly Schedule illustrates how Summit structures daily learning for virtual school students, incorporating Community Time, Self-Directed Learning (SDL), project work across five subject areas, Habits & Content Development, and weekly mentor check-ins.

The document highlights six Equity Pillars, noted as having been featured in a January USP Deep Dive on Racial Equity and Student Outcomes. These pillars include: getting to know students through disaggregated data by race and income; promoting adult introspection and cultural competence; affirming student identity and culture as assets; avoiding assumptions while holding all students to high standards; accounting for adult behavior and the learning environments adults create; and building community and relationships between adults and students.

Regular rhythms of learning for Summit students include Community Groups and Circle Time, Weekly Mentor 1:1s, Self-Directed Learning, Project Work Collaboration, Peer-to-Peer Mentoring, Small Group Interventions, Habits of Success Learning, and Self-Directed Assessment.

This document connects to the Foundation's USP strategy around personalized learning, edtech investment, and racial equity in K-12 education, demonstrating how a platform-based approach can operationalize equity-centered, competency-based instruction at scale.

FILE: 022526 Lessons for Personalization in K-12 Presentation Deck.pptx
This February 2026 presentation, led by Joel Rose (New Classrooms) and Diane Tavenner (Summit Learning/Futre.me), reviews lessons learned from over a decade of personalized learning work in K-12 and explores how AI can address the longstanding challenge of scaling personalization. The session revisits a 2013 Learning Session on personalized learning, examining what has changed and what barriers remain.

New Classrooms' Teach to One model demonstrated that personalized, competency-based pathways in math — blending grade-level and pre-grade content — produce stronger outcomes (approximately 1.2 years of math growth in year one) and improve student motivation and agency. However, the model struggled with 'product-market fit' due to systemic constraints: the 'grammar of school,' accountability pressures favoring grade-level-only instruction, and EdReports/HQIM alignment requirements. The presentation argues AI can now unlock 'full stack' solutions combining high expectations with personalized pathways, with a lighter implementation footprint and better district-level integration.

Summit Learning scaled to 15 schools and a platform reaching 330 schools nationally, with schools ranking in the top 20% statewide. Key lessons include: motivation stems from clarity, coherence, and agency; technology works when it makes learning journeys visible; and human relationships and implementation quality are as critical as the model itself. Diane Tavenner's pivot to Futre.me reflects a broader shift toward technology-enabled direct reach to young people beyond the school-scaling model.

A central theme is why this moment is different: districts that have adopted HQIM are acutely feeling the gap caused by students lacking prerequisite knowledge, creating a pain point that competency-based, AI-enabled solutions can address. Low-lift on-ramps to competency-based learning (e.g., the Baltimore example) are seen as pathways to deeper implementation. AI near-term opportunities include more engaging personalized digital content and grouping/communication tools for teachers; longer-term opportunities include AI tutors and natural language district reporting.

The session is relevant to K-12 USP strategy around math outcomes, HQIM adoption, and the role of AI and edtech in scaling effective instructional models. Cross-cutting attendees from Pathways, WSI-Charters, Postsecondary, and USP Data roles suggest broader strategic interest in personalization lessons across the portfolio.

FILE: 022526 Postsecondary Strategy Update Appendix A.pptx
This appendix presentation, dated February 2026 and marked Postsecondary Success Confidential, details the Digitally Enabled Teaching & Learning (DETL) strategy with a focus on the Learnvia platform and its AI portfolio. Learnvia is a free-to-students platform initially funded for math gateway courses (Calculus 1/2/3, Precalculus, Quantitative Reasoning) with plans to expand into additional STEM gateway courses including Physics, Biology, Chemistry, and Statistics. Key strategic decisions discussed include: (1) expansion into dual credit and high school use cases, requiring adjustments to content, motivation framing, AI tutor calibration, and efficacy studies for younger audiences; and (2) a reinvestment decision on broadening Learnvia's scope, leveraging authoring processes and tools developed in 2025.

A central strategic framework distinguishes between Learnvia's fully closed-loop AI system—where learner data persistently informs tutoring, assessment, and personalization—and base LLM approaches (e.g., ChatGPT) that are largely session-bound with limited persistent learner context. The document presents evidence that closed-loop systems yield higher learning gains and proposes a portfolio that balances both approaches: (1) Learnvia as a full closed-loop system for depth and efficacy, and (2) a 'Learnvia Lite' SDK App built on ChatGPT's API to demonstrate superior learning outcomes at scale and influence AI lab product development.

A key opportunity identified is the 'Benchmark Gap'—current AI math benchmarks (MMLU, MATH, GSM8K) measure answer accuracy rather than actual learning. The Gates Foundation and Learnvia are positioned as strategic catalysts that can provide real-world learning validation (Machine Learning Evaluation, or MLE) in authentic classrooms including HBCUs, Tribal Colleges and Universities (TCUs), and community colleges. This gives the Foundation leverage to establish preferred integration and co-development relationships with AI labs competing for the education market.

The phased roadmap for the Learnvia Lite SDK App runs from 2026 through Q3 2028 (24 months), with a planning and building phase followed by a validation phase targeting a +2 percentage point improvement in course completion, translating to an estimated 80,000 additional completions via open-source distribution to LLM providers. Strategic next steps include recruiting HBCU, TCU, and community college validation partners, defining SDK architecture, developing a benchmark-gap value proposition for AI labs, and proposing a joint validation pilot with at least one AI lab by Q1 2027.

The document also presents a framework for AI infrastructure decisions, distinguishing capabilities that should be trained into the model (pedagogical intelligence, adaptive reasoning) versus engineered into the application layer (high-stakes advising decisions, dynamic institutional data). This connects to the broader USP postsecondary strategy around credential attainment, equity-focused interventions, and scaling technology-enabled solutions for underserved student populations.

FILE: 022526 Postsecondary Strategy Update Appendix B.pptx
This appendix presentation, dated February 2026, is part of a broader Postsecondary Strategy Update and focuses on Digital Holistic Student Supports (DHSS) and the evolving role of AI—particularly large language models (LLMs)—in postsecondary advising and student success ecosystems.

The document identifies four key factors shaping postsecondary advising and holistic student support as they relate to AI Labs. It presents a detailed matrix of 16 core AI-enabled functionality areas within the System of Engagement (SoE) DHSS framework, categorized by the barriers LLMs face in developing these capabilities and their strategic priority for DHSS partners. High-barrier functions—such as degree audit and tracking, registration, early alert systems, advanced predictive analytics, and financial/FAFSA support—require DHSS-specific infrastructure, compliance alignment, and real-time institutional data that LLMs cannot access without enablers. Lower-barrier functions like task management, career readiness, and community collaboration tools are identified as areas where LLMs can already perform or soon replicate, and DHSS partners are advised to embed LLM capabilities rather than build bespoke solutions.

A central strategic argument is that a virtuous cycle exists: LLMs must adopt public good infrastructure to expand their functionality, while DHSS partners must demonstrate market engagement and innovation to elevate certain capabilities into strategic priorities. The document identifies potential public good investments—including in data infrastructure—that could benefit both DHSS partners and LLM players, and highlights synergies with USP Data and Pathways teams' proposed AI public good investments.

A significant portion of the presentation addresses the Unified Data Transformation System (UDTS) as foundational to institutional AI readiness. It argues that current transformation strategies (PDP, ITA, incremental digital changes) were designed for a pre-AI environment and must evolve. UDTS is positioned as enabling institutions—particularly those operating on legacy systems with limited resources—to govern, standardize, and deploy AI responsibly. The document estimates a potential net incremental impact of approximately 47,000 completers from 2026–2030, with +15,700 additional students per year if rates are maintained, contingent on this institutional transformation approach.

Only ~17% of surveyed institutions have a coordinated, institution-level AI adoption strategy, underscoring the urgency of the proposed UDTS-enabled transformation model. The presentation connects directly to USP strategy by linking DHSS investments, data infrastructure, and AI readiness into a cohesive institutional transformation framework aimed at improving postsecondary completion for students at under-resourced institutions.

FILE: 022526 Postsecondary Strategy Update Appendix C.pptx
This appendix presentation, dated February 2026 and marked Postsecondary Success Confidential, outlines the strategy and projected impact of an AI-Powered Strategic Planning Advisor (2025–27) as part of the foundation's Intermediary Supported Transformation approach under Postsecondary Success. The document is explicitly aligned with Ambition 2045 and frames the AI advisor as a scalable public good intended to be technically feasible, broadly desirable, and financially and organizationally viable.

The AI advisor is designed for secure integration of institutional data sources including PDP and ITA, with FERPA and Global Access compliance. Success metrics include: at least 70% of users reporting improved decision quality or speed; pilot institutions documenting 2–3 AI-informed decisions per phase; Net Promoter Score growth over time; and at least 2 intermediaries expressing intent to white-label or integrate by 2027–28. Efficiency targets include a 25–30% reduction in intermediary coaching time and executive leadership preparation time. The tool is specifically designed to reflect the strengths and constraints of institutions serving historically marginalized students, with active use at Minority-Serving Institutions (MSIs) and regional public institutions.

A parallel public goods dissemination strategy is under exploration to reach field stakeholders not ready for the AI agent without intermediary support. The document also identifies potential expansion pathways, including adding labor market and workforce data to align with DHSS, pathways, and value-related foundation investments. Specific use cases include faster courseware deployment, improved transfer and credit mobility, and smarter resource allocation. A deeper DHSS integration is flagged as especially strategic, as it could convert the AI agent into a live platform capable of real-time opportunity identification and scenario modeling.

The final slide projects impact on 2030 goals, noting that cohorts engaged in catalytic transformation supports are expected to outpace comparison cohorts. The presentation highlights planned acceleration investments including the AI Strategy Agent, UDTS/Transformation alignment, and Data/PS AI integration pilots as mechanisms to better understand how transformation can be accelerated and for which institutional segments and intermediaries. The document connects directly to the foundation's broader postsecondary institutional transformation strategy and signals a pivot toward innovative AI-enabled delivery models.

FILE: 022526 Postsecondary Strategy Update Appendix D.pptx
This document is Appendix D of a February 2026 Postsecondary Strategy Update, focused on evaluating Innovative Models in higher education. It outlines eligibility criteria for organizations considered under this framework: they must award credentials of value (associate's, bachelor's, or other credentials advancing economic mobility), serve U.S. learners, be accredited or on a path to federally recognized accreditation within five years, and have at least 250 enrolled learners by end of 2026. The rubric is open to institutions, employers, vendors, platforms, intermediaries, and consortia.

A central theme is the role of artificial intelligence in transforming postsecondary education. The document maps institutions along a maturity spectrum from 'Coping' and 'Experimenting' to 'Reengineering' and 'Untethered.' It argues that most institutional AI activity remains at lower maturity levels and that Gates Foundation investment interest lies in accelerating Emerging Models and National Scale Enterprises operating at higher integration levels — those that fundamentally reconceive human resource costs and revenue models in higher education.

Three representative innovative models are highlighted: (1) University of the People, an accredited fully online institution with 170,000+ students worldwide offering degrees for $3,500–$6,500 through a volunteer faculty model, use of OER, and deliberate avoidance of Title IV funding to minimize compliance costs; (2) Matter and Space, a start-up within Southern New Hampshire University (SNHU) developing human-centered AI learning systems that build dynamic learner models responsive to real-time cognitive and emotional states, blending tutoring, lessons, and assessments in an AI-native, mobile-first experience; and (3) Newstate University, an AI-native online institution offering degrees and certificates focused on AI skills via a $300/month subscription model (~$3,600 total degree cost), operating outside Title IV with no FAFSA requirement, supporting flexible start and completion timelines.

These models collectively represent design patterns the Foundation is tracking for their potential to dramatically reduce costs, increase access, and improve outcomes for underserved learners. The document connects to the USP Postsecondary strategy by identifying scalable, equity-oriented models that challenge traditional higher education cost structures and accreditation norms, with particular attention to value and return on investment for learners seeking economic mobility.

Rubric for Innovative Model Evaluation
Eligibility Criteria
Public, nonprofit, and for-profit organizations may apply if they fulfill all minimum eligibility criteria: 
Credential-awarding. Must award Bachelor’s or Associate’s degrees, or other credentials of value that advance learner economic mobility
U.S.-serving. Must serve learners in the United States, but do not need to be a U.S. entity*
Accredited or accreditation-seeking. Must show a commitment and path to achieve U.S. Federally recognized accreditation (regional or national) within the next five years, but does not need to participate in Title IV Federal Student Aid
Learner-enrolling. By the end of 2026, have ongoing, unduplicated enrollment of 250 learners in programs that end in credentials

Institutions are likely the most competitive type of respondents. However, we are open to employers, vendors, platforms, intermediaries, or consortia, etc. if they fulfill the minimum criteria.
