FILE: 022526 Postsecondary Strategy Update Appendix E.pptx
This document is Appendix E of a February 2026 Postsecondary Strategy Update, produced by the Gates Foundation's Postsecondary Success team. It is titled 'Sector Trends & Implications' and frames the U.S. higher education landscape through a PEST (Political, Economic, Societal, Technological) analytical lens. The core strategic framing states that the Foundation is adapting its collective work to shape how the postsecondary system responds to PEST forces, with dual goals: influencing the direction of the system and building an evidence base for what comes next.

The presentation is structured as a series of Power BI dashboard views, indicating that the substantive data and analysis are embedded in interactive visualizations rather than static slide content. Sections cover: an Overview, followed by dedicated slides for each PEST dimension (Political, Economic, Societal, Technological), and then two outcome-focused sections on College Entry and Societal Outcomes and Progression through the Higher Education System.

Although the slide text itself is minimal due to the Power BI format, the document's strategic importance lies in its role as a sector intelligence tool supporting the Postsecondary Strategy. It signals that the Foundation is actively monitoring external forces — including likely shifts in federal policy, economic pressures on students and institutions, changing public perceptions of college value, and the rise of technology including AI — and translating those trends into strategic implications for grantmaking and influence.

The focus on college entry outcomes and progression through higher education aligns with the Foundation's longstanding emphasis on increasing credential attainment for low-income students and students of color. By connecting macro sector trends to student-level outcomes data, this appendix supports evidence-based strategy adaptation within the Postsecondary Success portfolio. It is intended as a confidential internal reference for Foundation staff involved in postsecondary strategy development and investment decisions.

FILE: 022526 Postsecondary Strategy Update Presentation Deck.pptx
This February 2026 presentation provides a comprehensive update on the Gates Foundation's Postsecondary Success (PS) strategy, delivered to USP leadership including the President of US Programs and directors across K-12, Pathways, Washington State Initiative, Early Learning, and Data functions. The strategy is organized around three goals: Goal 1 focuses on generating proof of scaled transformation through National Scale Enterprises (NSEs) and 200+ intermediary institutions, targeting improved completion rates, enrollment growth, and credential attainment for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and Pell-eligible students. Goal 2 centers on developing and scaling tech-enabled solutions, specifically AI-enabled courseware targeting 450 institutions and 355,000 students by 2030 with ~80% pass rates and halved equity gaps, and Digital Holistic Student Supports (DHSS) across 8 lighthouse institutions serving 40,000+ students. Goal 3 addresses critical enablers including institutional readiness, a unified data and technology system, and a system of engagement. The portfolio is structured across investment areas including NSE development, intermediary support, courseware, DHSS, and transformation public goods, with allocations categorized by size and impact performance ratings. A significant portion of the presentation analyzes the current PEST (Political, Economic, Societal, Technological) environment reshaping U.S. higher education. Political disruptions include 19 higher education executive orders, student visa revocations, state-level censorship bills, and federal funding uncertainty. Economic pressures include enrollment volatility, projected high school graduate declines by 2041, negative credit outlooks from major ratings agencies, and increased financial strain on institutions. Societal shifts include Gen Z workforce unpreparedness, rapid growth of alternative credentials, and ed-to-workforce misalignment. Technological disruptions include the explosive growth of AI in education (projected $32-41B market by 2030), large-scale AI deployments at institutions like CSU-OpenAI, and displacement of legacy edtech models. The presentation also references early pilot results from a Learnvia Calculus I product reaching 650 students across 9 instructors in Fall 2025. The aggregate credential generation goal is 180,000 additional students earning credentials between 2026 and 2029, with specific targets for focus populations. This document connects to USP strategy by framing postsecondary attainment at scale as both a proof-of-concept and a lever for broader sector reform.

FILE: Appendix G_State Footprint of Postsecondary Success Investments.pptx
This appendix presentation from the February 2012 Postsecondary Success Strategy Review outlines the Gates Foundation's geographic and state-level investment strategy for postsecondary success. It explains why states are critical levers for change: most higher education regulation and funding occurs at the state level, states can drive K-12 alignment, aggregate data for reporting, and foster cultures of learning across colleges through existing statewide networks. The Foundation selected four focus states — Florida, North Carolina, Ohio, and Texas — based on their concentration of target students, policy momentum, and college innovation capacity. These states were identified as priority sites for coordinating policy and program work toward statewide productivity improvement. The presentation shows that PS grants reach all 50 states but reflect deeper investment in the four focus states. Policy initiatives are seeding action in 40 states, with focus states each hosting three or more initiatives. Key initiatives referenced include Core2College, Accelerating Opportunity, Developmental Education Initiative, Completion Innovation Challenge, Complete College America, NGA-Productivity, Completion by Design, and Achieving the Dream. Demographic data from IPEDS (2009) supports the prioritization of focus states based on completion rates and student population characteristics. A 'momentum' slide tracks activity across focus and second-tier states, noting that 54% of the portfolio is invested in tier 1 and 2 states, with 19% of grant dollars in California. Strategically, this document reflects the Foundation's effort to concentrate resources where they can achieve systemic statewide impact on postsecondary completion and productivity, particularly for underserved student populations, while maintaining a broad national presence through policy and advocacy investments.

FILE: FINAL Billg Learning Session K-12 Education Costs Presentation Deck TO PROJECT.pptx
This presentation was prepared for a Bill Gates learning session on K-12 education costs, organized under the US Programs / College Ready team. The session aimed to explain variance in per-pupil revenue and spending across K-12 systems, explore the relationship between expenditures and student outcomes, and compare funding and performance across traditional public and charter schools.

Key findings presented include: state and local sources constitute approximately 90% of total K-12 revenue, with 85% of spending directed toward labor. Per-pupil spending varies dramatically across states — from a high of approximately $21,480 in New York (with only 39% state-funded) to a low of $7,333 in Idaho (63% state-funded). Within-state inequities are equally stark; in New York, district-level per-pupil spending ranges from $12,000 to nearly $34,000. Washington State shows relatively uniform state formula funding but wide local funding disparities, while Pennsylvania exhibits inequities at both state and local levels.

The presentation challenges the assumption that higher spending drives better outcomes, noting little correlation between per-pupil expenditures and state graduation rates. A case study comparing San Diego and Los Angeles illustrates how districts with similar student demographics and different funding levels can produce different achievement results. The presentation also notes that on the surface, systems appear to allocate resources similarly but achieve different results, pointing to management structures, union strength, labor costs, and non-academic program spending as key drivers of variation.

On charter schools, the presentation references the 2013 CREDO National Charter School Study, noting that across 27 states, approximately one-third of charter schools outperform their local public school markets despite receiving less per-pupil funding. This is framed as evidence that effective schooling can be delivered at lower cost.

Emerging tools highlighted include Colorado's SchoolView, Smarter School Spending, and School by Design — platforms designed to improve linkages between financial data and student performance decision-making. These tools are relevant to the foundation's interest in resource equity and efficiency. This document connects to USP strategy around improving K-12 system performance, charter sector investment, and data-driven resource allocation.

FILE: 2025 Nov Ambition 2045 Summary Deck for all USP colleagues.pdf
This internal USP presentation, dated November 2025, outlines the Gates Foundation U.S. Program's Ambition 2045 strategy — a 20-year plan to use education as a lever for upward economic mobility. It is explicitly marked for internal USP use only and represents work in progress, not final decisions.

The foundation's overarching 2045 vision includes three global goals, with USP anchored to the third: helping hundreds of millions of people break free from poverty through expanded economic mobility via education. USP's specific 2045 goal (draft) is that by 2045, [x]% more Americans aged 18–34 will attain credentials that recognize their skills and provide measurable economic returns. The vision emphasizes eliminating the predictive power of race, ethnicity, gender, and socioeconomic status on attainment and prosperity.

Six guiding principles shape goal-setting: mission-aligned and impact-driven; aspirational, achievable, and measurable; equity-centered with a Targeted Universalism approach; connected to existing field momentum; resonant and enduring; and grounded in evidence with a future-oriented lens toward AI.

The strategy is organized around three planning horizons (2025–30, 2030–35, 2035–40), with Horizon 1 (2030) as the immediate focus. Four AI-enabled solution priorities anchor Horizon 1:
1. Full-Stack Core Instruction and Tutoring — targeting 1.6 grade-level gains in one year for students below grade level, with scale reaching 7.4M elementary and 4.4M middle school students in CA, FL, NY, and TX.
2. AI-Enabled Gateway Math Courses — using Learnvia courseware to achieve ~80% average course success rates at 450 institutions, reaching 355K students, with a 50% reduction in equity gaps.
3. AI-Enabled Personalized Advising — combining Pathways, WSI, and Digital Holistic Student Supports (DHSS) to improve FAFSA completion (+7–10pp), college enrollment (+5pp for first-gen), and postsecondary completion (+5–7pp).
4. AI-Enabled Learning Mobility — using credit mapping and data transfer tools (CMDTs) to improve credit applicability from a 32% baseline to 37–45%, reaching 1.2–1.6M learners.

Delivery is framed across three models: solution-based, institutional, and multi-sector. Existing delivery portfolios (NSI, Horizons/Charters, WSI, NSE, IFS) provide baseline 2030 projections.

The strategy also identifies cross-cutting enablers across seven categories: AI integration and interoperability, AI development tools and datasets, AI guardrails, assessment and quality standards, market and field shaping, policy and regulation, and ecosystem/community trust. AI infrastructure and hyperscaler engagement (e.g., Google, OpenAI) is a nascent but critical priority area.

Blue Sky innovation investments focus on influencing hyperscalers via AI infrastructure/standards and institutional transformation. The document closes by noting that investment-level body-of-work targets and organizational design decisions are not yet finalized.

FILE: 2025 Sept 9 DD+ Amb45 Update.pdf
This August 26, 2025 presentation was delivered to US Program (USP) Division Leaders as an update on the Ambition 2045 (Amb45) goal-setting process. It outlines the strategic architecture, guiding principles, draft 2045 goal, Horizon 1 priorities through 2030, and an updated project timeline.

The document establishes six guiding principles for goal-setting: mission-aligned and impact-driven; aspirational, achievable, and measurable; equity-centered with a targeted universalism approach; connected to existing field priorities; resonant and enduring; and grounded in evidence with future orientation including AI.

The USP 2045 goal is framed as advancing Bill Gates's broader poverty aspiration — specifically the vision that 'hundreds of millions of people break free from poverty.' The draft 2045 goal statement reads: 'More learners are on track to earn high-value credentials and pursue pathways that lead to upward mobility.' The current USP vision emphasizes that race, ethnicity, gender, and socioeconomic status should no longer be predictors of educational attainment and economic mobility.

The goal architecture consists of four layers: (1) the 2045 goal, (2) a cross-USP Theory of Action (ToA) focusing on high-leverage moments such as gateway course performance, postsecondary transitions, and credential completion, (3) Horizon 1 priorities and targets for 2030, and (4) body of work and investment targets (not yet started).

Horizon 1 (2025–2030) priorities center on four AI-enabled solutions: (1) Full Stack Core Instruction and Tutoring — combining curriculum, tutoring, and adaptive learning to double math achievement rates for bottom-quartile learners; (2) AI-Enabled Gateway Math Courses — scaling AI courseware and faculty professional development to boost pass rates and close equity gaps in STEM; (3) AI-Enabled Personalized Advising — investing in tools spanning high school and postsecondary to improve pathway persistence; and (4) AI-Enabled Credit Mobility Platforms — improving visibility and transferability of credits across institutions. A fifth priority, Delivery, focuses on institutional and multi-sector change to make the four solutions work coherently together.

The document also maps draft momentum points from an East-West (E-W) learner journey framework, including on-track 4th grade math, Algebra I by 9th grade, FAFSA completion, postsecondary enrollment, gateway course completion, credit accumulation, and credential attainment.

A multi-horizon roadmap (2025–2045) shows how levers and priorities will evolve, with Horizon 1 establishing proof of efficacy, Horizons 2–3 expanding and innovating, and Horizon 4 responsibly winding down while sustaining partner momentum.

The updated project timeline runs August 2025 through February 2026, with key milestones including a September 15 Division Memo, September 24 first draft targets, October 1 feedback, October 10 second draft, a TBD date for socializing targets with the Chair, and end-of-year alignment with the Foundation Board process.

FILE: 20251009_USP Ambition 2045 Q&A_all USP.pdf
This document is a comprehensive Q&A (last updated November 19, 2025) addressing US Program (USP) staff questions about Ambition 2045, the Gates Foundation's 20-year strategic vision for the US Program division, anchored to the foundation's planned close in 2045. It covers five major topic areas: the Ambition 2045 overview, Horizon 1 (2030) priorities, impact on current work, timeline, and engagement and leadership structures.

The 2045 ambition is grounded in ensuring every learner has what they need to achieve upward mobility, using a targeted universalism framework that sets universal goals pursued through targeted strategies for populations furthest from those goals. The strategy is organized into five-year 'horizons,' with Horizon 1 running through 2030.

For 2030, USLT has identified four AI-enabled solution priorities: (1) full-stack core instruction and tutoring, (2) AI-enabled gateway math courses, (3) AI-enabled personalized advising, and (4) AI-enabled credit mobility platforms. These are supported by delivery, AI infrastructure, enablers, and innovation priorities still under development. The 2030 priorities focus on five key learner momentum points: 9th grade algebra success, quality dual enrollment in high school, high school-to-postsecondary transition, gateway postsecondary math completion, and credit transfer and learning mobility.

The document explains that Ambition 2045 is not a repackaging of existing work but a genuine strategic stretch effort. Technology-enabled and AI-driven solutions remain a core comparative advantage. Economic mobility remains central to USP's mission despite the wind-down of the EMO team. Flexibility is built into the planning structure because AI and policy changes are expected to rapidly evolve the education landscape.

Regarding impact on current work, no changes to deliverables are expected through end of 2025. In 2026, Directors will partner with teams to align portfolios with 2030 goals. Bodies of work not tightly connected to solution priorities will be assessed transparently, with changes communicated in advance. Resource allocation will use existing ROB processes plus new Ambition 2045-aligned checkpoints. A division-wide reorganization is not a primary focus but operating model considerations are underway.

The timeline calls for near-final 2030 targets to have been shared in mid-November 2025, with full finalization including delivery, enablers, and AI infrastructure goals by February 2026. The 2045 goal statement is also expected by early 2026. Ambition 2045 is not yet to be shared externally with partners or grantees until internal strategies are finalized.

Engagement structures include USLT leading alignment and goal design, Deputy Directors connecting strategy to portfolio action, and subject matter expert delegates refining targets. All USP staff are encouraged to raise input through Directors or anonymous surveys. The document emphasizes transparency, phased communication, and team agency in execution.

FILE: 20251205_Slides for USLT hosted office hours_all colleagues.pdf
This presentation provides a comprehensive overview of the U.S. Program's (USP) Ambition 2045 (AMB 45) strategy, including the draft 2045 Vision and Goal, the 2030 Goal Architecture, priority solution targets, and the operating model transition framework. Presented in December 2025 for an all-colleagues office hours session hosted by the USLT.

The draft 2045 Vision articulates education as an engine of upward mobility where race, ethnicity, gender, and socioeconomic status no longer predict attainment, access, or prosperity. The 2045 Goal — still being finalized — targets a measurable percentage increase in credential attainment among Americans aged 18–34 that provides economic returns.

The 2030 Goal Architecture translates this long-term vision into five prioritized initiatives: (1) Full-Stack Core Instruction and Tutoring — AI-enabled learning acceleration targeting 1.6 grade-level gains in one year for 14K–30K students at 25–40 demonstration sites, scaling to 7.4M elementary and 4.4M middle school students in CA, FL, NY, and TX; (2) AI-Enabled Gateway Math Courses — targeting 80% course success rates (+10pp) and 50% reduction in equity gaps across 450 institutions, reaching 355K students; (3) AI-Enabled Personalized Advising — improving FAFSA completion (+7–10pp), enrollment (+5pp for first-gen), and postsecondary completion (+5–7pp), reaching up to 8.6M students via LLM-embedded tools; (4) AI-Enabled Learning Mobility — improving credit applicability from 32% to 37–45% for 1.2–1.6M learners using credit mapping and data transfer tools; and (5) Institutional or Multi-Sector Delivery — targets to be added, with WSI Charters, NSEs, and IFS as key delivery vehicles.

Three cross-cutting enablers support these priorities: AI Infrastructure and Hyperscalers (benchmarks, datasets, knowledge graphs), Cross-cutting Enablers (assessments, policy, data), and Blue Sky Innovations for future horizons toward 2045.

The operating model slide frames the bridge from current to future state through six components: Governance, Management Systems and Resource Allocation, Org Structure, People Capabilities, Systems and Infrastructure, and Learning and Evaluation — all activated through shared culture and leadership norms. Strategy inputs include external context, strategic goals, Bill's direction, and funding priorities, while execution outputs include measured AMB 45 impact, learning and adaptation, and field influence growth.

This document is central to understanding how USP is operationalizing AMB 45, connecting near-term 2030 milestones to the long-term 2045 credential attainment goal across K-12, postsecondary, and pathways strategy areas.

FILE: Ambition 2045 Memo_Nov 10.pdf
This confidential ELT memo and accompanying presentation materials, dated November 2025, outline the Gates Foundation U.S. Program's Ambition 2045 strategic vision and its eight critical path priorities, with detailed 2030 targets established for four key solution priorities. The overarching 2045 vision is for education to be a true engine of upward mobility through human-centered, AI-enabled support, resulting in measurably more Americans attaining credentials with economic returns.

The document identifies five pivotal moments in learners' educational journeys: success in 9th grade algebra, quality dual enrollment in high school, transition to postsecondary enrollment, completing first postsecondary courses and persisting in college, and completing a credential with full credit recognition.

The four completed solution priorities with 2030 targets are:

1. Full-Stack Core Instruction and Tutoring: AI-enabled HQIM and tutoring platforms targeting 1.6 grade levels of learning growth per year for students below grade level, demonstrated across 25–40 sites serving up to 30,000 students, and scaled to reach 7.4M elementary and 4.4M middle school students in CA, FL, NY, and TX at $60–$80 per student.

2. AI-Enabled Gateway Math Courses: Using AI courseware (Learnvia) to raise pass rates to ~80% (a ~10 percentage point increase), cut equity gaps by 50%, and reach 355,000 students across 450 institutions, with $0 cost to students via open access distribution.

3. AI-Enabled Personalized Advising: Leveraging LLMs and tools like Pathways, WSI, and DHSS to increase FAFSA completion (+7–10pp), immediate college enrollment (+2–5pp, +5pp for first-gen), first-year retention (+4–7pp), and credential completion (+5–7pp), reaching up to 8.6M high school students and 300,000 postsecondary students at $12–$20 per student.

4. AI-Enabled Learning Mobility: Deploying Credit Recognition and Equivalency Database (CRED) and AI-powered Credit Mobility Data Tools to raise credit applicability from 32% to 37–45%, reach 1.2–1.6M learners, and reduce institutional cost per learner from ~$16 to below $13.

Four additional priorities (5–8) covering institutional/multi-sector delivery, AI infrastructure and hyperscaler engagement, cross-cutting enablers (assessment, policy, data), and blue-sky innovations are noted as still in target-setting development. The document also maps 19 existing 2025 USP priorities to the Ambition 2045 framework, identifying key partners including Khan Academy, Carnegie Learning, Zearn, AERDF, ASU, SNHU, WGU, and others. Organizational and talent implications are flagged as upcoming areas of focus.

FILE: Guiding Principles for Goal Setting Ambition 2045.pdf
This internal document, updated June 2025, outlines the guiding principles that will shape goal setting for the Gates Foundation US Program (USP) as it works toward its Ambition 2030/2045 strategic framework. The document is structured around two sets of principles: those governing what the goals themselves will be, and those governing how the work will be conducted.

The six goal-setting principles establish that USP goals must be: (1) mission-aligned and impact-driven, directly supporting the 2045 ambition to break the cycle of poverty and expand paths to prosperity, with the vision that all youth have what they need to thrive; (2) aspirational, achievable, and measurable, with clear outcomes, success indicators, and time frames, using 2030 milestones to track progress; (3) equity-centered using a Targeted Universalism approach, pursuing universal ambitions through strategies tailored to communities most impacted by systemic barriers; (4) connected to existing priorities and aligned with field momentum and partner priorities; (5) resonant, communicable, and enduring across diverse stakeholders and through shifts in leadership, political context, and technology—including AI; and (6) grounded in evidence and oriented to the future, referencing the concept of 'walking into the future backwards' drawn from Indigenous Pacific and Māori worldviews.

The behavioral principles for conducting the work emphasize division-wide accountability, learning from prior work, tracking impact through interim goals, leveraging place-based approaches, relying on directors to synthesize community input, engaging external experts when needed, supporting staff and partners through transitions, and maintaining clarity about what is out of scope.

A FAQ section addresses key strategic questions: The 2030 goals are the current primary focus, with a planned reassessment of 2045 goals based on learning. Not every USP team will be explicitly named in the goal articulation—goals will center critical path priorities where USP has the greatest opportunity for impact. New strategies are unlikely to be introduced; existing strategies will be refined as needed. Community voice will be integrated primarily through prior stakeholder engagement conducted by program teams. The document signals that USP is in an active goal-refinement phase, consolidating strategy rather than expanding scope, and emphasizes alignment across the division leadership team as a foundational operating principle.

FILE: Gates 2045 Vision Research_11.26.pdf
This November 2025 presentation reports findings from six focus groups conducted across Texas and California with three audience segments — parents, K-12 education leaders, and higher education leaders — to assess resonance of the Gates Foundation U.S. Program's 2045 vision narrative and 2030 goals. The research was designed to inform messaging refinements prior to broader external communications.

Key findings reveal that awareness of the Gates Foundation's U.S. education work is low across all audiences, with most associating the Foundation primarily with global health initiatives. Despite this, participants broadly aligned with the directional goals of the 2045 vision, though skepticism — particularly among parents — runs deep around AI integration in schools.

All audience segments expressed concern that U.S. education is headed in the wrong direction, citing erosion of foundational skills, loss of human connection, chronic underfunding, equity gaps, and politicization of schools. Participants prioritized practical pathways to economic mobility, back-to-basics academic rigor, equity and access, and holistic community support.

The four 2030 goals tested — AI-enabled 9th grade algebra instruction, AI-enabled gateway college math courseware, AI-enabled personalized advising for college enrollment, and AI-enabled credit transfer mobility — received mixed to qualified support. The algebra and advising goals received the most favorable reactions. Across all goals, participants raised concerns about overreliance on AI, feasibility of stated metrics, absence of human connection, and lack of cited evidence.

Four messaging themes emerged as candidates for amplification: (1) Multiple Pathways to Success — framing education as offering diverse routes beyond 2- and 4-year degrees; (2) Human in the Loop — positioning AI as a tool that enhances rather than replaces teachers and counselors; (3) Inclusive Lens — demonstrating how the vision serves learners across socioeconomic contexts; and (4) Parents as Partners — elevating parents as active collaborators in student learning.

Key language concerns included negative reactions to 'AI-enabled support' (perceived as vague and displacement-oriented), 'key points in their learning journey' (who defines these?), and 'valued credentials' (interpreted as implying some pathways lack worth). The mention of policymakers as collaborators triggered backlash, particularly among parents who want less government involvement in education.

Strategic implications for USP messaging: the Foundation must lead with human-centered framing, provide transparent evidence and implementation detail, and build trust through demonstrated results over time rather than aspirational language alone. The document directly informs refinement of the Amb45 narrative and 2030 goal communications strategy.

FILE: April 10 Pillar 6 Delegate Group Summary and Recommendation.pdf
This April 2026 memo from Jamie McKee to USP Division Staff summarizes the work and recommendations of the Exemplars, Public Goods, and Policy (Pillar 6) delegate group in the context of the Ambition 2045 strategy. The memo addresses the challenge that the education system does not consistently function as a coherent system for learners, resulting in inequitable outcomes across K–12 and postsecondary.

Pillar 6 is defined as a 'scaling discipline' for the division — integrating policy, communications, delivery, and measurement into a coordinated approach that enables the field to adopt, implement, and sustain effective practices at scale. The pillar's role is explicitly not to develop or deliver solutions, but to enable systems to adopt and sustain them, and to change how systems operate so those practices become standard and durable.

The conceptual operating model centers on a small, prioritized set of EW-aligned indicators and operates as a disciplined scaling engine: (1) identify exemplars and bright spots, (2) measure and learn what drives outcomes, (3) strategize for scale through policy, public goods, technical assistance, communications, and aligned incentives, and (4) refine and scale through differentiated support across contexts.

The memo's central recommendation is that the division align on a shared EW indicator stack spanning K–12 through postsecondary, organized into four categories: (1) Foundational Academic Signals — Grade 3 and Grade 8 proficiency in Math/ELA and Algebra I completion by Grade 9; (2) Momentum and Transition Signals — 9th grade on-track status, FAFSA completion, advanced math completion, dual enrollment access, and immediate postsecondary enrollment; (3) Progression and Attainment Signals — first-year credit accumulation, college and career advising access, gateway course completion, postsecondary persistence, and economic mobility; and (4) System Capacity and Scaling Signals — equitable placement in rigorous coursework, 8th grade Algebra availability and access, and effective school leadership.

Three specific recommendations are made: align on the proposed EW indicator stack as a shared foundation for strategy and measurement; use these indicators as an organizing anchor for Pillar 6 and to guide division-wide resourcing, portfolio alignment, and cross-pillar coordination; and leverage a coordinated MLE (Monitoring, Learning, and Evaluation) approach grounded in the impact model to track progress and inform strategic adjustments. This memo is foundational for cross-pillar USP coordination under Ambition 2045.

This blog post by Allan Golston, President of the Gates Foundation U.S. Program, published March 31, 2026, articulates a sharpened strategic direction for USP education work in light of two converging realities: the foundation's defined sunset in 2045 and the rapid emergence of AI as a transformative tool in education. This presentation outlines the U.S. Program's 2030 Goal Architecture, a strategic framework aligned to the Ambition 2045 vision that 'education will be a true engine of upward mobility.' The overarching goal is for a measurable increase in the percentage of Americans aged 18–34 who attain credentials with economic value. The document defines seven strategic priorities with specific 2030 targets.

Priority 1 (Full Stack Core Instruction & Tutoring) aims to accelerate learning rates from 0.9–1.3 to 1.6 grade levels per year for students below grade level, targeting 25–40 demonstration sites serving 14K–30K students, scaling to 5.5M–7.4M elementary and 3.3M–4.4M middle school students in CA, FL, NY, and TX.

Priority 2 (AI-Enabled Gateway Math Courses) targets ~80% average course success rates (or +10pp over baseline) and a ~50% reduction in equity gaps, reaching 355K students across 450 institutions using AI-enabled courseware (Learnvia).

Priority 3 (AI-Enabled Personalized Advising) targets +7–10pp FAFSA completion, +5pp college enrollment for first-gen students, and +5–7pp postsecondary completion rates. It aims to reach up to 8.6M high school students via LLM integration and 300K postsecondary students through DHSS tools.

Priority 4 (AI-Enabled Learning Mobility) targets increasing credit applicability from 32% to 37–45% using AI credit-mapping and data-transfer tools (CMDTs), reaching 1.2–1.6M learners by 2030.

Priority 5 (AI & Data Infrastructure) aims to cut by 30–50% the time for solution partners to reach efficacy targets, reach 40% of learners with portable memory/context solutions, and leverage 2–3x ($415–540M) through hyperscaler partnerships.

Priority 6 (Exemplars, Public Goods, State Policy, Funding, Communications & Engagement) focuses on building system conditions for scale through assessment quality, shared data measures, place-based exemplars, and enabling policy environments.

Priority 7 (Breakthrough Innovation) establishes a high-risk, high-reward portfolio ($130M–$140M) for transformative ideas that could fundamentally reshape learning, navigation, and credentialing beyond current system architecture.

The document also outlines director accountabilities across five leaders (Bob Hughes, Patrick Methvin, Matt Gee, Jamie McKee, Carrie Douglass) and transitions for Cheryl Hyman (to Learning Mobility), Angela Jones (departing), and Allan Golston (EMO wind-down). The framework maps priorities to an East-West ladder of outcomes spanning math proficiency in grades 3 and 8 through postsecondary persistence and credential completion.

FILE: Mar 13 Ambition 2045 Strategy Overview.pdf
This document is a summary overview presentation of the Gates Foundation U.S. Program's Ambition 2045 strategy, outlining the division's long-term vision, goals, priorities, and theory of action for improving learner outcomes across the K-12 and postsecondary education continuum.

The core Ambition 2045 goals are to double the rate of credential-of-value attainment growth for 18- to 34-year-olds and enable 10 million more learners — especially those furthest from opportunity — to attain credentials that deliver sustained economic value by 2045. These are noted as internal draft targets not for external sharing.

The strategy is organized around Seven 2030 Priorities: (1) Full Stack Core Instruction & Tutoring to keep K-12 learners on track; (2) AI-Enabled Gateway Math Courses to help learners pass critical math milestones in high school and postsecondary settings; (3) AI-Enabled Personalized Advising to support postsecondary applications, enrollment, persistence, and certificate completion; (4) AI-Enabled Learning Mobility to ensure credits count and learners can navigate fragmented systems; (5) AI & Data Infrastructure to strengthen institutions with safe, human-centered design; (6) Exemplars, Public Goods, State Policy, Funding, Communications & Engagement to bring innovations to scale; and (7) Breakthrough Innovations to pursue transformative, horizon-level impact.

The Theory of Action holds that learner outcomes can be improved by investing in AI-enabled solutions, cross-cutting enablers, and breakthrough innovations while safeguarding human agency and trust. The strategy identifies four Critical Momentum Points along the learner journey: Algebra I completion by 9th grade, immediate postsecondary enrollment, learning counts on a credential pathway, and gateway course completion.

Organizationally, USP is restructuring to integrate WSI, Charters, and Pathways teams, with a new organizational structure planned by end of July and a new operating model targeting 2027 implementation. Portfolio-level decisions and deputy director accountabilities were expected by end of May, with individual role clarity communicated by June.

What is changing includes divisional focus, structure, and cross-team alignment. What is not changing includes the commitment to excellence, the conviction that education drives prosperity, and the focus on targeted solutions ensuring all learners benefit regardless of race, ethnicity, gender, or background.

This document connects directly to the broader USP strategy by reframing investments around AI-enabled tools and infrastructure, establishing measurable momentum milestones, and reorganizing the division to deepen and accelerate impact toward equitable credential attainment.

FILE: USP Ambition 2045 Q&A_All USP.pdf
This document is a comprehensive staff-facing FAQ for the US Program's Ambition 2045 strategy, last updated March 13, 2026. It covers the vision, priorities, organizational changes, operating model, and transition logistics associated with USP's long-term strategy through foundation closure in 2045.

The 2045 goal is to double the growth rate of credential attainment for Americans ages 18–34, resulting in 10 million more learners attaining credentials of economic value, with a focus on those facing the greatest obstacles. The strategy applies a targeted universalism framework and is organized into 5-year 'horizons,' with Horizon 1 running through 2030.

For Horizon 1, seven strategic priorities are established: (1) Full-stack core instruction and tutoring including HQIM and AI-enabled math; (2) AI-enabled gateway postsecondary math courses; (3) AI-enabled personalized advising for college transitions; (4) AI-enabled learning mobility and credit transfer; (5) AI and data infrastructure; (6) Exemplars, public goods, state policy, funding, communications and engagement; and (7) Breakthrough innovations. Specific 2030 targets are defined for priorities 1–5, including adoption at scale, pass rate improvements, and equity gap reductions.

The document addresses significant organizational restructuring: WSI and Charters will be embedded across other priorities rather than remaining standalone teams; the Pathways team will be dissolved as a standalone unit with its director transitioning to a Principal Officer role focused on Learning Mobility; and NAT colleagues from GPA have been integrated into USP under Priority 6. The reorganization is driven by Ambition 2045 strategy, not the foundation-wide OpEx initiative, though both are happening concurrently.

The FAQ clarifies roles: USLT leads strategy alignment and decisions; OOP coordinates communications and cross-divisional implementation; DDs shape bodies of work through delegate groups. Delegate groups are internal subject matter experts helping define the next layer of strategic detail; participation does not signal future role changes.

Key timelines include: portfolio-level decisions and DD remits by April 2026; full staff communication by end of May; individual role clarity by June; new org structure operational by July. These dates are held lightly.

The document also addresses responsible AI use, emphasizing learner-centered design, equity, privacy, and human oversight. It explains why AI is central to the strategy given its transformative potential and the foundation's comparative advantage in shaping AI's role in education. The FAQ notes that the OpEx initiative is not driving the reorg, and provides guidance for communicating with grantees and partners during the transition.
